Beyond-all-order asymptotics for homoclinic snaking of localised patterns in reaction-transport systems
Edgardo Villar-Sepúlveda

Abstract
Spatially localised stationary patterns of arbitrary wide spatial extent emerge from subcritical Turing bifurcations in one-dimensional reaction-diffusion systems. They lie on characteristic bifurcation curves that oscillate around a Maxwell point as part of a homoclinic snaking phenomenon. Here, a generalisation of the exponential asymptotics method by Chapman & Kozyreff is developed to provide leading-order expressions for the width of the snaking region close to a Turing bifurcation’s super/sub-critical transition in arbitrary n n -component reaction-diffusion systems. First, general expressions are provided for the regular asymptotic approximation of the Maxwell point, which depends algebraically on the parametric distance from the codimension-two super/sub-critical Turing bifurcation. Then, expressions are derived for the width of the snaking, which is exponentially small in the same distance. The general theory is developed using a vectorised form of regular and exponential asymptotic expansions for localised patterns, which are matched at a Stokes’ line. Detailed calculations for a particular example are algebraically cumbersome, depend sensitively on the form of the reaction kinetics, and rely on the summation of a weakly convergent series obtained via optimal truncation. Nevertheless, the process can be automated, and code is provided that carries out the calculations automatically, only requiring the user to input the model and the values of parameters at a codimension-two bifurcation point . The general theory also applies to higher-order equations, including those that can be recast as a reaction-diffusion system. The theory is illustrated by comparing numerical computations of localised patterns in two versions of the Swift-Hohenberg equation with different nonlinearities, and versions of some classic activator-inhibitor reaction-diffusion systems.



Submitted for publication: August 24, 2026

Keywords
: exponential asymptotics, reaction-transport
systems
, homoclinic snaking, localised patterns, pattern formation

1
Introduction

The spontaneous formation of spatially periodic patterns through a finite wavenumber instability of systems of reaction-diffusion equations arises in several areas of applied mathematics, beginning with Alan Turing’s seminal work on the chemical basis of morphogenesis
[
22
]
. See
[
11
,
19
]
, for recent reviews and updates. If the Turing bifurcation is subcritical, giving rise to unstable periodic patterns that subsequently restabilise, then there should be a family of transverse heteroclinic connections between the background steady state and stable periodic patterns
[
5
,
10
,
13
]
. Unfolding this family leads to localised structures of arbitrarily wide spatial extent that are formed by homoclinic connections to the background with arbitrary oscillations near the periodic pattern
[
27
]
. These connections organise themselves in what has been dubbed a snaking bifurcation diagram as depicted in Figure
1
. See, for example,
[
1
,
5
,
9
,
16
,
23
]
and references therein.

Beck
et al

[
2
]
provided proof of the existence and properties of the snaking bifurcation diagram in a large class of reversible systems under generic assumptions on the existence of the heteroclinic connection. Computational methods can find much of the fine structure of homoclinic snaking in examples, but in general, analytical descriptions of the localised patterns remain problematic, even in one spatial dimension. See
[
4
]
for a review of the state of the art
of localized patterns
in one or more spatial dimensions.

One possibility to get an analytical handle on localised pattern formation due to homoclinic snaking is in the asymptotic limit of a codimension-two super/sub-critical Turing bifurcation. From such a bifurcation, there emerges a
Maxwell point
at which the background-to-periodic heteroclinic connection exists. Unfortunately, it is known that the normal form of such a bifurcation, up to any algebraic order, is integrable and so any heteroclinic connection is non-transverse and there can be no homoclinic snakes within the normal form (see e.g.
[
15
,
27
]
). Therefore, the snaking region must emerge beyond all algebraic orders in parameters that unfold the normal form. The computation of this beyond-all-orders width about the Maxwell point, for arbitrary reaction-diffusion systems posed on the real line forms the subject of the present paper.

At present, this beyond-all-orders computation has only been attempted in a few special cases
[
10
,
12
,
13
]
. A canonical example of pattern formation is the fourth-order Swift-Hohenberg partial differential equation (PDE). As we shall see, such higher-order equations can be brought into the framework of the class of reaction-diffusion equations studied in this paper, provided we allow for a singular temporal evolution operator, which is fine because we are primarily concerned with the existence of stationary patterns here, not their dynamics.

Figure 1
:
Qualitative description of the snake of localised patterns snaking around a Maxwell point in a one-parameter diagram. See text for details. The inset shows a two-parameter diagram where the magenta curves represent loci of the outermost folds of the snake, surrounding the Maxwell point (black curve) arising from a codimension-two Turing bifurcation (the point of transition between red and blue lines, which represent supercritical and subcritical Turing bifurcation lines, respectively). The asymptotic scaling of the width
of the snake
between the two magenta curves as we approach the codimension-two point is what the analysis in the paper seeks to explain. While intended to be qualitative, the numerical curves are taken from the Swift-Hohenberg 2-3
example,
studied in Section
7
.

This paper is motivated by the groundbreaking work by Chapman & Kozyreff
[
10
,
18
]
, who used
exponential asymptotics
to study homoclinic snaking in the Swift-Hohenberg equation with quadratic-cubic nonlinearities posed on the line. They used matching of inner and outer expansions of exponentially growing and decaying solutions upon crossing a Stokes’ line in the complex plane. The problem is reduced to the computation of a single coefficient of an exponentially small amplitude, which can be achieved through an iteration scheme. They found the iteration to be slowly converging and instead simply fit this coefficient to numerical results. Later, Dean
et al.

[
13
]
applied the same technique to a version of the Swift-Hohenberg with cubic-quintic nonlinearities. The additional odd symmetry made the calculation slightly more straightforward, and they were able to make their iteration scheme converge to an amplitude that matched extremely well with numerical results. More recently, the same method was implemented by De Witt
[
12
]
for a modified version of the Schnakenberg system, which is a reaction-diffusion equation with two components.

1.1
Problem description

We pose a general reaction-diffusion system on the line as:

\displaystyle\mathbb{M}\,\partial_{t}\,\mathbf{u}=\mathbf{f}(\mathbf{u};a,b)+\hat{D}\,\partial_{xx}\mathbf{u},\qquad x\in\mathbb{R},

(1)

where
\mathbf{u}=\mathbf{u}(x,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
n\geq 2
, is a vector of real functions that are assumed to be well-defined for all
(x,t)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}_{0}
,
\hat{D}
is a diffusion matrix, which can be any square matrix as long as (
1
) is well-posed and the assumptions below hold,
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
are parameters of the system, and
\mathbb{M}
is the mass matrix of the system, which can be any square matrix, as long as (
1
) is well-posed. Note that writing the system in this way allows the analysis to be applied to systems of
n\geq 1
equations containing higher, even order spatial derivatives. For example, a fourth-order equation
\partial_{t}u_{1}=\partial_{xxxx}u_{1}
can be written as (
1
) simply by adding the extra equation
\partial_{xx}u_{1}-u_{2}=0
.

In this article, we aim to generalize the method of
exponential asymptotics
to study
homoclinic snaking
near codimension-two Turing bifurcation points under the assumption that such a phenomenon occurs. Specifically, we will determine conditions for the existence of Maxwell points, where the homogeneous steady state has the same energy as a large-amplitude periodic orbit, and construct approximations of the localized structures that appear near such points.

Specifically, assuming that localized solutions appear around a Maxwell point that exists close to a codimension-two Turing bifurcation point, where conditions for the latter will also be determined, then there exists real numbers,
\beta_{1}>0
,
\beta_{3}<0
, and
\eta
; a complex number,
K_{2}
; and a rational function
R(\varepsilon)
, such that homoclinic snaking exists for
b=\hat{b}+\delta b
, where
\hat{b}
is the value of
b
at the Maxwell point, and

\displaystyle\absolutevalue{\delta b}\leq\absolutevalue{\frac{\sqrt{2} \, \beta_3}{2 \, \beta_1^2 \, R(\varepsilon)}}\,\frac{\pi\,\absolutevalue{K_2}\,e^{-\frac{\pi}{2}\,\left(\frac{1}{k\,\sqrt{\beta_{1}}\,\varepsilon^{2}}+\eta\right)}}{\varepsilon^{6}},

(2)

where
k
is the critical wavenumber at the codimension-two Turing bifurcation point, and
\varepsilon
is a measure of the separation in
a
from the codimension-two Turing bifurcation point. Furthermore,
\beta_{1}
,
\beta_{3}
,
\eta
,
K_{2}
, and
R
have closed-form definitions that will be provided in this article, and there is code available in
[
24
]
to compute them for an arbitrary
n
-component reaction-diffusion system. Details on how to use the code are provided in
F
.

To start developing ideas
, we assume that (
1
) has a homogeneous steady state
\mathbf{P}=\mathbf{P}(a,b)
such that
\mathbf{f}(\mathbf{P};a,b)=\mathbf{0}
for all
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
, with
J_{\mathbf{u}}\,\mathbf{f}(\mathbf{P};a,b)
, the Jacobian matrix of
\mathbf{f}(\mathbf{u};a,b)
at
\mathbf{u}=\mathbf{P}
, being an invertible matrix.

Furthermore, we assume that
\mathbf{P}
goes through a codimension-two Turing bifurcation point when
(a,b)=\left(a_{0},b_{0}\right)
. That is, there exists
k^{*}>0
, the
wavenumber
, such that

\displaystyle\det\left(J_{\mathbf{u}}\,\mathbf{f}\left(\mathbf{P};a_{0},b_{0}\right)-\left(k^{*}\right)^{2}\,\hat{D}\right)=0,

\displaystyle\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial k}\left(\det\left(J_{\mathbf{u}}\,\mathbf{f}\left(\mathbf{P};a_{0},b_{0}\right)-k^{2}\,\hat{D}\right)\right)\right|_{k=k^{*}}=0,\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle\dim\left(\ker\left(J_{\mathbf{u}}\,\mathbf{f}\left(\mathbf{P};a_{0},b_{0}\right)-\left(k^{*}\right)^{2}\,\hat{D}\right)\right)=1.

Furthermore, we assume that

\displaystyle\det\left(J_{\mathbf{u}}\,\mathbf{f}\left(\mathbf{P};a_{0},b_{0}\right)-k^{2}\,\hat{D}\right)\neq 0,

(3)

for all
k\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\left\{k^{*}\right\}
; and the existence of localized structures close to this codimension-two bifurcation point.

Without loss of generality, we assume that
\mathbf{P}=\mathbf{0}
for all
a,b
. If not, we can make the change
\mathbf{u}\to\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{P}
, which translates
\mathbf{P}
to the origin. Furthermore, for the same reason, without loss of generality, we assume that
\left(a_{0},b_{0}\right)=(0,0)
. Moreover, for simplicity of notation, we will omit the dependence on
a
and
b
and drop the star sign of the critical wavenumber
k^{*}
when there is no place for confusion.

The process we are about to follow comprises the steps shown in Figure
2
, which comprise

1.

Obtain an expression for the
amplitude equation centred at a codimension-2 Turing bifurcation point and determine conditions for the existence of a Maxwell point.
(Section
2
).

2.

Expand the system about the singularities of such amplitudes, which involves the computation of inner and outer solutions, which need to be matched (Sections
3
and
4
).

3.

Determine the order of
n
at which we truncate our asymptotic expansion optimally and study the equation for the remainder (Sections
5
and
6
).

4.

Find conditions that let us ensure that the remainder tends to zero as its corresponding independent variable tends to
\pm\infty
,
which will require the study of Stokes’ lines, where the key exponentially small contribution to our solution triggers, letting us
ensure that our expansion will be well-defined, providing us with an estimation for the width of the homoclinic snaking (end of Section
6
).

Figure 2
:
Diagram of the steps we are to follow to determine the bounds of the homoclinic snaking.

1.2
Outline

The outline of this article is as follows. Section
2
starts by introducing the standard notation used throughout the article, and computes a regular asymptotic expansion up to
third
order of the kind of solutions we are interested in.
The expansion up to order five is carried out in
A
. Also, for later use, we will require going up to seventh order; those results are presented in
D
. Sections
3
and
4
consider the behaviour of the asymptotic expansion as the order tends to
\infty
, with the two sections dealing respectively with late-term expansion in an inner and an outer region. Section
5
then develops the equation for the remainder up to fifth order after truncating the expansion at a high order, without considering the forcing due to truncation. As for the amplitude equation, it will be required to develop the equation for the remainder up to seventh order; those results are presented in
E
. The effect of this forcing is considered in Section
6
, which gives a final condition to determine the width of the snaking.
C
shows how to match solutions at the boundaries of the domains where different expansions are valid in order to join fronts.
B
shows what happens in the case in which a generic
expansion is not suitable to determine the width of the snake
, and, to illustrate the theory, Section
7
presents results from several examples in which this generic assumption holds and one in which it does not. In each case, we evaluate the specific regular and beyond-all-orders coefficients and compare the results with numerical computations. Note that this evaluation step is computationally cumbersome, but we provide the reader with code in an associated GitHub repository,
[
24
]
, which can be used to replicate the results, as well as compute the snaking width for other examples.
F
explains the logic of the code in
[
24
]
, to let everyone compute the width of the snaking easily for any
n
-component reaction-diffusion systems.
Finally, Section
8
presents a brief discussion and suggests avenues for future work.

2
Regular asymptotics

We start the analysis of our system by performing a regular asymptotic expansion. This process has been carried out in different ways in the past in order to study the criticality of Turing bifurcations (see, e.g.
[
25
]
). However, near a codimension-two Turing bifurcation point, localized solutions are expected to appear apart from the periodic states. To study such solutions, we need to obtain a general and accurate expression for the amplitude of different patterns that arise near a codimension-two Turing bifurcation. As we explain below, such an expression will have singularities that need to be studied to control the convergence of the solution we aim to build through this process.

We start by introducing some notation to be used throughout this article.

2.1
Including parameters and kinetics in a general way

First, we scale
x\to x/k
and expand (
1
) as:

\displaystyle\mathbb{M}\,\frac{\partial\mathbf{u}}{\partial t}=\sum_{i=0}^{M_{a}}\sum_{j=0}^{M_{b}}a^{i}\,b^{j}\,\mathbf{f}_{i,j}(\mathbf{u})+k^{2}\,\hat{D}\,\frac{\partial^{2}\mathbf{u}}{\partial x^{2}},

(4)

where
M_{a},M_{b}>0
are the degrees of the right-hand side of (
4
) in terms of
a
and
b
, respectively (they could be infinite) Here, we assume that for every
i,j
,
\mathbf{f}_{i,j}
is an analytic function on
\mathbf{u}
, which implies that we can expand it using Taylor series up to order infinity. In particular, if we Taylor-expand each function
\mathbf{f}_{i,j}
around
\mathbf{u}=\mathbf{0}
, the system becomes

\displaystyle\mathbb{M}\,\frac{\partial\mathbf{u}}{\partial t}=\sum_{i=0}^{M_{a}}\sum_{j=0}^{M_{b}}a^{i}\,b^{j}\sum_{m\geq 1}\frac{1}{m!}\,\left(\sum_{r=1}^{n}u_{r}\,\frac{\partial}{\partial u_{r}}\right)^{m}\mathbf{f}_{i,j}(\mathbf{0})+k^{2}\,\hat{D}\,\frac{\partial^{2}\mathbf{u}}{\partial x^{2}}.

We are interested in the study of stationary solutions, so we assume that
\dfrac{\partial\mathbf{u}}{\partial t}=\mathbf{0}
. Let
0<\varepsilon\ll 1
be a small positive number. Define a long spatial variable by
X=\varepsilon^{2}\,x
. Thus, the system becomes

\displaystyle\mathbf{0}=\sum_{i=0}^{M_{a}}\sum_{j=0}^{M_{b}}a^{i}\,b^{j}\sum_{m\geq 1}\frac{1}{m!}\,\left(\sum_{r=1}^{n}u_{r}\,\frac{\partial}{\partial u_{r}}\right)^{m}\mathbf{f}_{i,j}(\mathbf{0})+k^{2}\,\hat{D}\,\left(\frac{\partial^{2}\mathbf{u}}{\partial x^{2}}+2\,\varepsilon^{2}\,\frac{\partial^{2}\mathbf{u}}{\partial x\partial X}+\varepsilon^{4}\,\frac{\partial^{2}\mathbf{u}}{\partial X^{2}}\right).

(5)

Furthermore, consider the following expansions:

\displaystyle\mathbf{u}

\displaystyle\to\sum_{r=1}^{N}\varepsilon^{r}\,\mathbf{u}^{[r]}+\mathbf{R}_{N},

\displaystyle a

\displaystyle\to\sum_{p\geq 1}a_{p}\,\varepsilon^{p},

\displaystyle b

\displaystyle\to\sum_{q\geq 1}b_{q}\,\varepsilon^{q}+\delta b,

where
\mathbf{R}_{N}
stands for the remainder of our approximation of the solution after truncating the asymptotic expansion at order
N
, for every pair of integers
p,q\geq 1
,
a_{p},b_{q}
are real numbers that approximate the Maxwell point, and
\delta b
is the separation of
b
from such a point. Our first goal is then to find an approximation of the Maxwell point that exists close to codimension-two Turing bifurcation points, together with an approximation of a solution that joins the homogeneous steady state
\mathbf{0}
to a large-amplitude periodic orbit with the same minimal energy as
\mathbf{0}
. We do this by finding suitable parameters in the asymptotic expansion to find a Maxwell point, whilst making sure that said asymptotic expansion is valid.

Now, for each integer
0\leq i\leq M_{a},0\leq j\leq M_{b}
, we introduce the following symmetric, multilinear vector functions

\displaystyle\mathbf{F}_{2,i,j}\left(\mathbf{v}_{1},\mathbf{v}_{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2!}\,\left.\left(\sum_{1\leq p_{1},p_{2}\leq n}v_{1,p_{1}}\,v_{2,p_{2}}\,\frac{\partial^{2}\mathbf{f}_{i,j}(\mathbf{u})}{\partial u_{p_{1}}\partial u_{p_{2}}}\right)\right|_{\mathbf{u}=\mathbf{0}},

(6)

\displaystyle\mathbf{F}_{3,i,j}\left(\mathbf{v}_{1},\mathbf{v}_{2},\mathbf{v}_{3}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{3!}\,\left.\left(\sum_{1\leq p_{1},p_{2},p_{3}\leq n}v_{1,p_{1}}\,v_{2,p_{2}}\,v_{3,p_{3}}\,\frac{\partial^{3}\mathbf{f}_{i,j}(\mathbf{u})}{\partial u_{p_{1}}\partial u_{p_{2}}\partial u_{p_{3}}}\right)\right|_{\mathbf{u}=\mathbf{0}},

\displaystyle\mathbf{F}_{4,i,j}\left(\mathbf{v}_{1},\mathbf{v}_{2},\mathbf{v}_{3},\mathbf{v}_{4}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4!}\,\left.\left(\sum_{1\leq p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4}\leq n}v_{1,p_{1}}\,v_{2,p_{2}}\,v_{3,p_{3}}\,v_{4,p_{4}}\,\frac{\partial^{4}\mathbf{f}_{i,j}}{\partial u_{p_{1}}\partial u_{p_{2}}\partial u_{p_{3}}\partial u_{p_{4}}}\right)\right|_{\mathbf{u}=\mathbf{0}},

\displaystyle\mathbf{F}_{5,i,j}\left(\mathbf{v}_{1},\mathbf{v}_{2},\mathbf{v}_{3},\mathbf{v}_{4},\mathbf{v}_{5}\right)

\displaystyle=\begin{multlined}\frac{1}{5!}\,\left.\left(\sum_{1\leq p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4},p_{5}\leq n}v_{1,p_{1}}\,v_{2,p_{2}}\,v_{3,p_{3}}\,v_{4,p_{4}}\,v_{5,p_{5}}\right.\right.\\
\times\left.\left.\frac{\partial^{5}\mathbf{f}_{i,j}}{\partial u_{p_{1}}\partial u_{p_{2}}\partial u_{p_{3}}\partial u_{p_{4}}\partial u_{p_{5}}}\right)\right|_{\mathbf{u}=\mathbf{0}},\end{multlined}

where
\mathbf{v}_{\ell}=\left(v_{\ell,1},\ldots,v_{\ell,n}\right)^{\intercal}
, for
\ell=1,\ldots,5
, and we note that these functions can be naturally generalized to higher orders.

Also, for simplicity of notation, we define

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{\ell}=J_{\mathbf{u}}\,\mathbf{f}_{0,0}(\mathbf{0})-\ell^{2}\,k^{2}\,\hat{D},

for each non-negative integer
\ell
, and we note that
\mathcal{M}_{\ell}
is invertible for every
\ell\neq 1
.

2.2
Construction of a regular amplitude equation

This section aims to show how to develop a regular asymptotic expansion for the amplitude of patterns arising at a Turing bifurcation,
A_{1}=A_{1}(X)\in\mathbb{C}
, under the ansatz
u\sim A_{1}\,e^{ix}\,\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]}
where
\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]}
is an eigenvector of
\mathcal{M}_{1}
. In particular, we want to show that
A_{1}
satisfies an amplitude equation of the form

\displaystyle\alpha_{1}\,A_{1}^{\prime\prime}+i\,\alpha_{2}\,A_{1}^{\prime}+i\,\alpha_{3}\,\absolutevalue{A_1}^{2}\,A_{1}^{\prime}+i\,\alpha_{4}\,A_{1}^{2}\,\bar{A}_{1}^{\prime}+\alpha_{5}\,A_{1}+\alpha_{6}\,\absolutevalue{A_1}^{2}\,A_{1}+\alpha_{7}\,\absolutevalue{A_1}^{4}A_{1}=0,

(7)

where
\alpha_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
for each
i=1,\ldots,7
, and
\alpha_{1}\neq 0
. For this, we perform an asymptotic expansion of system (
5
) in terms of
\varepsilon
. We do this order by order as follows.

Order
\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{\varepsilon})
.

At this order, (
5
) becomes

\displaystyle\mathbf{0}=J_{\mathbf{u}}\,\mathbf{f}_{0,0}(\mathbf{0})\,\mathbf{u}^{[1]}+k^{2}\,\hat{D}\,\frac{\partial^{2}\mathbf{u}^{[1]}}{\partial x^{2}},

which implies

\displaystyle\mathbf{u}^{[1]}=A_{1}\,e^{i\hat{x}}\,\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]}+c.c.,

(8)

where
c.c.
stands for the complex-conjugate of the term to the left of it,
\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]}\neq\mathbf{0}
fulfills

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{1}\,\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]}=\mathbf{0},

(9)

and
\hat{x}=x-\hat{\chi}
, where
\hat{\chi}
is a real variable that stands for a spatial translation in
x
, which plays a key degree of freedom one needs for matching solutions in
C
. However, for simplicity of notation, when there is no confusion, we will simply write
x
instead of
\hat{x}
.

Order
\mathcal{O}\left(\varepsilon^{2}\right)
.

At this order, (
5
) becomes

\displaystyle\mathbf{0}=J_{\mathbf{u}}\,\mathbf{f}_{0,0}(\mathbf{0})\,\mathbf{u}^{[2]}+a_{1}\,J_{\mathbf{u}}\,\mathbf{f}_{1,0}(\mathbf{0})\,\mathbf{u}^{[1]}+b_{1}\,J_{\mathbf{u}}\,\mathbf{f}_{0,1}(\mathbf{0})\,\mathbf{u}^{[1]}+\mathbf{F}_{2,0,0}\left(\mathbf{u}^{[1]},\mathbf{u}^{[1]}\right)+k^{2}\,\hat{D}\,\frac{\partial^{2}\mathbf{u}^{[2]}}{\partial x^{2}},

which implies

\displaystyle\left(J_{\mathbf{u}}\,\mathbf{f}_{0,0}(\mathbf{0})+k^{2}\,\hat{D}\,\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial x^{2}}\right)\,\mathbf{u}^{[2]}

\displaystyle=-a_{1}\,A_{1}\,e^{ix}\,J_{\mathbf{u}}\,\mathbf{f}_{1,0}(\mathbf{0})\,\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]}-b_{1}\,A_{1}\,e^{ix}\,J_{\mathbf{u}}\,\mathbf{f}_{0,1}(\mathbf{0})\,\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]}

\displaystyle\quad-\mathbf{F}_{2,0,0}\left(\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]},\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]}\right)\left(\absolutevalue{A_1}^{2}+A_{1}^{2}\,e^{2ix}\right)+c.c.

Therefore, as this equation is linear, we have that

\displaystyle\mathbf{u}^{[2]}=\absolutevalue{A_1}^{2}\,\mathbf{W}_{0}^{[2]}+A_{1}\,e^{ix}\,\mathbf{W}_{1}^{[2]}+A_{1}^{2}\,e^{2ix}\,\mathbf{W}_{2}^{[2]}+A_{2}\,e^{ix}\,\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]}+c.c.,

where
A_{2}=A_{2}(X)
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{0}\,\mathbf{W}_{0}^{[2]}

\displaystyle=-\mathbf{F}_{2,0,0}\left(\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]},\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]}\right),

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{1}\,\mathbf{W}_{1}^{[2]}

\displaystyle=-a_{1}\,J_{\mathbf{u}}\,\mathbf{f}_{1,0}(\mathbf{0})\,\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]}-b_{1}\,J_{\mathbf{u}}\,\mathbf{f}_{0,1}(\mathbf{0})\,\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]},

(10)

and

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{2}\,\mathbf{W}_{2}^{[2]}

\displaystyle=-\mathbf{F}_{2,0,0}\left(\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]},\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]}\right)

Here, we recall that
\det\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{M}}_{1}\right)=0
. Therefore, (
10
) might not have a solution for every value of
a_{1},b_{1}\in\mathbb{R}
. For this expansion to be valid, we need all the equations to have a solution, so we make use of the Fredholm alternative, which states that for a generic Fredholm operator,
L
, we have that

\displaystyle\mbox{im}(L)^{\perp}=\ker\left(L^{*}\right),

(11)

In particular, using the usual inner product of vectors in
\mathbb{C}^{n}
, we have that for a real matrix
M
,
M^{*}=M^{\intercal}
. Considering this, we define
\bm{\psi}_{\pm}=e^{\pm ix}\,\bm{\psi}
, where
\bm{\psi}\neq\mathbf{0}
fulfills

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{1}^{\intercal}\,\bm{\psi}=\mathbf{0}.

(12)

With this, and using (
11
), we choose
a_{1},b_{1}\in\mathbb{R}
such that

\displaystyle\left\langle a_{1}\,J_{\mathbf{u}}\,\mathbf{f}_{1,0}(\mathbf{0})\,\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]}+b_{1}\,J_{\mathbf{u}}\,\mathbf{f}_{0,1}(\mathbf{0})\,\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]},\bm{\psi}\right\rangle=0,

(13)

which ensures that (
10
) has a solution.

Order
\mathcal{O}\left(\varepsilon^{3}\right)
.

At this order, (
5
) becomes

\displaystyle\mathbf{0}=J_{\mathbf{u}}\,\mathbf{f}_{0,0}(\mathbf{0})\,\mathbf{u}^{[3]}+a_{1}\,J_{\mathbf{u}}\,\mathbf{f}_{1,0}(\mathbf{0})\,\mathbf{u}^{[2]}+b_{1}\,J_{\mathbf{u}}\,\mathbf{f}_{0,1}(\mathbf{0})\,\mathbf{u}^{[2]}+a_{2}\,J_{\mathbf{u}}\,\mathbf{f}_{1,0}(\mathbf{0})\,\mathbf{u}^{[1]}+b_{2}\,J_{\mathbf{u}}\,\mathbf{f}_{0,1}(\mathbf{0})\,\mathbf{u}^{[1]}

\displaystyle+a_{1}^{2}\,J_{\mathbf{u}}\,\mathbf{f}_{2,0}(\mathbf{0})\,\mathbf{u}^{[1]}+a_{1}\,b_{1}\,J_{\mathbf{u}}\,\mathbf{f}_{1,1}(\mathbf{0})\,\mathbf{u}^{[1]}+b_{1}^{2}\,J_{\mathbf{u}}\,\mathbf{f}_{0,2}(\mathbf{0})\,\mathbf{u}^{[1]}+2\,\mathbf{F}_{2,0,0}\left(\mathbf{u}^{[1]},\mathbf{u}^{[2]}\right)

\displaystyle+a_{1}\,\mathbf{F}_{2,1,0}\left(\mathbf{u}^{[1]},\mathbf{u}^{[1]}\right)+b_{1}\,\mathbf{F}_{2,0,1}\left(\mathbf{u}^{[1]},\mathbf{u}^{[1]}\right)+\mathbf{F}_{3,0,0}\left(\mathbf{u}^{[1]},\mathbf{u}^{[1]},\mathbf{u}^{[1]}\right)

\displaystyle+k^{2}\,\hat{D}\,\left(\frac{\partial^{2}\mathbf{u}^{[3]}}{\partial x^{2}}+2\,\frac{\partial^{2}\mathbf{u}^{[1]}}{\partial x\partial X}\right),

which implies

\displaystyle\left(J_{\mathbf{u}}\,\mathbf{f}_{0,0}(\mathbf{0})+k^{2}\,\hat{D}\,\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial x^{2}}\right)\,\mathbf{u}^{[3]}

\displaystyle=-a_{1}\,J_{\mathbf{u}}\,\mathbf{f}_{1,0}(\mathbf{0})\,\mathbf{u}^{[2]}-b_{1}\,J_{\mathbf{u}}\,\mathbf{f}_{0,1}(\mathbf{0})\,\mathbf{u}^{[2]}-a_{2}\,J_{\mathbf{u}}\,\mathbf{f}_{1,0}(\mathbf{0})\,\mathbf{u}^{[1]}

\displaystyle\quad-b_{2}\,J_{\mathbf{u}}\,\mathbf{f}_{0,1}(\mathbf{0})\,\mathbf{u}^{[1]}-a_{1}^{2}\,J_{\mathbf{u}}\,\mathbf{f}_{2,0}(\mathbf{0})\,\mathbf{u}^{[1]}-a_{1}\,b_{1}\,J_{\mathbf{u}}\,\mathbf{f}_{1,1}(\mathbf{0})\,\mathbf{u}^{[1]}

\displaystyle\quad-b_{1}^{2}\,J_{\mathbf{u}}\,\mathbf{f}_{0,2}(\mathbf{0})\,\mathbf{u}^{[1]}-2\,\mathbf{F}_{2,0,0}\left(\mathbf{u}^{[1]},\mathbf{u}^{[2]}\right)-a_{1}\,\mathbf{F}_{2,1,0}\left(\mathbf{u}^{[1]},\mathbf{u}^{[1]}\right)

\displaystyle\quad-b_{1}\,\mathbf{F}_{2,0,1}\left(\mathbf{u}^{[1]},\mathbf{u}^{[1]}\right)-\mathbf{F}_{3,0,0}\left(\mathbf{u}^{[1]},\mathbf{u}^{[1]},\mathbf{u}^{[1]}\right)-2\,k^{2}\,\hat{D}\,\frac{\partial^{2}\mathbf{u}^{[1]}}{\partial x\partial X},

Therefore, the solution to this equation is given by:

\displaystyle\mathbf{u}^{[3]}

\displaystyle=\absolutevalue{A_1}^{2}\,\mathbf{W}_{0}^{[3]}+A_{1}\,e^{ix}\,\mathbf{W}_{1}^{[3]}+\absolutevalue{A_1}^{2}A_{1}\,e^{ix}\,\mathbf{W}_{1,2}^{[3]}+i\,A_{1}^{\prime}\,e^{ix}\,\mathbf{W}_{1,3}^{[3]}+A_{1}^{2}\,e^{2ix}\,\mathbf{W}_{2}^{[3]}

\displaystyle\quad+A_{1}^{3}\,e^{3ix}\,\mathbf{W}_{3}^{[3]}+2\,A_{1}\,\bar{A}_{2}\,\mathbf{W}_{0}^{[2]}+A_{2}\,e^{ix}\,\mathbf{W}_{1}^{[2]}+A_{3}\,e^{ix}\,\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]}+2\,A_{1}\,A_{2}\,e^{2ix}\,\mathbf{W}_{2}^{[2]}+c.c.,

where
A_{3}=A_{3}(X)
, the sign
′
denotes differentiation with respect to
X
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{0}\,\mathbf{W}_{0}^{[3]}

\displaystyle=-a_{1}\,J_{\mathbf{u}}\,\mathbf{f}_{1,0}(\mathbf{0})\,\mathbf{W}_{0}^{[2]}-b_{1}\,J_{\mathbf{u}}\,\mathbf{f}_{0,1}(\mathbf{0})\,\mathbf{W}_{0}^{[2]}-2\,\mathbf{F}_{2,0,0}\left(\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]},\mathbf{W}_{1}^{[2]}\right)

\displaystyle\quad-a_{1}\,\mathbf{F}_{2,1,0}\left(\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]},\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]}\right)-b_{1}\,\mathbf{F}_{2,0,1}\left(\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]},\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]}\right),

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{1}\,\mathbf{W}_{1}^{[3]}

\displaystyle=-a_{1}\,J_{\mathbf{u}}\,\mathbf{f}_{1,0}(\mathbf{0})\,\mathbf{W}_{1}^{[2]}-b_{1}\,J_{\mathbf{u}}\,\mathbf{f}_{0,1}(\mathbf{0})\,\mathbf{W}_{1}^{[2]}-a_{2}\,J_{\mathbf{u}}\,\mathbf{f}_{1,0}(\mathbf{0})\,\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]}-b_{2}\,J_{\mathbf{u}}\,\mathbf{f}_{0,1}(\mathbf{0})\,\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]}

(14)

\displaystyle-a_{1}^{2}\,J_{\mathbf{u}}\,\mathbf{f}_{2,0}(\mathbf{0})\,\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]}-a_{1}\,b_{1}\,J_{\mathbf{u}}\,\mathbf{f}_{1,1}(\mathbf{0})\,\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]}-b_{1}^{2}\,J_{\mathbf{u}}\,\mathbf{f}_{0,2}(\mathbf{0})\,\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]},

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{1}\,\mathbf{W}_{1,2}^{[3]}=-2\,\mathbf{F}_{2,0,0}\left(\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]},2\,\mathbf{W}_{0}^{[2]}+\mathbf{W}_{2}^{[2]}\right)-3\,\mathbf{F}_{3,0,0}\left(\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]},\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]},\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]}\right),

(15)

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{1}\,\mathbf{W}_{1,3}^{[3]}=-2\,k^{2}\,\hat{D}\,\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]},

(16)

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{2}\,\mathbf{W}_{2}^{[3]}

\displaystyle=-a_{1}\,J_{\mathbf{u}}\,\mathbf{f}_{1,0}(\mathbf{0})\,\mathbf{W}_{2}^{[2]}-b_{1}\,J_{\mathbf{u}}\,\mathbf{f}_{0,1}(\mathbf{0})\,\mathbf{W}_{2}^{[2]}-2\,\mathbf{F}_{2,0,0}\left(\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]},\mathbf{W}_{1}^{[2]}\right)

\displaystyle\quad-a_{1}\,\mathbf{F}_{2,1,0}\left(\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]},\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]}\right)-b_{1}\,\mathbf{F}_{2,0,1}\left(\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]},\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]}\right),

and

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{3}\,\mathbf{W}_{3}^{[3]}=-2\,\mathbf{F}_{2,0,0}\left(\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]},\mathbf{W}_{2}^{[2]}\right)-\mathbf{F}_{3,0,0}\left(\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]},\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]},\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]}\right).

Note that, as stated after (
3
), when
(a,b)=(0,0)
, we are at a codimension-two Turing bifurcation point. Therefore, from
[
25
]
, we have that

\displaystyle\left\langle 2\,\mathbf{F}_{2,0,0}\left(\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]},2\,\mathbf{W}_{0}^{[2]}+\mathbf{W}_{2}^{[2]}\right)+3\,\mathbf{F}_{3,0,0}\left(\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]},\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]},\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]}\right),\bm{\psi}\right\rangle=0,

which implies that (
15
) has a solution.

On the other hand, to ensure that (
14
) has a solution, we need that
a_{1},b_{1},a_{2},b_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
fulfill

\displaystyle\left\langle a_{1}\,J_{\mathbf{u}}\,\mathbf{f}_{1,0}(\mathbf{0})\,\mathbf{W}_{1}^{[2]}+b_{1}\,J_{\mathbf{u}}\,\mathbf{f}_{0,1}(\mathbf{0})\,\mathbf{W}_{1}^{[2]}+a_{2}\,J_{\mathbf{u}}\,\mathbf{f}_{1,0}(\mathbf{0})\,\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]}+b_{2}\,J_{\mathbf{u}}\,\mathbf{f}_{0,1}(\mathbf{0})\,\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]}\right.

\displaystyle\left.+a_{1}^{2}\,J_{\mathbf{u}}\,\mathbf{f}_{2,0}(\mathbf{0})\,\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]}+a_{1}\,b_{1}\,J_{\mathbf{u}}\,\mathbf{f}_{1,1}(\mathbf{0})\,\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]}+b_{1}^{2}\,J_{\mathbf{u}}\,\mathbf{f}_{0,2}(\mathbf{0})\,\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]},\bm{\psi}\right\rangle=0.

Next, to see that (
16
) has a solution, we consider the following standard result:

Lemma 1
.

For
\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]}
and
\bm{\psi}
defined in (
9
) and (
12
), respectively, we have that

\displaystyle\left\langle\hat{D}\,\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]},\bm{\psi}\right\rangle=0.

Proof.

For each
k\in\mathbb{R}
, we can think of
\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]}=\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]}(k)
as a function of
k
is defined by the equation

\displaystyle\left(J_{\mathbf{u}}\,\,\mathbf{f}_{0,0}(\mathbf{0})-k^{2}\,\hat{D}\right)\,\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]}=\lambda(k)\,\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]},

where
\lambda(k)
represents the eigenvalue of
J_{\mathbf{u}}\,\,\mathbf{f}_{0,0}(\mathbf{0})-k^{2}\,\hat{D}
with the largest real part such that
\lambda\left(k^{*}\right)=0
for the value
k=k^{*}
where the Turing bifurcation occurs. With this, after differentiating this expression with respect to
k
, we have

\displaystyle-2\,k\,\hat{D}\,\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]}+\left(J_{\mathbf{u}}\,\,\mathbf{f}_{0,0}(\mathbf{0})-k^{2}\,\hat{D}\right)\,\frac{\differential\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]}}{\differential k}=\frac{\differential\lambda}{\differential k}(k)\,\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]}+\lambda(k)\,\frac{\differential\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]}}{\differential k}.

Therefore, when evaluating
k
at

k^{*}
—and dropping the star sign—, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{1}\,\frac{\differential\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]}}{\differential k}=2\,k\,\hat{D}\,\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]}.

Thus, when applying the inner product with respect to
\bm{\psi}
, we see that

\displaystyle 2\,k\,\left\langle\hat{D}\,\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]},\bm{\psi}\right\rangle=\left\langle\mathcal{M}_{1}\,\frac{\differential\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]}}{\differential k},\bm{\psi}\right\rangle=\left\langle\frac{\differential\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]}}{\differential k},\mathcal{M}_{1}^{\intercal}\,\bm{\psi}\right\rangle=0,

which concludes the proof.
∎

The rest of the calculation needed to obtain the amplitude equation at order five, together with the definitions of its coefficients (see (
7
)), can be carried out, but they are cumbersome, so we move those details to
A
.

2.3
Solving the amplitude equation

In this section, we look for heteroclinic orbits in (
7
), between the trivial steady state
A_{1}=0
and a finite-amplitude periodic pattern, whose amplitude is to be determined. We are interested in such fronts because they help us define the Maxwell point and, generically, give rise to homoclinic snaking, which we aim to study through
an expansion
beyond all orders.

To solve (
7
), we write
A_{1}
in polar coordinates as
A_{1}=R_{1}\,e^{i\,\varphi_{1}}
, where
R_{1}=R_{1}(X)\in\mathbb{R}
and
\varphi_{1}=\varphi_{1}(X)\in\mathbb{R}
. With this, (
7
) becomes

\displaystyle\alpha_{1}\,R_{1}^{\prime\prime}-\alpha_{1}\,R_{1}\,\left(\varphi_{1}^{\prime}\right)^{2}-\alpha_{2}\,R_{1}\,\varphi_{1}^{\prime}-\alpha_{3}\,R_{1}^{3}\,\varphi_{1}^{\prime}+\alpha_{4}\,R_{1}^{3}\,\varphi_{1}^{\prime}+\alpha_{5}\,R_{1}+\alpha_{6}\,R_{1}^{3}+\alpha_{7}\,R_{1}^{5}

\displaystyle+i\,\left(\alpha_{1}\,R_{1}\,\varphi_{1}^{\prime\prime}+2\,\alpha_{1}\,R_{1}^{\prime}\,\varphi_{1}^{\prime}+\alpha_{2}\,R_{1}^{\prime}+\left(\alpha_{3}+\alpha_{4}\right)\,R_{1}^{2}\,R_{1}^{\prime}\right)=0,

which can be split into two equations given by

\displaystyle\alpha_{1}\,R_{1}^{\prime\prime}-\alpha_{1}\,R_{1}\,\left(\varphi_{1}^{\prime}\right)^{2}-\alpha_{2}\,R_{1}\,\varphi_{1}^{\prime}-\alpha_{3}\,R_{1}^{3}\,\varphi_{1}^{\prime}+\alpha_{4}\,R_{1}^{3}\,\varphi_{1}^{\prime}+\alpha_{5}\,R_{1}+\alpha_{6}\,R_{1}^{3}+\alpha_{7}\,R_{1}^{5}=0,

(17)

and

\displaystyle\alpha_{1}\,R_{1}\,\varphi_{1}^{\prime\prime}+2\,\alpha_{1}\,R_{1}^{\prime}\,\varphi_{1}^{\prime}+\alpha_{2}\,R_{1}^{\prime}+\left(\alpha_{3}+\alpha_{4}\right)\,R_{1}^{2}\,R_{1}^{\prime}=0.

(18)

Now, note that (
18
) is equivalent to

\displaystyle R_{1}^{2}\,\varphi_{1}^{\prime\prime}+2\,R_{1}\,R_{1}^{\prime}\,\varphi_{1}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\alpha_{3}+\alpha_{4}}{\alpha_{1}}\,R_{1}^{3}\,R_{1}^{\prime}-\frac{\alpha_{2}}{\alpha_{1}}\,R_{1}\,R_{1}^{\prime},

which can be integrated directly to obtain

\displaystyle\varphi_{1}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\alpha_{3}+\alpha_{4}}{4\,\alpha_{1}}\,R_{1}^{2}-\frac{\alpha_{2}}{2\,\alpha_{1}}.

(19)

On the other hand, when replacing (
19
) into (
17
), we obtain

\displaystyle 2\,R_{1}^{\prime\prime}=\frac{\differential V}{\differential R_{1}},

(20)

where

\displaystyle\frac{\differential V}{\differential R_{1}}

\displaystyle=2\,\beta_{1}\,R_{1}+4\,\beta_{3}\,R_{1}^{3}+6\,\beta_{5}\,R_{1}^{5},

\displaystyle\beta_{1}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{\alpha_{1}}\,\left(\alpha_{5}+\frac{\alpha_{2}^{2}}{4\,\alpha_{1}}\right),

\displaystyle\beta_{3}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2\,\alpha_{1}}\,\left(\alpha_{6}+\frac{\alpha_{2}\,\left(\alpha_{3}-\alpha_{4}\right)}{2\,\alpha_{1}}\right),

(21)

\displaystyle\beta_{5}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{3\,\alpha_{1}}\left(\alpha_{7}+\frac{\left(\alpha_{3}+\alpha_{4}\right)\left(3\,\alpha_{3}-5\,\alpha_{4}\right)}{16\,\alpha_{1}}\right),

which implies that these new coefficients,
\beta_{1}
,
\beta_{3}
,
\beta_{5}
are shifted and scaled versions of
\alpha_{5}
,
\alpha_{6}
and
\alpha_{7}
according to the spatially-related terms present in (
7
).

Note that (
20
) is invariant under the change
X\to\upsilon-X
for every
\upsilon\in\mathbb{R}
. Furthermore, we have that
V
represents an energy potential which, for simplicity, we subject to the condition
V(0)=0
. This implies that

\displaystyle V\left(R_{1}\right)=\beta_{1}\,R_{1}^{2}+\beta_{3}\,R_{1}^{4}+\beta_{5}\,R_{1}^{6},

which has critical points at
R_{1}=0
and

\displaystyle R_{1,\pm}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{-\beta_{3}\pm\sqrt{\beta_{3}^{2}-3\,\beta_{1}\,\beta_{5}}}{3\,\beta_{5}}}.

We need these critical points to be real, so we require
\beta_{1}\,\beta_{5}>0
,
\beta_{3}\,\beta_{5}<0
, and
\beta_{3}^{2}\geq 3\,\beta_{1}\,\beta_{5}
.

Furthermore, note that

\displaystyle\frac{\differential^{2}V}{\differential R_{1}^{2}}(0)

\displaystyle=2\,\beta_{1},\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle\frac{\differential^{2}V}{\differential R_{1}^{2}}\left(R_{1,+}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{8}{3}\,\frac{\beta_{3}^{2}-3\,\beta_{1}\,\beta_{5}-\beta_{3}\,\sqrt{\beta_{3}^{2}-3\,\beta_{1}\,\beta_{5}}}{\beta_{5}}.

(22)

To find a Maxwell point, we need to find the condition under which
R_{1}=0
and
R_{1}=R_{1,+}
have the same minimal energy, which implies that we need
\beta_{1}>0
,
\beta_{3}<0
, and
\beta_{5}>0
. Furthermore, with these assumptions, we have that (
22
) is positive, which implies that
R_{1,+}
is also a minimum.

In addition, we require
R_{1}=0
to have the same minimal energy as
R_{1,+}
. Therefore, we need

\displaystyle V(0)=V\left(R_{1,+}\right)\qquad\iff\qquad\beta_{3}^{2}=4\,\beta_{1}\,\beta_{5},

(23)

which defines a first approximation to the Maxwell point and is an assumption we make from now on.

With this, if we multiply (
20
) by
R_{1}^{\prime}
and integrate it once, we obtain

\displaystyle\left(R_{1}^{\prime}\right)^{2}=R_{1}^{2}\,\left(\beta_{1}+\beta_{3}\,R_{1}^{2}+\beta_{5}\,R_{1}^{4}\right),

(24)

where we note that the right-hand side is a positive multiple of a parabola in
R_{1}^{2}
with a positive leading-order coefficient and a discriminant equal to zero due to (
23
). This implies that the right-hand side of (
24
) is non-negative, making (
24
) well-defined.

Let us now consider the following change
R_{1}^{2}=1/\mathcal{P}
, which implies:

\displaystyle R_{1}^{\prime}=-\frac{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}{2\,\mathcal{P}^{3/2}}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\left(\frac{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}{2\,\mathcal{P}^{3/2}}\right)^{2}=\frac{\beta_{1}}{\mathcal{P}}+\frac{\beta_{3}}{\mathcal{P}^{2}}+\frac{\beta_{5}}{\mathcal{P}^{3}}.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{P}^{\prime}\right)^{2}=4\,\left(\beta_{1}\,\mathcal{P}^{2}+\beta_{3}\,\mathcal{P}+\beta_{5}\right).

We proceed to solve this equation using the method of separation of variables. In particular, if we consider the negative branch of this equation when taking the square root, we have that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\,\int\frac{\differential\mathcal{P}}{\sqrt{\beta_{1}\,\mathcal{P}^{2}+\beta_{3}\,\mathcal{P}+\beta_{5}}}=-X+C,

where
C\in\mathbb{R}
is a constant of integration. Now, let
\mathcal{Q}=\sqrt{\beta_{1}\,\mathcal{P}^{2}+\beta_{3}\,\mathcal{P}+\beta_{5}}
. Then,

\displaystyle\beta_{1}\,\mathcal{P}^{2}+\beta_{3}\,\mathcal{P}+\beta_{5}-\mathcal{Q}^{2}=0,

which implies

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}=\frac{-\beta_{3}\pm\sqrt{\beta_{3}^{2}-4\,\beta_{1}\,\left(\beta_{5}-\mathcal{Q}^{2}\right)}}{2\,\beta_{1}}=\frac{-\beta_{3}\pm 2\,\sqrt{\beta_{1}}\,\mathcal{Q}}{2\,\beta_{1}}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\differential\mathcal{P}=\pm\,\frac{\differential\mathcal{Q}}{\sqrt{\beta_{1}}}.

With this, we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\,\int\frac{\differential\mathcal{P}}{\sqrt{\beta_{1}\,\mathcal{P}^{2}+\beta_{3}\,\mathcal{P}+\beta_{5}}}=\frac{1}{2}\,\int\frac{\differential\mathcal{Q}}{\mathcal{Q}\,\sqrt{\beta_{1}}}=\frac{1}{2\,\sqrt{\beta_{1}}}\,\log\left(\sqrt{\beta_{1}\,\mathcal{P}^{2}+\beta_{3}\,\mathcal{P}+\beta_{5}}\right),

where we choose the positive sign for
\mathcal{P}
, as it needs to be non-negative, by definition.

With this, we note that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\,\sqrt{\beta_{1}}}\,\log\left(\sqrt{\beta_{1}\,\mathcal{P}^{2}+\beta_{3}\,\mathcal{P}+\beta_{5}}\right)

\displaystyle=-X+C

\displaystyle\iff\beta_{1}\,\mathcal{P}^{2}+\beta_{3}\,\mathcal{P}+\beta_{5}

\displaystyle=C^{2}\,\exp\left(-4\,\sqrt{\beta_{1}}\,X\right),

which yields

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}=\frac{1}{R_{1}^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{-\beta_{3}+\sqrt{\beta_{3}^{2}-4\,\beta_{1}\,\left(\beta_{5}-C^{2}\,\exp\left(-4\,\sqrt{\beta_{1}}\,X\right)\right)}}{2\,\beta_{1}}

\displaystyle=\frac{-\beta_{3}+2\,\sqrt{\beta_{1}}\,C\,\exp\left(-2\,\sqrt{\beta_{1}}\,X\right)}{2\,\beta_{1}}

Therefore, we conclude that

\displaystyle R_{1}^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{4\,\beta_{1}}{-2\,\beta_{3}+\exp\left(-2\,\sqrt{\beta_{1}}\,X\right)},

(25)

where we have taken
C=\dfrac{1}{4\,\sqrt{\beta_{1}}}
, for simplicity. Note that (
25
) corresponds to an up-front since

\displaystyle\lim_{X\to-\infty}R_{1}^{2}=0,\qquad\text{and}\qquad\lim_{X\to\infty}R_{1}^{2}=-\frac{2\,\beta_{1}}{\beta_{3}}>0.

On the other hand, as equation (
20
) is invariant under the change
X\to-X
, then the down-front

\displaystyle\tilde{R}_{1}^{2}(X)=\frac{4\,\beta_{1}}{-2\,\beta_{3}+\exp\left(2\,\sqrt{\beta_{1}}\,X\right)},

(26)

is also a solution to (
20
). Figure
3
depicts these two fronts. Specifically, the red (respectively, blue) dashed line represents the up-front (respectively, down-front) given by (
25
) (respectively, (
26
)). On the other hand, the green continuous lines show an oscillatory pattern enclosed by these fronts. These two solutions’ existence is key for studying late terms in the asymptotic expansion, as their existence and matching provide conditions on some parameters of the expansion (see
C
).

Figure 3
:
Form of solutions captured by the amplitude equation, (
7
), at the Maxwell point. The red and blue dashed curves represent the envelope of oscillatory localized solutions to (
4
), represented by
R_{1}
. Specifically, the blue dashed line represents an up-front joining
R_{1}=0
to
R_{1}=\sqrt{-\dfrac{2\,\beta_{1}}{\beta_{3}}}
(represented by the top and bottom black horizontal lines) from left to right, whilst the red dashed curve represents a translated reflection of the blue curve, which turns out to be a down-front joining the same steady states in the opposite direction. Furthermore, the green continuous curve represents the oscillatory pattern enclosed by that envelope.

To keep consistency with notation, we focus
in what comes next
on the up-front, (
25
). The analysis for the down-front is analogous. This implies

\displaystyle\varphi_{1}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\frac{\beta_{1}\,\left(\alpha_{3}+\alpha_{4}\right)}{\alpha_{1}\left(2\,\beta_{3}-\exp\left(-2\,\sqrt{\beta_{1}}\,X\right)\right)}-\frac{\alpha_{2}}{2\,\alpha_{1}},

so
\varphi_{1}
takes the following form:

\displaystyle\varphi_{1}=\eta\,\log\left(1-2\,\beta_{3}\,\exp\left(2\sqrt{\beta_{1}}\,X\right)\right)-2\,\xi\,\sqrt{\beta_{1}}\,X-\xi\,\log\left(-2\,\beta_{3}\right)-\eta\,\log\left(2\,\sqrt{\beta_{1}}\right),

(27)

where

\displaystyle\xi=\frac{\alpha_{2}}{4\,\alpha_{1}\,\sqrt{\beta_{1}}},\qquad\text{and}\qquad\eta=\frac{\left(\alpha_{3}+\alpha_{4}\right)\,\sqrt{\beta_{1}}}{4\,\alpha_{1}\,\beta_{3}}.

(28)

Now, to simplify notation, we consider the following translation:

\displaystyle X\to-\frac{\log\left(-2\,\beta_{3}\right)}{2\,\sqrt{\beta_{1}}}+X,

which transforms (
25
) and (
27
) into

\displaystyle R_{1}^{2}

\displaystyle=-\frac{2\,\beta_{1}}{\beta_{3}}\,\frac{\exp\left(2\,\sqrt{\beta_{1}}\,X\right)}{1+\exp\left(2\,\sqrt{\beta_{1}}\,X\right)},

which has singularities at
X_{j}=\dfrac{(2\,j+1)\,\pi\,i}{2\,\sqrt{\beta_{1}}}
for
j\in\mathbb{Z}
, and

\displaystyle\varphi_{1}=\eta\,\log\left(1+\exp\left(2\sqrt{\beta_{1}}X\right)\right)-2\,\xi\,\sqrt{\beta_{1}}\,X-\eta\,\log\left(2\,\sqrt{\beta_{1}}\right).

respectively. With this, we note that

\displaystyle A_{1}

\displaystyle=R_{1}\,e^{i\,\varphi_{1}}

\displaystyle=\left(2\,\sqrt{\beta_{1}}\right)^{1-\eta\,i}\,\left(-2\,\beta_{3}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\,\sqrt{\frac{1}{1+\exp\left(-2\,\sqrt{\beta_{1}}\,X\right)}}\,\left(1+\exp\left(2\,\sqrt{\beta_{1}}\,X\right)\right)^{\eta\,i}

\displaystyle\quad\times\exp\left(-2\,i\,\sqrt{\beta_{1}}\,\xi\,X\right).

Now, we note that the leading-order expansion of different functions associated with
A_{1}(X)
around
X_{0}=\dfrac{\pi\,i}{2\,\sqrt{\beta_{1}}}
, which turns out to be one of the singularities of
R_{1}
that is closest to the real axis in
X

(
together with
X_{-1}
)
, is given by

\displaystyle A_{1}\,\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]}

\displaystyle\sim-i\,C_{A}\,\sqrt{\frac{-1}{X_{0}-X}}\left(X_{0}-X\right)^{\eta i}\,\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]},

\displaystyle\bar{A}_{1}\,\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]}

\displaystyle\sim-i\,C_{\bar{A}}\,\sqrt{\frac{-1}{X_{0}-X}}\left(X_{0}-X\right)^{-\eta i}\,\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]},

\displaystyle\absolutevalue{A_1}^{2}\,\mathbf{W}_{0}^{[2]}=R_{1}^{2}\,\mathbf{W}_{0}^{[2]}

\displaystyle\sim C_{A}\,C_{\bar{A}}\,\left(X_{0}-X\right)^{-1}\,\mathbf{W}_{0}^{[2]},

\displaystyle A_{1}^{2}\,\mathbf{W}_{2}^{[2]}

\displaystyle\sim C_{A}^{2}\,\left(X_{0}-X\right)^{-1+2\,\eta\,i}\,\mathbf{W}_{2}^{[2]},

\displaystyle\bar{A}_{1}^{2}\,\mathbf{W}_{2}^{[2]}

\displaystyle\sim C_{\bar{A}}^{2}\,\left(X_{0}-X\right)^{-1-2\,\eta\,i}\,\mathbf{W}_{2}^{[2]},

\displaystyle\absolutevalue{A_1}^{2}\,A_{1}\,\mathbf{W}_{1,2}^{[3]}=R_{1}^{2}\,A_{1}\,\mathbf{W}_{1,2}^{[3]}

\displaystyle\sim i\,C_{A}^{2}\,C_{\bar{A}}\,\left(\frac{-1}{X_{0}-X}\right)^{3/2}\left(X_{0}-X\right)^{\eta i}\,\mathbf{W}_{1,2}^{[3]},

\displaystyle\absolutevalue{A_1}^{2}\,\bar{A}_{1}\,\mathbf{W}_{1,2}^{[3]}=R_{1}^{2}\,\bar{A}_{1}\,\mathbf{W}_{1,2}^{[3]}

\displaystyle\sim i\,C_{A}\,C_{\bar{A}}^{2}\,\left(\frac{-1}{X_{0}-X}\right)^{3/2}\left(X_{0}-X\right)^{-\eta i}\,\mathbf{W}_{1,2}^{[3]},

\displaystyle i\,A_{1}^{\prime}\,\mathbf{W}_{1,3}^{[3]}

\displaystyle\sim-\frac{i}{2}\,(2\,\eta+i)\,C_{A}\,\sqrt{\frac{-1}{X_{0}-X}}\,\left(X_{0}-X\right)^{-1+\eta i}\,\mathbf{W}_{1,3}^{[3]},

\displaystyle-i\,\bar{A}_{1}^{\prime}\,\mathbf{W}_{1,3}^{[3]}

\displaystyle\sim-\frac{i}{2}\,(2\,\eta-i)\,C_{A}\,\sqrt{\frac{-1}{X_{0}-X}}\,\left(X_{0}-X\right)^{-1-\eta i}\,\mathbf{W}_{1,3}^{[3]},

\displaystyle A_{1}^{3}\,\mathbf{W}_{3}^{[3]}

\displaystyle\sim i\,C_{A}^{3}\,\left(\frac{-1}{X_{0}-X}\right)^{3/2}\left(X_{0}-X\right)^{3\,\eta\,i}\,\mathbf{W}_{3}^{[3]},

\displaystyle\bar{A}_{1}^{3}\,\mathbf{W}_{3}^{[3]}

\displaystyle\sim i\,C_{\bar{A}}^{3}\,\left(\frac{-1}{X_{0}-X}\right)^{3/2}\left(X_{0}-X\right)^{-3\,\eta\,i}\,\mathbf{W}_{3}^{[3]},

\displaystyle\absolutevalue{A_1}^{4}\,\mathbf{W}_{0,2}^{[4]}=R_{1}^{4}\,\mathbf{W}_{0,2}^{[4]}

\displaystyle\sim C_{A}^{2}\,C_{\bar{A}}^{2}\,\left(X_{0}-X\right)^{-2}\,\mathbf{W}_{0,2}^{[4]},

\displaystyle i\,\bar{A}_{1}\,A_{1}^{\prime}\,\mathbf{W}_{0,3}^{[4]}

\displaystyle\sim\frac{i}{2}\,(1-2\,\eta\,i)\,C_{A}\,C_{\bar{A}}\,\left(X_{0}-X\right)^{-2}\,\mathbf{W}_{0,3}^{[4]},

\displaystyle-i\,A_{1}\,\bar{A}_{1}^{\prime}\,\mathbf{W}_{0,3}^{[4]}

\displaystyle\sim-\frac{i}{2}\,(1+2\,\eta\,i)\,C_{A}\,C_{\bar{A}}\,\left(X_{0}-X\right)^{-2}\,\mathbf{W}_{0,3}^{[4]},

\displaystyle\absolutevalue{A_1}^{2}\,A_{1}^{2}\,\mathbf{W}_{2,2}^{[4]}=R_{1}^{2}\,A_{1}^{2}\,\mathbf{W}_{2,2}^{[4]}

\displaystyle\sim C_{A}^{3}\,C_{\bar{A}}\,\left(X_{0}-X\right)^{-2+2\,\eta\,i}\,\mathbf{W}_{2,2}^{[4]},

\displaystyle\absolutevalue{A_1}^{2}\,\bar{A}_{1}^{2}\,\mathbf{W}_{2,2}^{[4]}=R_{1}^{2}\,\bar{A}_{1}^{2}\,\mathbf{W}_{2,2}^{[4]}

\displaystyle\sim C_{A}\,C_{\bar{A}}^{3}\,\left(X_{0}-X\right)^{-2-2\,\eta\,i}\,\mathbf{W}_{2,2}^{[4]},

\displaystyle i\,A_{1}\,A_{1}^{\prime}\,\mathbf{W}_{2,3}^{[4]}

\displaystyle\sim\frac{i}{2}\,(1-2\,\eta\,i)\,C_{A}^{2}\,\left(X_{0}-X\right)^{-2+2\,\eta\,i}\,\mathbf{W}_{2,3}^{[4]},

\displaystyle-i\,\bar{A}_{1}\,\bar{A}_{1}^{\prime}\,\mathbf{W}_{2,3}^{[4]}

\displaystyle\sim-\frac{i}{2}\,(1+2\,\eta\,i)\,C_{\bar{A}}^{2}\,\left(X_{0}-X\right)^{-2-2\,\eta\,i}\,\mathbf{W}_{2,3}^{[4]}.

where

\displaystyle C_{A}

\displaystyle=i\,e^{\pi\,\xi}\,\left(2\,\sqrt{\beta_{1}}\right)^{1/2}\,\left(-2\,\beta_{3}\right)^{-1/2},

(29)

\displaystyle C_{\bar{A}}

\displaystyle=i\,e^{-\pi\,\xi}\,\left(2\,\sqrt{\beta_{1}}\right)^{1/2}\,\left(-2\,\beta_{3}\right)^{-1/2}.

Remark 1
.

First, note that

\displaystyle C_{\bar{A}}

\displaystyle=-e^{-2\,\pi\,\xi}\,\bar{C}_{A}.

(30)

Furthermore, observe that the expression of the leading-order term of
A_{1}
at
X_{0}
can be simplified even further if we assume

\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{-1}{X_{0}-X}}=i\,\sqrt{\frac{1}{X_{0}-X}}.

Nonetheless, as we are now working with a complex variable to carry out the analysis, we have to bear in mind that, in the complex plane, some functions are not uniquely defined. We will then state everything in this manner to maintain a general approach to development.

3
Late-term expansion — inner solution

In this section, we aim to study the asymptotic behaviour of our
amplitude
, under the assumption that we carry out the asymptotic expansion up to a high order
N\gg 1
. In particular, as explained in
[
10
]
, to understand the leading-order asymptotics in this case, which allows us to obtain an approximation of the width of the homoclinic snaking, we must find two kinds of solutions: an
inner
and an
outer
solution. The inner solution refers to an approximation that is valid only sufficiently close to a singularity, and the outer solution lets us understand the behaviour of solutions away from it. Of course, as these solutions are intended to represent different approximations of the same function, they need to be matched as
X
tends to the singularity.

In particular, using the natural extensions of the symmetric multilinear vector functions we defined in (
6
), let
M>0
be an integer and define
\mathbf{p}=\left(p_{1},p_{2},\ldots\right)
,
\mathbf{q}=\left(q_{1},q_{2},\ldots\right)
,
\mathbf{n}=\left(n_{1},\ldots,n_{M}\right)
,
\mathbf{r}=\left(r_{1},\ldots,r_{M}\right)
,
\mathbf{e}_{M}=(1,\ldots,1)
(the vector with
M
ones) and
\mathbf{e}=(1,2,\ldots,M)
. With this, assuming that
n
is sufficiently large, and Taylor expanding (
5
), we see that the
n
-th order equation is given by

\mathbf{0}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p=0\\
\mathbf{p}\cdot\mathbf{e}=p\end{subarray}}^{n-1}\,\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q=0\\
\mathbf{q}\cdot\mathbf{e}=q\end{subarray}}^{n-1-p}C_{\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}}\,\left(\prod_{s\geq 1}a_{s}^{p_{s}}\right)\,\left(\prod_{s\geq 1}b_{s}^{q_{s}}\right)\,J_{\mathbf{u}}\,\mathbf{f}_{p,q}(\mathbf{0})\,\mathbf{u}^{[n-p-q]}\\
+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p=0\\
\mathbf{p}\cdot\mathbf{e}=p\end{subarray}}^{n-1}\,\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q=0\\
\mathbf{q}\cdot\mathbf{e}=q\end{subarray}}^{n-1-p}\,\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}M=2\\
\mathbf{n}\cdot\mathbf{e}_{M}=n-p-q\end{subarray}}^{n-p-q}C_{\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}}\,\left(\prod_{s\geq 1}a_{s}^{p_{s}}\right)\,\left(\prod_{s\geq 1}b_{s}^{q_{s}}\right)\,\mathbf{F}_{M,p,q}\left(\mathbf{u}^{[n_{1}]},\ldots,\mathbf{u}^{[n_{M}]}\right)\\
+k^{2}\,\hat{D}\,\left(\frac{\partial^{2}\mathbf{u}^{[n]}}{\partial x^{2}}+2\,\frac{\partial^{2}\mathbf{u}^{[n-2]}}{\partial x\partial X}+\frac{\partial^{2}\mathbf{u}^{[n-4]}}{\partial X^{2}}\right),

(31)

where
C_{\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}}
are the coefficients associated with the powers of
\mathbf{a}
and
\mathbf{b}
in the Taylor expansion of
our vector field
, for each pair of vectors,
\mathbf{p}
and
\mathbf{q}
, defined above. From the results we have obtained so far, we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{u}^{[n]}=\sum_{r=-n}^{n}\mathbf{W}_{n,r}\,e^{irx},

(32)

where
n\geq 1
is an integer, and
\mathbf{W}_{n,r}=\mathbf{W}_{n,r}(X)
for every integers
n\geq 1,-n\leq r\leq n
. In particular, taking into account only the terms that are dominant at each mode as
X\to X_{0}
, we have
\mathbf{W}_{n,-r}=\overline{\mathbf{W}_{n,r}}
for each pair of integers,
n\geq 1,-n\leq r\leq n
,
r\neq 0
, and we note that

\displaystyle\mathbf{W}_{1,1}\,e^{ix}+c.c.

\displaystyle=A_{1}\,e^{ix}\,\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]}+c.c.,

\displaystyle\mathbf{W}_{2,0}

\displaystyle=2\,\absolutevalue{A_1}^{2}\,\mathbf{W}_{0}^{[2]},

\displaystyle\mathbf{W}_{2,2}\,e^{2ix}+c.c.

\displaystyle=A_{1}^{2}\,e^{2ix}\,\mathbf{W}_{2}^{[2]}+c.c.,

\displaystyle\mathbf{W}_{3,1}\,e^{ix}+c.c.

\displaystyle\sim\absolutevalue{A_1}^{2}\,A_{1}\,e^{ix}\,\mathbf{W}_{1,2}^{[3]}+i\,A_{1}^{\prime}\,e^{ix}\,\mathbf{W}_{1,3}^{[3]}+c.c.,

\displaystyle\mathbf{W}_{3,3}\,e^{3ix}+c.c.

\displaystyle=A_{1}^{3}\,e^{3ix}\,\mathbf{W}_{3}^{[3]}+c.c.,

\displaystyle\mathbf{W}_{4,0}

\displaystyle\sim\absolutevalue{A_1}^{4}\,\mathbf{W}_{0,2}^{[4]}+i\,\bar{A}_{1}\,A_{1}^{\prime}\,\mathbf{W}_{0,3}^{[4]}+c.c.,

\displaystyle\mathbf{W}_{4,2}\,e^{2ix}+c.c.

\displaystyle\sim\absolutevalue{A_1}^{2}\,A_{1}^{2}\,e^{2ix}\,\mathbf{W}_{2,2}^{[4]}+i\,A_{1}\,A_{1}^{\prime}\,e^{2ix}\,\mathbf{W}_{2,3}^{[4]}+c.c.

Therefore, when replacing (
32
) into (
31
) we obtain, for
n\geq 1
large and each
r\in[-n,n]
,

\mathbf{0}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p=0\\
\mathbf{p}\cdot\mathbf{e}=p\end{subarray}}^{n-1}\,\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q=0\\
\mathbf{q}\cdot\mathbf{e}=q\end{subarray}}^{n-1-p}C_{\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}}\,\left(\prod_{s\geq 1}a_{s}^{p_{s}}\right)\,\left(\prod_{s\geq 1}b_{s}^{q_{s}}\right)\,J_{\mathbf{u}}\,\mathbf{f}_{p,q}(\mathbf{0})\,\mathbf{W}_{n-p-q,r}\\
+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p=0\\
\mathbf{p}\cdot\mathbf{e}=p\end{subarray}}^{n-1}\,\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q=0\\
\mathbf{q}\cdot\mathbf{e}=q\end{subarray}}^{n-1-p}\,\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}M=2\\
\mathbf{n}\cdot\mathbf{e}_{M}=n-p-q\end{subarray}}^{n-p-q}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{r}\cdot\mathbf{e}_{M}=r\\
\absolutevalue{r_\ell}\leq n_{\ell}\end{subarray}}C_{\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}}\,\left(\prod_{s\geq 1}a_{s}^{p_{s}}\right)\,\left(\prod_{s\geq 1}b_{s}^{q_{s}}\right)\,\mathbf{F}_{M,p,q}\left(\mathbf{W}_{n_{1},r_{1}},\ldots,\mathbf{W}_{n_{M},r_{M}}\right)\\
+k^{2}\,\hat{D}\,\left(-r^{2}\,\mathbf{W}_{n,r}+2\,i\,r\,\mathbf{W}_{n-2,r}^{\prime}+\mathbf{W}_{n-4,r}^{\prime\prime}\right),

(33)

3.1
Asymptotic expansion

Again, what we need to do now is to solve a linear equation order by order. We highlight that the following analysis is valid for any singularity, but we focus on
X_{0}
, as it is one of the singularities closest to the real axis for
X
. In particular, based on
[
10
]

and the order we obtained for
A_{1}
, when expanded around
X_{0}
, we use the ansatz

\displaystyle\mathbf{W}_{n,r}(X)=\frac{\mathbf{V}_{n,r}}{\left(X_{0}-X\right)^{\frac{n}{2}-r\,\eta\,i}},

(34)

for our inner solution, which implies

\displaystyle\mathbf{W}_{n-2,r}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\frac{n-2-2\,r\,\eta\,i}{2}\,\frac{\mathbf{V}_{n-2,r}}{\left(X_{0}-X\right)^{\frac{n}{2}-r\,\eta\,i}},

\displaystyle\mathbf{W}_{n-4,r}^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle=\frac{(n-2-2\,r\,\eta\,i)(n-4-2\,r\,\eta\,i)}{4}\,\frac{\mathbf{V}_{n-4,r}}{\left(X_{0}-X\right)^{\frac{n}{2}-r\,\eta\,i}}.

Therefore, when replacing (
34
) into (
33
), we obtain

\mathbf{0}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p=0\\
\mathbf{p}\cdot\mathbf{e}=p\end{subarray}}^{n-1}\,\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q=0\\
\mathbf{q}\cdot\mathbf{e}=q\end{subarray}}^{n-1-p}C_{\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}}\,\left(\prod_{s\geq 1}a_{s}^{p_{s}}\right)\,\left(\prod_{s\geq 1}b_{s}^{q_{s}}\right)\,J_{\mathbf{u}}\,\mathbf{f}_{p,q}(\mathbf{0})\,\frac{\mathbf{V}_{n-p-q,r}}{\left(X_{0}-X\right)^{\frac{n-p-q}{2}-r\,\eta\,i}}\\
+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p=0\\
\mathbf{p}\cdot\mathbf{e}=p\end{subarray}}^{n-1}\,\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q=0\\
\mathbf{q}\cdot\mathbf{e}=q\end{subarray}}^{n-1-p}\,\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}M=2\\
\mathbf{n}\cdot\mathbf{e}_{M}=n-p-q\end{subarray}}^{n-p-q}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{r}\cdot\mathbf{e}_{M}=r\\
\absolutevalue{r_\ell}\leq n_{\ell}\end{subarray}}C_{\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}}\,\left(\prod_{s\geq 1}a_{s}^{p_{s}}\right)\,\left(\prod_{s\geq 1}b_{s}^{q_{s}}\right)\\
\times\mathbf{F}_{M,p,q}\left(\frac{\mathbf{V}_{n_{1},r_{1}}}{\left(X_{0}-X\right)^{\frac{n_{1}}{2}-r_{1}\,\eta\,i}},\ldots,\frac{\mathbf{V}_{n_{M},r_{M}}}{\left(X_{0}-X\right)^{\frac{n_{M}}{2}-r_{M}\,\eta\,i}}\right)\\
+k^{2}\,\hat{D}\,\left(-r^{2}\,\frac{\mathbf{V}_{n,r}}{\left(X_{0}-X\right)^{\frac{n}{2}-r\,\eta\,i}}+2\,i\,r\,\left(\frac{n-2-2\,r\,\eta\,i}{2}\,\frac{\mathbf{V}_{n-2,r}}{\left(X_{0}-X\right)^{\frac{n}{2}-r\,\eta\,i}}\right)\right.\\
\left.+\frac{(n-2-2\,r\,\eta\,i)\,(n-4-2\,r\,\eta\,i)}{4}\,\frac{\mathbf{V}_{n-4,r}}{\left(X_{0}-X\right)^{\frac{n}{2}-r\,\eta\,i}}\right),

which is equivalent to

\mathbf{0}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p=0\\
\mathbf{p}\cdot\mathbf{e}=p\end{subarray}}^{n-1}\,\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q=0\\
\mathbf{q}\cdot\mathbf{e}=q\end{subarray}}^{n-1-p}C_{\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}}\,\left(\prod_{s\geq 1}a_{s}^{p_{s}}\right)\,\left(\prod_{s\geq 1}b_{s}^{q_{s}}\right)\,J_{\mathbf{u}}\,\mathbf{f}_{p,q}(\mathbf{0})\,\frac{\mathbf{V}_{n-p-q,r}}{\left(X_{0}-X\right)^{\frac{-p-q}{2}}}\\
+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p=0\\
\mathbf{p}\cdot\mathbf{e}=p\end{subarray}}^{n-1}\,\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q=0\\
\mathbf{q}\cdot\mathbf{e}=q\end{subarray}}^{n-1-p}\,\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}M=2\\
\mathbf{n}\cdot\mathbf{e}_{M}=n-p-q\end{subarray}}^{n-p-q}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{r}\cdot\mathbf{e}_{M}=r\\
\absolutevalue{r_\ell}\leq n_{\ell}\end{subarray}}\left(\frac{C_{\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}}}{\left(X_{0}-X\right)^{\frac{-p-q}{2}}}\,\left(\prod_{s\geq 1}a_{s}^{p_{s}}\right)\,\left(\prod_{s\geq 1}b_{s}^{q_{s}}\right)\right.\\
\times\left.\mathbf{F}_{M,p,q}\left(\mathbf{V}_{n_{1},r_{1}},\ldots,\mathbf{V}_{n_{M},r_{M}}\right)\vphantom{\frac{C_{\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}}}{\left(X_{0}-X\right)^{\frac{-p-q}{2}}}}\right)\\
+k^{2}\,\hat{D}\,\left(-r^{2}\,\mathbf{V}_{n,r}+2\,i\,r\,\left(\frac{n-2-2\,r\,\eta\,i}{2}\right)\,\mathbf{V}_{n-2,r}\right.\\
\left.+\frac{(n-2-2\,r\,\eta\,i)(n-4-2\,r\,\eta\,i)}{4}\,\mathbf{V}_{n-4,r}\right),

and we observe that the only terms that play a role in the inner solution are those with no parameters. Therefore, we can simplify our equation for the inner solution as

\displaystyle\mathbf{0}

\displaystyle=\left(J_{\mathbf{u}}\,\mathbf{f}_{0,0}(\mathbf{0})-r^{2}\,k^{2}\,\hat{D}\right)\mathbf{V}_{n,r}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}M=2\\
\mathbf{n}\cdot\mathbf{e}_{M}=n\end{subarray}}^{n}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{r}\cdot\mathbf{e}_{M}=r\\
\absolutevalue{r_\ell}\leq n_{\ell}\end{subarray}}\mathbf{F}_{M,0,0}\left(\mathbf{V}_{n_{1},r_{1}},\ldots,\mathbf{V}_{n_{M},r_{M}}\right)

(35)

\displaystyle+k^{2}\,\hat{D}\,\left(2\,i\,r\,\left(\frac{n-2-2\,r\,\eta\,i}{2}\right)\,\mathbf{V}_{n-2,r}+\frac{(n-2-2\,r\,\eta\,i)\,(n-4-2\,r\,\eta\,i)}{4}\,\mathbf{V}_{n-4,r}\right),

Here, note that if we change
r
into
-r
in the previous expression, we have

\mathbf{0}=\left(J_{\mathbf{u}}\,\mathbf{f}_{0,0}(\mathbf{0})-r^{2}\,k^{2}\,\hat{D}\right)\mathbf{V}_{n,-r}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}M=2\\
\mathbf{n}\cdot\mathbf{e}_{M}=n\end{subarray}}^{n}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{r}\cdot\mathbf{e}_{M}=r\\
\absolutevalue{r_\ell}\leq n_{\ell}\end{subarray}}\mathbf{F}_{M,0,0}\left(\mathbf{V}_{n_{1},-r_{1}},\ldots,\mathbf{V}_{n_{M},-r_{M}}\right)\\
+k^{2}\,\hat{D}\,\left(-2\,i\,r\,\left(\frac{n-2+2\,r\,\eta\,i}{2}\right)\,\mathbf{V}_{n-2,-r}+\frac{(n-2+2\,r\,\eta\,i)(n-4+2\,r\,\eta\,i)}{4}\,\mathbf{V}_{n-4,-r}\right).

which gives us the conjugate of (
35
) if, based on (
30
), we consider

\displaystyle\mathbf{V}_{n,-r}=(-1)^{n}\,e^{-2\,r\,\pi\,\xi}\,\overline{\mathbf{V}_{n,r}},

(36)

for every integers
n\geq 1
and
-n\leq r\leq n
.

Now, in order to solve (
35
), we expand each vector
\mathbf{V}_{n,r}
as

\displaystyle\mathbf{V}_{n,r}=\kappa^{n-1}\,\Gamma\left(\frac{n-1}{2}+\gamma_{r}\right)\,\sum_{s\geq 0}\frac{\mathbf{v}_{r}^{[s]}}{n^{s}},

(37)

where
\kappa
and
\gamma_{r}
are complex variables yet to be determined.

Now, noting that

\displaystyle\Gamma\left(\frac{n-1}{2}+\gamma_{r}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{n-3}{2}+\gamma_{r}\right)\Gamma\left(\frac{n-3}{2}+\gamma_{r}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{n-3}{2}+\gamma_{r}\right)\left(\frac{n-5}{2}+\gamma_{r}\right)\Gamma\left(\frac{n-5}{2}+\gamma_{r}\right),

and replacing (
37
) into (
35
), we obtain

\mathbf{0}=\left(J_{\mathbf{u}}\,\mathbf{f}_{0,0}(\mathbf{0})-r^{2}\,k^{2}\,\hat{D}\right)\kappa^{n-1}\,\Gamma\left(\frac{n-1}{2}+\gamma_{r}\right)\,\sum_{s\geq 0}\frac{\mathbf{v}_{r}^{[s]}}{n^{s}}\\
+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}M=2\\
\mathbf{n}\cdot\mathbf{e}_{M}=n\end{subarray}}^{n}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{r}\cdot\mathbf{e}_{M}=r\\
\absolutevalue{r_\ell}\leq n_{\ell}\end{subarray}}\mathbf{F}_{M,0,0}\left(\mathbf{V}_{n_{1},r_{1}},\ldots,\mathbf{V}_{n_{M},r_{M}}\right)\\
+k^{2}\,\hat{D}\,\left(2\,i\,r\,\kappa^{n-3}\,\left(\frac{n-2-2\,r\,\eta\,i}{2}\right)\,\Gamma\left(\frac{n-3}{2}+\gamma_{r}\right)\,\sum_{s\geq 0}\frac{\mathbf{v}_{r}^{[s]}}{(n-2)^{s}}\right.\\
\left.+\kappa^{n-5}\,\frac{(n-2-2\,r\,\eta\,i)(n-4-2\,r\,\eta\,i)}{4}\,\Gamma\left(\frac{n-5}{2}+\gamma_{r}\right)\,\sum_{s\geq 0}\frac{\mathbf{v}_{r}^{[s]}}{(n-4)^{s}}\right),

which is equivalent to

\mathbf{0}=\left(J_{\mathbf{u}}\,\mathbf{f}_{0,0}(\mathbf{0})-r^{2}\,k^{2}\,\hat{D}\right)\kappa^{4}\,\sum_{s\geq 0}\frac{\mathbf{v}_{r}^{[s]}}{n^{s}}\\
+\kappa^{5-n}\,\Gamma\left(\frac{n-1}{2}+\gamma_{r}\right)^{-1}\,\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}M=2\\
\mathbf{n}\cdot\mathbf{e}_{M}=n\end{subarray}}^{n}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{r}\cdot\mathbf{e}_{M}=r\\
\absolutevalue{r_\ell}\leq n_{\ell}\end{subarray}}\mathbf{F}_{M,0,0}\left(\mathbf{V}_{n_{1},r_{1}},\ldots,\mathbf{V}_{n_{M},r_{M}}\right)\\
+k^{2}\,\hat{D}\,\left(2\,i\,r\,\kappa^{2}\left(\frac{n-2-2\,r\,\eta\,i}{n-3+2\,\gamma_{r}}\right)\,\sum_{s\geq 0}\frac{\mathbf{v}_{r}^{[s]}}{(n-2)^{s}}\right.\\
\left.+\frac{(n-2-2\,r\,\eta\,i)(n-4-2\,r\,\eta\,i)}{\left(n-3+2\,\gamma_{r}\right)\left(n-5+2\,\gamma_{r}\right)}\,\sum_{s\geq 0}\frac{\mathbf{v}_{r}^{[s]}}{(n-4)^{s}}\right),

Furthermore, by expanding the previous expression in powers of
1/n
, assuming that
n
is large, we have

\mathbf{0}=\left(J_{\mathbf{u}}\,\mathbf{f}_{0,0}(\mathbf{0})-r^{2}\,k^{2}\,\hat{D}\right)\,\kappa^{4}\,\sum_{s\geq 0}\frac{\mathbf{v}_{r}^{[s]}}{n^{s}}\\
+\kappa^{5-n}\,\Gamma\left(\frac{n-1}{2}+\gamma_{r}\right)^{-1}\,\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}M=2\\
\mathbf{n}\cdot\mathbf{e}_{M}=n\end{subarray}}^{n}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{r}\cdot\mathbf{e}_{M}=r\\
\absolutevalue{r_\ell}\leq n_{\ell}\end{subarray}}\mathbf{F}_{M,0,0}\left(\mathbf{V}_{n_{1},r_{1}},\ldots,\mathbf{V}_{n_{M},r_{M}}\right)\\
+k^{2}\,\hat{D}\,\left(2\,i\,r\,\kappa^{2}\left(1+\frac{-2\,\gamma_{r}-2\,r\,\eta\,i+1}{n}+\frac{\left(2\,\gamma_{r}-3\right)\left(2\,\gamma_{r}+2\,r\,\eta\,i-1\right)}{n^{2}}\right)\right.\\
\times\left(\mathbf{v}_{r}^{[0]}+\left(\frac{1}{n}+\frac{2}{n^{2}}\right)\,\mathbf{v}_{r}^{[1]}+\frac{\mathbf{v}_{r}^{[2]}}{n^{2}}\right)\\
+\left(1+\frac{-4\,\gamma_{r}-4\,r\,\eta\,i+2}{n}-\frac{\left(-2\,i\,\gamma_{r}+2\,\eta\,r+i\right)\left(-6\,i\,\gamma_{r}+2\,r\,\eta+9\,i\right)}{n^{2}}\right)\\
\times\left.\left(\mathbf{v}_{r}^{[0]}+\left(\frac{1}{n}+\frac{4}{n^{2}}\right)\,\mathbf{v}_{r}^{[1]}+\frac{\mathbf{v}_{r}^{[2]}}{n^{2}}\right)\right)+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{n^{3}}\right).

(38)

Now, as
n
is large, then powers of
1/n
can be considered small numbers, which lets us follow the usual approach to study this equation following an asymptotic
procedure on
1/n
.

3.2
Solution at
order

\mathcal{O}(1)

At this order, (
38
) becomes

\displaystyle M(\kappa,r)\,\mathbf{v}_{r}^{[0]}=-\mathbf{NL}(1),

(39)

where

\displaystyle M(\kappa,r)=\kappa^{4}\left(J_{\mathbf{u}}\,\mathbf{f}_{0,0}(\mathbf{0})-r^{2}\,k^{2}\,\hat{D}\right)+\kappa^{2}\left(2\,i\,r\,k^{2}\,\hat{D}\right)+k^{2}\,\hat{D},

and
\mathbf{NL}(1)
, are terms of order
\mathcal{O}(1)
associated with the nonlinearities of system (
4
), which depend on
\gamma_{r}
. We highlight that we will deal with this problem using mode dominance as follows. We define
\gamma
as the value of
\gamma_{r}
with the highest real part as
r
takes integer values between
-\infty
and
\infty
. With this, we can note that
\mathbf{NL}(1)=\mathbf{0}
for the values of
r
such that
\gamma_{r}=\gamma
, assuming they are finite. To see why this is not an assumption, consider the following result:

Lemma 2
.

The implicit function
\kappa=\absolutevalue{\kappa(r)}
determined by
\det(M(\kappa, r))=0
is even and bounded away from
r=\pm 1
. Furthermore, it is finite for
r=\pm 1
and
\displaystyle{\lim_{\absolutevalue{r}\to\infty}\kappa(r)=0}
.

Proof.

Note that

\displaystyle\overline{M(\kappa,r)}=M(\bar{\kappa},-r),

which proves that
\kappa=\absolutevalue{\kappa(r)}
is an even function.

Now, note that
\det(M(\kappa, r))
is a polynomial of degree
4\,n
in
\kappa
for all
\absolutevalue{r}\neq 1
. On the other hand, when
\absolutevalue{r}=1
, the degree of
\det(M(\kappa, r))
decreases to
4\,n-4
. However, due to the fundamental theorem of algebra, these polynomials will have well-defined finite complex roots for all values of
r
.

On the other hand, when
\absolutevalue{r}
gets large,

\displaystyle\det(M(\kappa, r))\sim\det\left(r\,k^{2}\,\kappa^{2}\,\left(-r\,\kappa^{2}+2\,i\right)\,\hat{D}\right).

which implies that
\kappa
must tend to zero as
\absolutevalue{r}\to\infty
in order to fulfill the equation
\det(M(\kappa, r))=0
, which proves the result.
∎

Now, to solve (
39
), we want
\mathbf{v}_{r}^{[0]}
to be non-zero for the values of
r
such that
\gamma_{r}=\gamma
. To determine those values of
r
, we need to find the values of
\kappa
with the largest amplitude such that
\det(M(\kappa, r))=0
, together with the values of
r
that
lead
to those values of
\kappa
, as these will generate the leading-order solution.

Observe that

\displaystyle M(\kappa,r)

\displaystyle=\kappa^{4}\left(J_{\mathbf{u}}\,\mathbf{f}_{0,0}(\mathbf{0})+\left(-r^{2}+\frac{2\,i\,r}{\kappa^{2}}+\frac{1}{\kappa^{4}}\right)\,k^{2}\,\hat{D}\right).

Next, note that there might be complex roots in
\ell
to the equation
\det\left(J_{\mathbf{u}}\,\mathbf{f}_{0,0}(\mathbf{0})-\ell^{2}\,\hat{D}\right)=0
. Nevertheless, as we are focusing on the patterns that arise at a codimension-two Turing bifurcation point, we only focus on the case
\ell\in\mathbb{R}
, which implies that
\ell=k
is the only solution
when we assume that we are working with a codimension-two point at a primary Turing bifurcation
. Thus,
\det(M(\kappa, r))=0
if

\displaystyle-r^{2}+\frac{2\,i\,r}{\kappa^{2}}+\frac{1}{\kappa^{4}}=-1\qquad\iff\qquad\kappa^{2}=\frac{i}{\left(r\pm 1\right)},

(40)

for
r\neq\pm 1
, whilst
\kappa^{2}=\pm i/2
when
r=\pm 1
. This implies that the dominant modes for
\kappa
are attained when
r\in\{0,\pm 2\}
. There is one case that needs extra care, though. That is the case in which system (
4
) is
odd in
\mathbf{u}
. In such a case, the modes
r=0,\pm 2
lead to trivial solutions, making the coming analysis unfeasible
[
13
]
. We will consider the general case for now and will develop the changes needed in the symmetric case in
B
.

Now, we define
\kappa_{\pm}^{2}=\pm i
, and take the following values of
\gamma
in order of dominance, taking into account that an increase of 1 in the value of
n
increases the power of
X_{0}-X
in the denominator of (
34
) by 1/2:

\displaystyle\gamma_{0,\pm 2}=\gamma,\qquad\gamma_{\pm 1}=\gamma-\frac{1}{2},\qquad\text{and}\qquad\gamma_{\pm p}=\gamma-\frac{p-2}{2},\quad\text{for }p\geq 3.

(41)

3.3
The case
\kappa^{2}=\kappa_{+}^{2}

In this case, we have

\displaystyle M\left(\pm\sqrt{i},r\right)

\displaystyle=-\left(J_{\mathbf{u}}\,\mathbf{f}_{0,0}(\mathbf{0})-(r-1)^{2}\,k^{2}\,\hat{D}\right).

In particular,

\displaystyle M\left(\pm\sqrt{i},0\right)

\displaystyle=M\left(\pm\sqrt{i},2\right)=-\left(J_{\mathbf{u}}\,\mathbf{f}_{0,0}(\mathbf{0})-k^{2}\,\hat{D}\right),

which implies that
\mathbf{v}_{0}^{[0]}=c_{0}^{[0]}\,\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]}
, and
\mathbf{v}_{2}^{[0]}=c_{2}^{[0]}\,\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]}
, where
c_{0}^{[0]},c_{2}^{[0]}\in\mathbb{R}
, and

\displaystyle\mathbf{v}_{-4}^{[0]}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{0},

\displaystyle\mathbf{v}_{-{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}3}}^{[0]}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{0},

\displaystyle\mathbf{v}_{-2}^{[0]}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{0},

\displaystyle\mathbf{v}_{-1}^{[0]}

\displaystyle=-2\,\kappa^{3}\,C_{\bar{A}}\,c_{0}^{[0]}\,\mathbf{W}_{2}^{[2]},

\displaystyle\mathbf{v}_{1}^{[0]}

\displaystyle=-2\,\kappa^{3}\,\left(C_{\bar{A}}\,c_{2}^{[0]}+C_{A}\,c_{0}^{[0]}\right)\,\mathbf{W}_{0}^{[2]},

\displaystyle\mathbf{v}_{3}^{[0]}

\displaystyle=-2\,\kappa^{3}\,C_{A}\,c_{2}^{[0]}\,\mathbf{W}_{2}^{[2]},

\displaystyle\mathbf{v}_{4}^{[0]}

\displaystyle=-3\,i\,C_{A}^{2}\,c_{2}^{[0]}\,\mathbf{W}_{3}^{[3]},

With this, we continue to the following order.

Order
\mathcal{O}(1/n)
.

Following the same procedure at this order, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbf{v}_{-2}^{[1]}

\displaystyle=-6\,i\,\mathbf{W}_{3}^{[3]}\,c_{0}^{[0]}\,C_{\bar{A}}^{2},

\displaystyle\mathbf{v}_{-1}^{[1]}

\displaystyle=-2\,\kappa\,C_{\bar{A}}\,\left(C_{\bar{A}}^{2}\,c_{2}^{[0]}\,\mathbf{W}_{2,2}^{[4]}+c_{0}^{[0]}\,\left(3\,C_{A}\,C_{\bar{A}}\,\mathbf{W}_{2,2}^{[4]}+(\eta+i\,(\gamma-1))\,\mathbf{W}_{2,3}^{[4]}\right)\right),

\displaystyle\mathbf{v}_{0}^{[1]}

\displaystyle=-\left(c_{0}^{[0]}\,\left((1-2\,\gamma)\,\mathbf{W}_{1,3}^{[3]}+4\,i\,C_{A}\,C_{\bar{A}}\,\mathbf{W}_{1,2}^{[3]}\right)+2\,i\,C_{\bar{A}}^{2}\,c_{2}^{[0]}\,\mathbf{W}_{1,2}^{[3]}\right),

\displaystyle\mathbf{v}_{1}^{[1]}

\displaystyle=\begin{multlined}-2\,\kappa\,\left(C_{A}\,c_{0}^{[0]}\,\left(4\,C_{A}\,C_{\bar{A}}\,\mathbf{W}_{0,2}^{[4]}+(\eta+\gamma\,i)\,\mathbf{W}_{0,3}^{[4]}\right)\right.\\
\left.+C_{\bar{A}}\,c_{2}^{[0]}\,\left(4\,C_{A}\,C_{\bar{A}}\,\mathbf{W}_{0,2}^{[4]}+(3\,\eta-\gamma\,i)\,\mathbf{W}_{0,3}^{[4]}\right)\right),\end{multlined}

\displaystyle\mathbf{v}_{2}^{[1]}

\displaystyle=-i\,\left(2\,C_{A}\,\left(2\,c_{2}^{[0]}\,C_{\bar{A}}+C_{A}\,c_{0}^{[0]}\right)\,\mathbf{W}_{1,2}^{[3]}+c_{2}^{[0]}\,(-2\,\gamma\,i+4\,\eta+i)\,\mathbf{W}_{1,3}^{[3]}\right),

\displaystyle\mathbf{v}_{3}^{[1]}

\displaystyle=-2\,\kappa\,C_{A}\,\left(C_{A}^{2}\,c_{0}^{[0]}\,\mathbf{W}_{2,2}^{[4]}+c_{2}^{[0]}\,\left(3\,C_{A}\,C_{\bar{A}}\,\mathbf{W}_{2,2}^{[4]}+(-\gamma\,i+3\,\eta+i)\,\mathbf{W}_{2,3}^{[4]}\right)\right),

With this, we are ready to determine a solvability condition at the following order.

Order
\mathcal{O}(1/n^{2})

A
t this order, we obtain two solvability conditions given by

S_{c,0}=-\left(\vphantom{C_{A}^{2}\,C_{\bar{A}}^{2}}(4\,\gamma\,(\gamma-2)+3)\,\alpha_{1}+4\,C_{A}\,C_{\bar{A}}\,\left((\eta+(\gamma-1)\,i)\,\alpha_{3}-(2\,\eta+i)\,\alpha_{4}\right)\right.\\
\left.+12\,C_{A}^{2}\,C_{\bar{A}}^{2}\,\alpha_{7}\right)\,c_{0}^{[0]}\\
+2\,C_{\bar{A}}^{2}\left((-2\,\eta+i)\,\alpha_{3}+(4\,\eta+i\,(1-2\,\gamma))\,\alpha_{4}-4\,C_{A}\,C_{\bar{A}}\,\alpha_{7}\right)\,c_{2}^{[0]}=0,

and

S_{0,2}=-2\,C_{A}^{2}\,\left((2\,\eta+i)\,\alpha_{3}+i\,(1-2\,\gamma)\,\alpha_{4}+4\,C_{A}\,C_{\bar{A}}\,\alpha_{7}\right)\,c_{0}^{[0]}\\
-\left((2\,\gamma-3+4\,\eta\,i)(2\,\gamma-1+4\,\eta\,i)\,\alpha_{1}\right.\\
\left.+4\,C_{A}\,C_{\bar{A}}\left((3\,\eta+i\,(1-\gamma))\,\alpha_{3}+(-2\,\eta+i)\,\alpha_{4}\right)+12\,C_{A}^{2}\,C_{\bar{A}}^{2}\,\alpha_{7}\right)\,c_{2}^{[0]}=0.

Note that this is a linear system of equations for
c_{0}^{[0]}
and
c_{2}^{[0]}
, which has nontrivial solutions when
\beta_{3}^{2}=4\,\beta_{1}\,\beta_{5}
if and only if
its corresponding determinant equals zero, which occurs if and only if

\displaystyle\gamma\in\{-1-\eta\,i,-\eta\,i,2-\eta\,i,3-\eta\,i\}.

Therefore, as
\gamma
was defined to be the value of
\gamma_{r}
with the highest real part, then we take
\gamma=3-\eta\,i
, which yields

\displaystyle c_{2}^{[0]}=\frac{h_{1}}{k_{1}}\,c_{0}^{[0]},

(42)

where

\displaystyle h_{1}

\displaystyle=(4\,\gamma\,(\gamma-2)+3)\,\alpha_{1}+4\,C_{A}\,C_{\bar{A}}\,\left((\eta+(\gamma-1)\,i)\,\alpha_{3}-(2\,\eta+i)\,\alpha_{4}\right)+12\,C_{A}^{2}\,C_{\bar{A}}^{2}\,\alpha_{7},

\displaystyle k_{1}

\displaystyle=2\,C_{\bar{A}}^{2}\left((-2\,\eta+i)\,\alpha_{3}+(4\,\eta+i\,(1-2\,\gamma))\,\alpha_{4}-4\,C_{A}\,C_{\bar{A}}\,\alpha_{7}\right).

3.4
The case
\kappa^{2}=\kappa_{-}^{2}

In this case, we have

\displaystyle M\left(\pm\sqrt{-i},r\right)=-\left(J_{\mathbf{u}}\,\mathbf{f}_{0,0}(\mathbf{0})-(r+1)^{2}\,k^{2}\,\hat{D}\right).

Therefore,
\mathbf{v}_{-2}^{[0]}=c_{-2}^{[0]}\,\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]}
, and
\mathbf{v}_{0}^{[0]}=c_{0}^{[0]}\,\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]}
, where
c_{-2}^{[0]},c_{0}^{[0]}\in\mathbb{R}
, and

\displaystyle\mathbf{v}_{-4}^{[0]}

\displaystyle=3\,i\,C_{\bar{A}}^{2}\,c_{-2}^{[0]}\,\mathbf{W}_{3}^{[3]},

\displaystyle\mathbf{v}_{-3}^{[0]}

\displaystyle=-2\,\kappa^{3}\,C_{\bar{A}}\,c_{-2}^{[0]}\,\mathbf{W}_{2}^{[2]},

\displaystyle\mathbf{v}_{-1}^{[0]}

\displaystyle=-2\,\kappa^{3}\,\mathbf{W}_{0}^{[2]}\,\left(C_{\bar{A}}\,c_{0}^{[0]}+C_{A}\,c_{-2}^{[0]}\right),

\displaystyle\mathbf{v}_{1}^{[0]}

\displaystyle=-2\,\kappa^{3}\,C_{A}\,c_{0}^{[0]}\,\mathbf{W}_{2}^{[2]},

\displaystyle\mathbf{v}_{2}^{[0]}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{0},

\displaystyle\mathbf{v}_{3}^{[0]}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{0},

\displaystyle\mathbf{v}_{4}^{[0]}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{0},

Order
\mathcal{O}(1/n)

At this order, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbf{v}_{-3}^{[1]}

\displaystyle=-2\,\kappa\,C_{\bar{A}}\,\left(C_{\bar{A}}^{2}\,c_{0}^{[0]}\,\mathbf{W}_{2,2}^{[4]}+c_{-2}^{[0]}\,\left(3\,C_{A}\,C_{\bar{A}}\,\mathbf{W}_{2,2}^{[4]}+(3\,\eta+i\,(\gamma-1))\,\mathbf{W}_{2,3}^{[4]}\right)\right),

\displaystyle\mathbf{v}_{-2}^{[1]}

\displaystyle=-\left(c_{-2}^{[0]}\,\left((2\,\gamma-1-4\,\eta\,i)\,\mathbf{W}_{1,3}^{[3]}-4\,i\,C_{A}\,C_{\bar{A}}\,\mathbf{W}_{1,2}^{[3]}\right)-2\,i\,C_{\bar{A}}^{2}\,c_{0}^{[0]}\,\mathbf{W}_{1,2}^{[3]}\right),

\displaystyle\mathbf{v}_{-1}^{[1]}

\displaystyle=\begin{multlined}-2\,\kappa\,\left(C_{A}\,c_{-2}^{[0]}\,\left(4\,C_{A}\,C_{\bar{A}}\,\mathbf{W}_{0,2}^{[4]}+(3\,\eta+\gamma\,i)\,\mathbf{W}_{0,3}^{[4]}\right)\right.\\
\left.+c_{0}^{[0]}\,C_{\bar{A}}\,\left(4\,C_{A}\,C_{\bar{A}}\,\mathbf{W}_{0,2}^{[4]}+(\eta-\gamma\,i)\,\mathbf{W}_{0,3}^{[4]}\right)\right),\end{multlined}

\displaystyle\mathbf{v}_{0}^{[1]}

\displaystyle=-\left((1-2\,\gamma)\,c_{0}^{[0]}\,\mathbf{W}_{1,3}^{[3]}-2\,i\,C_{A}\,\left(2\,C_{\bar{A}}\,c_{0}^{[0]}+C_{A}\,c_{-2}^{[0]}\right)\,\mathbf{W}_{1,2}^{[3]}\right),

\displaystyle\mathbf{v}_{1}^{[1]}

\displaystyle=-2\,C_{A}\,\kappa\,\left(C_{A}^{2}\,c_{-2}^{[0]}\,\mathbf{W}_{2,2}^{[4]}+c_{0}^{[0]}\,\left(3\,C_{A}\,\mathbf{W}_{2,2}^{[4]}\,C_{\bar{A}}+(\eta+(1-\gamma)\,i)\,\mathbf{W}_{2,3}^{[4]}\right)\right),

\displaystyle\mathbf{v}_{2}^{[1]}

\displaystyle=6\,i\,C_{A}^{2}\,c_{0}^{[0]}\,\mathbf{W}_{3}^{[3]},

which implies that the solvability conditions at order
\mathcal{O}\left(1/n^{2}\right)
are given by

S_{c,-2}=\left(-(2\,\gamma-3-4\,i\,\eta)(2\,\gamma-1-4\,i\,\eta)\,\alpha_{1}\right.\\
\left.+4\,C_{A}\,C_{\bar{A}}\,\left(-i\,(\gamma-3\,\eta\,i-1)\,\alpha_{3}+(2\,\eta+i)\,\alpha_{4}\right)-12\,C_{A}^{2}\,C_{\bar{A}}^{2}\,\alpha_{7}\right)\,c_{-2}^{[0]}\\
-2\,C_{\bar{A}}^{2}\left((2\,\eta-i)\,\alpha_{3}+i\,(2\,\gamma-1)\,\alpha_{4}+4\,C_{A}\,C_{\bar{A}}\,\alpha_{7}\right)\,c_{0}^{[0]}=0,

and

S_{c,0}=-2\,C_{A}^{2}\,\left((2\,\eta+i)\,\alpha_{3}+(-4\,\eta+i\,(1-2\,\gamma))\,\alpha_{4}+4\,C_{A}\,C_{\bar{A}}\,\alpha_{7}\right)\,c_{-2}^{[0]}\\
-\left(\vphantom{C_{A}^{2}\,C_{\bar{A}}^{2}\,\alpha_{7}}(4\,\gamma\,(\gamma-2)+3)\,\alpha_{1}+4\,C_{A}\,C_{\bar{A}}\,\left((\eta-i\,(\gamma-1))\,\alpha_{3}-(2\,\eta-i)\,\alpha_{4}\right)\right.\\
\left.+12\,C_{A}^{2}\,C_{\bar{A}}^{2}\,\alpha_{7}\right)\,c_{0}^{[0]}=0.

As before, it can be proven that this system of equations has nontrivial solutions when
\beta_{3}^{2}=4\,\beta_{1}\,\beta_{5}
if and only if

\displaystyle\gamma\in\{-1+\eta\,i,\eta\,i,2+\eta\,i,3+\eta\,i\}.

Furthermore, when
\gamma
takes one of these values, we have

\displaystyle c_{-2}^{[0]}=\frac{\bar{h}_{1}}{\bar{k}_{1}}\,c_{0}^{[0]}.

Moreover, from (
37
), we conclude that
c_{0}^{[0]}
and
c_{\pm 2}^{[0]}
are key terms of our inner solution, which can be obtained via the following limit:

\displaystyle c_{r}^{[0]}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\left\langle\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]},\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]}\right\rangle}\,\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{\kappa^{-(n-1)}\,\left\langle\mathbf{V}_{n,r},\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]}\right\rangle}{\Gamma\left(\frac{n-1}{2}+\gamma\right)},

(43)

for
r=-2,0,2
and the corresponding value of
\kappa
, provided that these limits exist.

3.5
Combining eigenvectors

Now, we note that the solution comprising the dominant values of
\kappa
is given by

\displaystyle\mathbf{u}^{[n]}

\displaystyle=\sum_{r=-n}^{n}\mathbf{W}_{n,r}(X)\,e^{irx}=\sum_{r=-n}^{n}\frac{\mathbf{V}_{n,r}}{\left(X_{0}-X\right)^{\frac{n}{2}-r\,\eta\,i}}\,e^{irx}

\displaystyle=\sum_{r=-n}^{n}\frac{\kappa^{n-1}\,\Gamma\left(\frac{n-1}{2}+\gamma_{r}\right)}{\left(X_{0}-X\right)^{\frac{n}{2}-r\,\eta\,i}}\,\sum_{s\geq 0}\frac{\mathbf{v}_{r}^{[s]}}{n^{s}}\,e^{irx}

\displaystyle\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}n\to\infty\\[2.1097pt]
X\to X_{0}\end{subarray}}{\sim}\sum_{r=0,2}\begin{multlined}\left(\left(\sqrt{i}\right)^{n-1}\,\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{n-1}{2}+\gamma\right)}{\left(X_{0}-X\right)^{\frac{n}{2}-r\,\eta\,i}}\,\mathbf{v}_{r}^{[0]}\,e^{irx}\right.\\
\left.+\left(-\sqrt{i}\right)^{n-1}\,\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{n-1}{2}+\gamma\right)}{\left(X_{0}-X\right)^{\frac{n}{2}-r\,\eta\,i}}\,\mathbf{v}_{r}^{[0]}\,e^{irx}\right)\end{multlined}

\displaystyle\qquad+\sum_{r=-2,0}\begin{multlined}\left(\left(\sqrt{-i}\right)^{n-1}\,\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{n-1}{2}+\gamma\right)}{\left(X_{0}-X\right)^{\frac{n}{2}-r\,\eta\,i}}\,\mathbf{v}_{r}^{[0]}\,e^{irx}\right.\\
\left.+\left(-\sqrt{-i}\right)^{n-1}\,\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{n-1}{2}+\gamma\right)}{\left(X_{0}-X\right)^{\frac{n}{2}-r\,\eta\,i}}\,\mathbf{v}_{r}^{[0]}\,e^{irx}\right)\end{multlined}

\displaystyle=\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{n-1}{2}+\gamma\right)}{\left(X_{0}-X\right)^{\frac{n}{2}}}\,e^{i(n-1)\pi/4}\,\left(\lambda_{1}+(-1)^{n}\,\lambda_{2}\right)\,\left(1+\frac{h_{1}}{k_{1}}\,\frac{e^{2ix}}{\left(X_{0}-X\right)^{-2\,\eta\,i}}\right)\,\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]}

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{n-1}{2}+\bar{\gamma}\right)}{\left(X_{0}-X\right)^{\frac{n}{2}}}\,e^{-i(n-1)\pi/4}\,\left(\lambda_{3}+(-1)^{n}\,\lambda_{4}\right)\,\left(1+\frac{\bar{h}_{1}}{\bar{k}_{1}}\,\frac{e^{-2ix}}{\left(X_{0}-X\right)^{2\,\eta\,i}}\right)\,\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]},

(48)

where
\lambda_{i}
correspond to the corresponding coefficients
c_{r}^{[0]}
we found before for the corresponding dominant value of
\kappa
.

Last but not least, for the inner solution we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{W}_{2\,n,2\,r-1}=\mathbf{0},\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathbf{W}_{2\,n-1,2\,r}=\mathbf{0},\quad\forall\,n\in\mathbb{N},r\in\mathbb{Z}.

Therefore, as
\mathbf{u}^{[n]}
, has even harmonics, we need
\lambda_{2}=\lambda_{1}
, and
\lambda_{4}=\lambda_{3}
, which implies

\displaystyle\mathbf{u}^{[n]}

\displaystyle\sim i^{\frac{n-1}{2}}\,\lambda_{1}\,\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{n-1}{2}+\gamma\right)}{\left(X_{0}-X\right)^{\frac{n}{2}}}\,\left(1+(-1)^{n}\right)\,\left(1+\frac{h_{1}}{k_{1}}\,\frac{e^{2ix}}{\left(X_{0}-X\right)^{-2\,\eta\,i}}\right)\,\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]}

(49)

\displaystyle+(-i)^{\frac{n-1}{2}}\,\lambda_{3}\,\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{n-1}{2}+\bar{\gamma}\right)}{\left(X_{0}-X\right)^{\frac{n}{2}}}\,\left(1+(-1)^{n}\right)\,\left(1+\frac{\bar{h}_{1}}{\bar{k}_{1}}\,\frac{e^{-2ix}}{\left(X_{0}-X\right)^{2\,\eta\,i}}\right)\,\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]}.

Thus, from now on, we assume that
n
is even (when
assumed to be large
).

Remark 2
.

Note that, for each dominant value of
\kappa
we consider, there are two associated dominant values of
r
. Therefore, as there are four dominant values of
\kappa
, the summations in (
48
) and (
49
) comprise eight terms in total. Nevertheless, something similar will happen to the other four terms, so they are omitted.

4
