).
Both of these graphs were obtained with the Mathematica code within the folders

in
[

]
.

).

]
, with the variable

.

With this, we have that

\displaystyle B_{1}

\displaystyle\sim\left(-\frac{\sqrt{2}\,\beta_{3}\,L_{2}^{+}}{4\,\beta_{1}^{2}}-\frac{\sqrt{2}\,\beta_{3}\,L_{2}^{-}}{4\,\beta_{1}^{2}}-\frac{\sqrt{9407-6651\,\sqrt{2}}}{3\,\sqrt{2}\,\alpha_{1}\,\beta_{3}\,\varepsilon^{2}}\,\delta\sigma\right)\,\frac{(1+2\,\eta\,i)}{2}\,\sqrt{-\frac{\beta_{1}}{\beta_{3}}}\,e^{2\,\sqrt{\beta_{1}}\,X}

\displaystyle\sim\begin{multlined}\left(-\frac{\sqrt{2}\,\pi\,\beta_{3}}{\beta_{1}^{2}}\,\frac{\absolutevalue{K_2}\,e^{-\frac{\pi}{2}\,\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{\beta_{1}}\,\varepsilon^{2}}+\eta\right)}}{\varepsilon^{6}}\,\cos\left(K_{2}^{o}-\hat{\chi}+2\,\eta\,\log(\varepsilon)\right)\right.\\
\left.-\frac{\sqrt{9407-6651\,\sqrt{2}}}{3\,\sqrt{2}\,\alpha_{1}\,\beta_{3}\,\varepsilon^{2}}\,\delta\sigma\right)\,\frac{(1+2\,\eta\,i)}{2}\,\sqrt{-\frac{\beta_{1}}{\beta_{3}}}\,e^{2\,\sqrt{\beta_{1}}\,X},\end{multlined}

as
X\to\infty
. Therefore, as in the previous examples, the condition we need to ensure the remainder converges to 0 as
X\to\infty
is given by

\displaystyle\absolutevalue{\delta\sigma}\leq\frac{6\,\pi\,\alpha_{1}\,\beta_{3}^{2}}{\beta_{1}^{2}\,\sqrt{9407-6651\,\sqrt{2}}}\,\frac{\absolutevalue{K_2}\,e^{-\frac{\pi}{2}\,\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{\beta_{1}}\,\varepsilon^{2}}+\eta\right)}}{\varepsilon^{4}},

which is, approximately, given by

\displaystyle\absolutevalue{\Delta\sigma}\leq\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}15.866362}\,e^{-0.594604\,\frac{\pi}{\varepsilon^{2}}}}{\varepsilon^{4}}.

The graph with the matching is shown in Figure
9
, which depicts, once again, that the theory we have developed matches numerics superbly. We highlight that the result shown in
[
12
]
does not appear to be successful because of the choice of the parameters to do the matching. In fact, if one takes
b=\lambda
, then all the powers of
(\delta\lambda)^{p}
will appear in the final expression for
B_{1}
, for every integer
p\geq 1
, and finding a bound in that case is much more intricate. In that case, it would have been better to scale
t\to\lambda\,t
and
x\to\sqrt{\lambda}\,x
in order to carry out this analysis. We also remark that the recurrence (
35
) takes longer to run for this model in comparison to the previous two examples due to the presence of the variable
v
in the denominator of (
94
). Fortunately,
c_{2}^{[0]}
has a tidy behaviour from the beginning (see Figure
8
), so not many terms were necessary to
obtain a good approximation of it
.

\varepsilon\approx 0.394849
(respectively,
\sigma\approx-1.5488164814
).

7.4
Brusselator

In
[
23
]
, the authors developed pattern formation and pattern localization for Schnakenberg-type models used to model chemical reactions or root-hair development. In particular, they proved several results for each of those models, but highlighted that the Brusselator is simple enough so that codimension-two points can be found explicitly, but complex enough to show localized structures (in fact, they proved that there is a way to scale the model so that the coordinates of its codimension-two Turing bifurcation points do not change under a change in the diffusion coefficient). Therefore, we focus on the Brusselator system, which can be written as:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}u

\displaystyle=a-c\,u+u^{2}\,v+\delta^{2}\,\partial_{xx}u,

(96)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}v

\displaystyle=(c-1)\,u-u^{2}\,v+\partial_{xx}v,

and we use
b=c
. This system has only one homogeneous steady state given by

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}=\left(a,\frac{c-1}{a}\right).

which goes through a Turing bifurcation whenever
c=a^{2}\,\delta^{2}+2\,a\,\delta+2
.

The authors of
[
23
]
proved this model has codimension-two points at
a_{\pm}=\dfrac{1}{16\,\delta}\,\left(21\pm\sqrt{313}\right)
. In particular, if we fix
\delta=\dfrac{21+\sqrt{313}}{48}
, then the variables of the expansion for the codimension-2 point at
a=a_{+}
can be obtained
with the code in
[
24
]
,
and are shown in Table
4
. Now, note that when making the change

\displaystyle(u,v)

\displaystyle\to\left(u+a,v+\frac{c-1}{a}\right),

we obtain the system given by

\displaystyle\partial_{t}u

\displaystyle=a^{2}v-\frac{u^{2}}{a}+2\,a\,u\,v+u^{2}\,v-2\,u+c\,\left(u+\frac{u^{2}}{a}\right)+\delta^{2}\,\partial_{xx}u,

(97)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}v

\displaystyle=-a^{2}\,v+\frac{u^{2}}{a}-2\,a\,u\,v-c\,u-u^{2}\,v+u-c\,\left(u+\frac{u^{2}}{a}\right)+\partial_{xx}v.

Variable

Value

a_{2}

1

c_{2}

\dfrac{545+29\,\sqrt{313}}{192}

c_{4}

\dfrac{27181639265}{160777328256}+\dfrac{1139313695\,\sqrt{313}}{53592442752}

\dfrac{255568350955114905604104012761}{70461515885712580123818315840}-\dfrac{74364627222521911575006255847\,\sqrt{313}}{352307579428562900619091579200}

).

[

]
, with the variable
modelname=‘Brusselator’
.

Here, we note that a change in
c
away from the Maxwell point, which will be called
\Delta c
to avoid confusion with
\delta
, will affect two terms, a linear and a quadratic term:
u+u^{2}/a
. Nevertheless, we only need to care about the dominant term, which is the linear one, which implies that the equation for the amplitude of the remainder becomes

\displaystyle\alpha_{1}\,B_{1}^{\prime\prime}+i\,\alpha_{3}\,\absolutevalue{A_1}^{2}\,B_{1}^{\prime}+i\,\alpha_{3}\,A_{1}\,A_{1}^{\prime}\,\bar{B}_{1}+i\,\alpha_{3}\,\bar{A}_{1}\,A_{1}^{\prime}\,B_{1}+\alpha_{5}\,B_{1}+\alpha_{6}\,A_{1}^{2}\,\bar{B}_{1}+2\,\alpha_{6}\,\absolutevalue{A_1}^{2}\,B_{1}

\displaystyle+2\,\alpha_{7}\,\absolutevalue{A_1}^{2}\,A_{1}^{2}\,\bar{B}_{1}+3\,\alpha_{7}\,\absolutevalue{A_1}^{4}\,B_{1}+\frac{1}{66}\,\left(37-\sqrt{313}\right)\,\frac{\Delta c}{\varepsilon^{4}}\,A_{1}=0.

Now, in order to determine the width of the snaking, let us recall that we need to run the recursion given by (
35
) with the initial conditions given by

\displaystyle A_{1}

\displaystyle\approx 0.109085\,i,

\displaystyle A_{3}

\displaystyle\approx{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}-0.180032-0.130054\,i}.

Figure
10
shows the results of the convergence of the argument of the limit in (
43
) for
r=2
, for different values of
n/2
. In particular, when fitting a curve as for the Swift-Hohenberg equation, we note that

\displaystyle c_{2}^{[0]}\approx{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}0.000763389}\,e^{-{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}0.547143}\,i}.

(a)

(b)

Figure 10
:
Similar to Figure
4
, but for model (

).
Both of these graphs were obtained with the Mathematica code within the folders
Iteration/Brusselator
in
[
24
]
.

Furthermore, we have

\displaystyle B_{1}

\displaystyle\sim\frac{1}{8\,\beta_{1}}\,\sqrt{-\frac{\beta_{1}}{\beta_{3}}}\,\left(-\frac{\sqrt{2}\,\beta_{3}\,L_{2}^{+}}{\beta_{1}}-\frac{\sqrt{2}\,\beta_{3}\,L_{2}^{-}}{\beta_{1}}+\frac{\sqrt{313}-37}{33\,\sqrt{2}\,\alpha_{1}\,\varepsilon^{4}}\,\Delta c\right)\,(1+2\,\eta\,i)\,e^{2\,\sqrt{\beta_{1}}\,X}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{8\,\beta_{1}}\,\sqrt{-\frac{\beta_{1}}{\beta_{3}}}\,\left(-\frac{4\,\sqrt{2}\,\beta_{3}}{\beta_{1}}\,\frac{\pi\,\absolutevalue{K_2}\,e^{-\frac{\pi}{2}\,\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{\beta_{1}}\,\varepsilon^{2}}+\eta\right)}}{\varepsilon^{6}}\,\cos\left(K_{2}^{o}-\hat{\chi}+2\,\eta\,\log(\varepsilon)\right)\right.

\displaystyle\quad\left.+\frac{\sqrt{313}-37}{33\,\sqrt{2}\,\alpha_{1}\,\varepsilon^{4}}\,\Delta c\right)\,(1+2\,\eta\,i)\,e^{2\,\sqrt{\beta_{1}}\,X},

as
X\to\infty
.

Thus, to ensure that the remainder tends to zero as
X\to\infty
, we need

\displaystyle\absolutevalue{\Delta c}\leq\frac{264\,\alpha_{1}\,\beta_{3}}{\beta_{1}\,\left(\sqrt{313}-37\right)}\,\frac{\pi\,\absolutevalue{K_2}\,e^{-\frac{\pi}{2}\,\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{\beta_{1}}\,\varepsilon^{2}}+\eta\right)}}{\varepsilon^{2}},

where
\absolutevalue{K_2}\approx{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}0.0255587}
(see (
61
)). Therefore,

\displaystyle\absolutevalue{\Delta c}\leq\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}18.869315}\,e^{-4.808385\,\frac{\pi}{\varepsilon^{2}}}}{\varepsilon^{2}}.

(98)

The result of the matching of this function with respect to numerical computation of the snaking for model (
96
) is shown by the black line in Figure
11(a)
, which shows that although the prediction we have obtained seems to follow the same behaviour as the actual width of the snaking, the coefficient in front of (
98
) needs to be higher. In fact, in this case, we have that

\displaystyle\absolutevalue{\frac{h_1}{k_1}}\approx 0.006504\ll 1,

which implies that the mode
r=2
is much less dominant than
r=0
(see (
42
)). Now, observe the convergence of
c_{0}^{[0]}
, shown in Figure
12
. In this case, we note that we have two limits to which the curve seems to converge. This happens because the mode
r=0
is present for every dominant value of
\kappa^{2}=\kappa_{\pm}^{2}
. That is, there is an interaction between
all of
these modes.

To summarize, if we take the average between the two limits of convergence, which turn out to be
0.0760281
and
0.200181
, and divide the result by
8
, considering that there are four different values of
\kappa
(that is, four different solutions, see Section
3
)
and the mode
r=0
needs to be split into the real and imaginary parts for each of these modes (see Remark
2
)
, we obtain

\displaystyle\absolutevalue{c_0^{[0]}}\approx{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}0.017263}.

Now, if we use this value for computing
K_{2}
, instead of
c_{2}^{[0]}
, taking into account that the matching does not change when considering that
h_{0}^{[0]}
is defined as the complex-conjugate of
h_{2}^{[0]}
when the integrals that defined them are set to be real (see (
61
)), we obtain

\displaystyle\absolutevalue{\Delta c}\leq\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}426.704943}\,e^{-4.808385\,\frac{\pi}{\varepsilon^{2}}}}{\varepsilon^{2}},

(99)

which leads to the red line in Figure
11(a)
, showing a remarkably good match, even for high values of
\varepsilon
.

7.5
4-component Brusselator

Finally, we will show how our code can be used to carry out the calculations for higher-component systems. In particular, once again, motivated by the Brusselator, we now work with two Brusselator models coupled linearly, which form one 4-component reaction-diffusion system studied in
[
8
]
. That system is given by

\displaystyle\partial_{t}u

\displaystyle=a-(b+1)\,u+u^{2}\,v+\alpha\,(w-u)+\delta^{2}\,\partial_{xx}u,

(100)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}v

\displaystyle=b\,u-u^{2}\,v+\beta\,(z-v)+\partial_{xx}v,

\displaystyle\partial_{t}w

\displaystyle=a-(b+1)\,w+w^{2}\,z+\alpha\,(u-w)+\delta^{2}\,\partial_{xx}w,

\displaystyle\partial_{t}z

\displaystyle=b\,w-w^{2}\,z+\beta\,(v-z)+\partial_{xx}z,

and it is a model that has a homogeneous steady state given by

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}=\left(a,\frac{b}{a},a,\frac{b}{a}\right).

Now, if we fix

\displaystyle\left(\alpha,\beta,\delta\right)=\left(1,\frac{1}{2},\frac{21+\sqrt{313}}{48}\right),

then
\mathbf{P}
goes through a codimension-two Turing bifurcation at

\displaystyle(a,b)=\left(3,\frac{1}{128}\left(841+37\,\sqrt{313}\right)\right).

The variables of the expansion at this codimension-two point are shown in Table
5
.

).

[
24

, with the variable
modelname=‘Bru 4’
.

Now, if we make the translation
(u,v,w,z)\to(u,v,w,z)+\mathbf{P}
, we obtain the system

\displaystyle\partial_{t}u

\displaystyle=b\,\left(u+\frac{u^{2}}{2\,a}\right)+v\,(2\,a+u)^{2}-2\,u+w+\delta^{2}\,\partial_{xx}u,

\displaystyle\partial_{t}v

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\,\left(-b\,\left(\frac{u\,(2\,a+u)}{a}\right)-v\,\left(2\,(2\,a+u)^{2}+1\right)+z\right)+\partial_{xx}v,

\displaystyle\partial_{t}w

\displaystyle=b\,\left(w+\frac{w^{2}}{2\,a}\right)+z\,(2\,a+w)^{2}-2\,w+u+\delta^{2}\,\partial_{xx}w,

\displaystyle\partial_{t}z

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\,\left(-b\,\left(\frac{w\,(2\,a+w)}{a}\right)-z\,\left(2\,(2\,a+w)^{2}+1\right)+v\right)+\partial_{xx}z.

Now, as in the previous examples, recall that we need to run the iteration (
35
) to be able to estimate the width of the snaking in this case, with initial conditions given by

\displaystyle A_{1}

\displaystyle\approx 0.109084591223343\,i,

\displaystyle A_{3}

\displaystyle\approx{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}-0.180031849961344-0.130054306337421\,i}.

The result of that iteration is shown in Figure
13
. We highlight here that, as in the previous example, the most dominant mode turns out to be
r=0
, reason why we show the convergence of
c_{0}^{[0]}
instead of
c_{2}^{[0]}
.
The corresponding limits of the recurrence in this case
turn out to be approximately
0.115257
and
0.247105
. Therefore, following the same idea as in the previous example, if we divide the average of these two limits by
8
, we obtain that

\displaystyle\absolutevalue{c_0^{[0]}}\approx{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}0.0226476},

which implies
\absolutevalue{K_2}\approx{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}0.758257}

(see (
61
))
.

To summarize the process, by following the same ideas as in the previous example, we have to solve the following equation for the amplitude of the remainder at order five, (
66
):

\displaystyle\alpha_{1}\,B_{1}^{\prime\prime}+i\,\alpha_{3}\,\absolutevalue{A_1}^{2}\,B_{1}^{\prime}+i\,\alpha_{3}\,A_{1}\,A_{1}^{\prime}\,\bar{B}_{1}+i\,\alpha_{3}\,\bar{A}_{1}\,A_{1}^{\prime}\,B_{1}+\alpha_{5}\,B_{1}+\alpha_{6}\,A_{1}^{2}\,\bar{B}_{1}+2\,\alpha_{6}\,\absolutevalue{A_1}^{2}\,B_{1}

\displaystyle+2\,\alpha_{7}\,\absolutevalue{A_1}^{2}\,A_{1}^{2}\,\bar{B}_{1}+3\,\alpha_{7}\,\absolutevalue{A_1}^{4}\,B_{1}+\frac{1}{33}\,\left(37-\sqrt{313}\right)\,\frac{\Delta b}{\varepsilon^{4}}\,A_{1}=0.

With this, we have

\displaystyle B_{1}

\displaystyle\sim\frac{1}{4\,\beta_{1}}\,\sqrt{-\frac{\beta_{1}}{\beta_{3}}}\,\left(-\frac{\sqrt{2}\,\beta_{3}\,L_{2}^{+}}{2\,\beta_{1}}-\frac{\sqrt{2}\,\beta_{3}\,L_{2}^{-}}{2\,\beta_{1}}+\frac{\sqrt{313}-37}{33\,\sqrt{2}\,\alpha_{1}\,\varepsilon^{4}}\,\Delta b\right)\,(1+2\,\eta\,i)\,e^{2\,\sqrt{\beta_{1}}\,X}

\displaystyle=\begin{multlined}\frac{1}{4\,\beta_{1}}\,\sqrt{-\frac{\beta_{1}}{\beta_{3}}}\,\left(-\frac{2\,\sqrt{2}\,\beta_{3}}{\beta_{1}}\,\frac{\pi\,\absolutevalue{K_2}\,e^{-\frac{\pi}{2}\,\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{\beta_{1}}\,\varepsilon^{2}}+\eta\right)}}{\varepsilon^{6}}\,\cos\left(K_{2}^{o}-\hat{\chi}+2\,\eta\,\log(\varepsilon)\right)\right.\\
\quad\left.+\frac{\sqrt{313}-37}{33\,\sqrt{2}\,\alpha_{1}\,\varepsilon^{4}}\,\Delta b\right)\,(1+2\,\eta\,i)\,e^{2\,\sqrt{\beta_{1}}\,X},\end{multlined}

as
X\to\infty
. Therefore, as in the previous examples, we conclude that

\displaystyle\absolutevalue{\Delta b}\leq\frac{132\,\alpha_{1}\,\beta_{3}}{\beta_{1}\,\left(\sqrt{313}-37\right)}\,\frac{\pi\,\absolutevalue{K_2}\,e^{-\frac{\pi}{2}\,\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{\beta_{1}}\,\varepsilon^{2}}+\eta\right)}}{\varepsilon^{2}},

which can be written, approximately, as

\displaystyle\absolutevalue{\Delta b}\leq\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}559.799849}\,e^{-4.808385\,\frac{\pi}{\varepsilon^{2}}}}{\varepsilon^{2}},

(101)

leading to the red line in Figure (
14(a)
), which shows, once again, a remarkably good match, letting us conclude that the theory we have developed produces a great result when the expansions and constants are computed accurately.

Remark 7
.

We close this section by highlighting that it is hard to work with small values of
\varepsilon
in numerical computations. We omit the details, but using suitable tolerances in AUTO, we had to take extremely long domains to find reliable estimates for the width of the homoclinic snaking when it becomes smaller than
\mathcal{O}(10^{-7})
. Given the exponentially small asymptotic estimates of the theory, in practice, this means that the smallest values of
\varepsilon
for which we can demonstrate the agreement of the numerics with the theory is only about 0.4. Nevertheless, we note that the homoclinic snaking occurs, generically, at a distance of order
\varepsilon^{2}
or
\varepsilon^{4}
from the codimension-two Turing bifurcation line, which allowed us to demonstrate good agreement between our analysis and numerics by computing how the width of the snake scales with
\varepsilon
.

8
Discussion

In this paper, we have achieved what we set out to do. We have successfully generalized the theory of exponential asymptotics used to study homoclinic snaking close to a codimension-two Turing bifurcation in the Swift-Hohenberg equation
[
10
,
13
]
to arbitrary systems of reaction-diffusion equations undergoing the same instability. Accompanying codes to do all the calculations automatically in Python and Mathematica for any reaction-diffusion systems have also been provided
[
24
]
, and the codes were used to do the calculations for each of the examples shown in this paper. Further study will include the use of these codes to study homoclinic snaking in models where it has been found, but not much has been said about the region in the parameter space where such a phenomenon can be found. Although this calculation is extensive, the purpose of this article is to provide tools for everyone to be able to understand it easily and use it as needed.

We highlight that some details in this paper have not been treated, as it is already quite lengthy. For example, we have not explained how to use information on the phase of the exponentially small estimate in order to study the fine details of the homoclinic snaking. Furthermore, with additional symmetries or conserved quantities, there can be additional branches. Moreover, it is well known that examples such as the Swift-Hohenberg equation, which have variational structures, give rise to ‘ladder’ asymmetric stationary localised patterns connecting the two interleaving snaking branches.

Also, we have not discussed the temporal stability of patterns. Under certain conditions on the matrix
\mathbb{M}
and forms of
\mathbf{f}
, much can be said at the general level, without needing to resort to model-specific calculations. Details are left for future work.

A more challenging open question is to consider analogous structures that arise near Turing bifurcations corresponding to the dispersion curve having a non-zero imaginary part. Similar codimension-two bifurcation points occur for such finite wavenumber Hopf bifurcations (also known as wave bifurcations), but are more complex due to the presence of both standing and travelling waves. Nevertheless, in earlier work
[
26
]
, the present author has computed the regular asymptotic expansion to produce the amplitude equations for wave bifurcations up to order five. The same approach as that presented here is expected to be generalisable, but a general calculation is expected to be yet more cumbersome.

Another generalisation under consideration is to study so-called slanted snaking, in the presence of a zero wavenumber mode in addition to the Turing instability
[
17
]
. Particular classes of systems in higher spatial dimensions would also be interesting to study, although complete generalisations there seem a long way off.

Furthermore, as the theory developed here is similar to the WKB theory since they are both based on asymptotics
[
3
]
, and the latter has been used to study Turing bifurcations and localized patterns in heterogeneous reaction-diffusion equations (see e.g.
[
20
]
), a generalization of this approach to study localized solutions in heterogeneous systems is also under consideration.

One last thing to highlight is that the process carried out in this article is a formal derivation of the region in the parameter space where homoclinic snaking can be found close to codimension-two Turing bifurcation points. It would be nice to develop a more rigorous approach to deal with this kind of problem, as often such approaches follow from more formal asymptotic procedures, like the ones used here.

In this section, we continue the asymptotic expansion of system (

) up until order 5 to obtain the amplitude equation at that order.

\displaystyle\mathbf{0}

\displaystyle=J_{\mathbf{u}}\,\mathbf{f}_{0,0}(\mathbf{0})\,\mathbf{u}^{[4]}+a_{1}\,J_{\mathbf{u}}\,\mathbf{f}_{1,0}(\mathbf{0})\,\mathbf{u}^{[3]}+b_{1}\,J_{\mathbf{u}}\,\mathbf{f}_{0,1}(\mathbf{0})\,\mathbf{u}^{[3]}+a_{2}\,J_{\mathbf{u}}\,\mathbf{f}_{1,0}(\mathbf{0})\,\mathbf{u}^{[2]}+b_{2}\,J_{\mathbf{u}}\,\mathbf{f}_{0,1}(\mathbf{0})\,\mathbf{u}^{[2]}

\displaystyle\left\langle a_{1}\,J_{\mathbf{u}}\,\mathbf{f}_{1,0}(\mathbf{0})\,\mathbf{W}_{1}^{[3]}+b_{1}\,J_{\mathbf{u}}\,\mathbf{f}_{0,1}(\mathbf{0})\,\mathbf{W}_{1}^{[3]}+a_{2}\,J_{\mathbf{u}}\,\mathbf{f}_{1,0}(\mathbf{0})\,\mathbf{W}_{1}^{[2]}+b_{2}\,J_{\mathbf{u}}\,\mathbf{f}_{0,1}(\mathbf{0})\,\mathbf{W}_{1}^{[2]}\right.

(106)

Remark 8
.

Note equations (
13
), (
105
) and (
106
) can be written as a linear system of equations,

\displaystyle\upsilon_{1}\,a_{1}+\upsilon_{2}\,b_{1}

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\upsilon_{3}\,a_{1}+\upsilon_{4}\,b_{1}

\displaystyle=0,

(107)

\displaystyle\upsilon_{5}\,a_{1}+\upsilon_{6}\,b_{1}

\displaystyle=0,

where
\upsilon_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
for all
i=1,\ldots,6
. This implies that, if the determinant of any
2\times 2
submatrix of the matrix of coefficients of this system is different from zero, then
a_{1}=b_{1}=0
. That is, generically, these variables need to be zero. Nevertheless, we carry on with a general asymptotic analysis, as there might be systems in which one can carry out the analysis with these variables being different from zero.

Order
\mathcal{O}\left(\varepsilon^{5}\right)
.

At this order, (
5
) becomes

\displaystyle\mathbf{0}

\displaystyle=J_{\mathbf{u}}\,\mathbf{f}_{0,0}(\mathbf{0})\,\mathbf{u}^{[5]}+a_{1}\,J_{\mathbf{u}}\,\mathbf{f}_{1,0}(\mathbf{0})\,\mathbf{u}^{[4]}+b_{1}\,J_{\mathbf{u}}\,\mathbf{f}_{0,1}(\mathbf{0})\,\mathbf{u}^{[4]}+a_{2}\,J_{\mathbf{u}}\,\mathbf{f}_{1,0}(\mathbf{0})\,\mathbf{u}^{[3]}+b_{2}\,J_{\mathbf{u}}\,\mathbf{f}_{0,1}(\mathbf{0})\,\mathbf{u}^{[3]}

\displaystyle\quad+a_{3}\,J_{\mathbf{u}}\,\mathbf{f}_{1,0}(\mathbf{0})\,\mathbf{u}^{[2]}+b_{3}\,J_{\mathbf{u}}\,\mathbf{f}_{0,1}(\mathbf{0})\,\mathbf{u}^{[2]}+a_{4}\,J_{\mathbf{u}}\,\mathbf{f}_{1,0}(\mathbf{0})\,\mathbf{u}^{[1]}+b_{4}\,J_{\mathbf{u}}\,\mathbf{f}_{0,1}(\mathbf{0})\,\mathbf{u}^{[1]}

\displaystyle\quad+a_{1}^{2}\,J_{\mathbf{u}}\,\mathbf{f}_{2,0}(\mathbf{0})\,\mathbf{u}^{[3]}+a_{1}\,b_{1}\,J_{\mathbf{u}}\,\mathbf{f}_{1,1}(\mathbf{0})\,\mathbf{u}^{[3]}+b_{1}^{2}\,J_{\mathbf{u}}\,\mathbf{f}_{0,2}(\mathbf{0})\,\mathbf{u}^{[3]}+2\,a_{1}\,a_{2}\,J_{\mathbf{u}}\,\mathbf{f}_{2,0}(\mathbf{0})\,\mathbf{u}^{[2]}

At this point, we are not interested in the full solution to this equation (find the complete development up to order seven in

\displaystyle\quad+3\,a_{1}\,b_{1}\,\mathbf{F}_{3,1,1}\left(\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]},\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]},\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]}\right)+3\,b_{1}^{2}\,\mathbf{F}_{3,0,2}\left(\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]},\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]},\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]}\right),

\displaystyle\mathbf{Q}_{7}^{[5]}

\displaystyle=2\,\mathbf{F}_{2,0,0}\left(\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]},2\,\mathbf{W}_{0,2}^{[4]}+\mathbf{W}_{2,2}^{[4]}\right)+2\,\mathbf{F}_{2,0,0}\left(\mathbf{W}_{2}^{[2]},\mathbf{W}_{3}^{[3]}\right)+2\,\mathbf{F}_{2,0,0}\left(\mathbf{W}_{1,2}^{[3]},2\,\mathbf{W}_{0}^{[2]}+\mathbf{W}_{2}^{[2]}\right)

\displaystyle\quad+3\,\mathbf{F}_{3,0,0}\left(\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]},\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]},3\,\mathbf{W}_{1,2}^{[3]}+\mathbf{W}_{3}^{[3]}\right)+12\,\mathbf{F}_{3,0,0}\left(\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]},\mathbf{W}_{0}^{[2]},\mathbf{W}_{0}^{[2]}\right)

\displaystyle\quad+6\,\mathbf{F}_{3,0,0}\left(\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]},\mathbf{W}_{2}^{[2]},2\,\mathbf{W}_{0}^{[2]}+\mathbf{W}_{2}^{[2]}\right)+8\,\mathbf{F}_{4,0,0}\left(\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]},\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]},\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]},3\,\mathbf{W}_{0}^{[2]}+2\,\mathbf{W}_{2}^{[2]}\right)

\displaystyle\quad+10\,\mathbf{F}_{5,0,0}\left(\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]},\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]},\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]},\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]},\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]}\right).

Again, we need to ensure that (
108
) has a solution and, as before, we can apply the inner product with
\bm{\psi}
. Finally, we arrive at the amplitude equation (
7
), where
\alpha_{i}=\left\langle\mathbf{Q}_{i}^{[5]},\,\bm{\psi}\right\rangle
for
i=1,\ldots,7
, and we require
\alpha_{1}\neq 0
.

Appendix B
The case in which
c_{0}^{[0]}=c_{2}^{[0]}=0

Generically, we have that the constants
c_{0}^{[0]}
or
c_{2}^{[0]}
let us estimate the width of the homoclinic snaking successfully, close to codimension-two Turing bifurcation points (see Section
6
). Nevertheless, there are cases in which both of these constants equal zero, which does not let us give a proper estimation for this width (see e.g.
[
13
]
). This happens, generically, when the system (
1
) has the symmetry
\mathbf{u}\to-\mathbf{u}
, a case in which the even terms of the expansion vanish. To overcome this issue, the same general ansatzes we used in Section
3
are still valid, but some key considerations need to be made. In particular,

1.

The dominant values of
\kappa^{2}
become
\pm i/2
, and the dominant modes for which these values are attained are
r=\pm 1,\pm 3
(see sub-Section
3.2
).

2.

The ansatz for the values of
\gamma_{r}
, (
41
) becomes

\displaystyle\gamma_{0}=\gamma_{\pm 2}=\gamma-\frac{1}{2},\qquad\gamma_{\pm 1}=\gamma,\qquad\gamma_{\pm r}=\gamma-\frac{r-3}{2},\quad\text{for }r\geq 3.

3.

After making the same expansion as in (
37
) to obtain the inner solution with these new values of
\gamma_{r}
(see Section
3
for the ideas that need to be followed to study this case), we conclude that

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}\in\{-1-2\,\eta\,i,-2\,\eta\,i,2-2\,\eta\,i,3-2\,\eta\,i\},

from where we take, once again, the one with the highest real part,
\gamma=3-2\,\eta\,i
.

4.

As the dominant multiples of the critical wavenumber,
k
, are now odd, we need to assume that
n
is odd. Furthermore, we need to consider the changes of the modes in the forcing due to truncation of the equation for the remainder (see Section
6
). In particular, the forcing due to truncation provided by
\kappa^{2}=\kappa_{+}^{2}/2
is now given by

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{N+1}\,\kappa^{N}\,e^{2ix}\,\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{N}{2}+\gamma\right)}{\left(X_{0}-X\right)^{\frac{N}{2}+\gamma}}\sum_{r=1,3}e^{i(r-2)\hat{x}}\,\mathbf{C}_{r},

where

\displaystyle\mathbf{C}_{r}

\displaystyle=2\,h_{r}^{[0]}\,\left(-2\,\left(2-r\right)+2\,i\,\varepsilon\,\hat{\theta}\right)\,k^{2}\,\hat{D}\,\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]},

which implies

\displaystyle\mathbf{C}_{1}=h_{1}^{[0]}\,\left(-4+4\,i\,\varepsilon\,\hat{\theta}\right)\,k^{2}\,\hat{D}\,\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]},\qquad\mathbf{C}_{3}=h_{3}^{[0]}\,\left(4+4\,i\,\varepsilon\,\hat{\theta}\right)\,k^{2}\,\hat{D}\,\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]},

and
h_{1}^{[0]}
,
h_{3}^{[0]}
are the analogs of
h_{0}^{[0]}
,
h_{2}^{[0]}
, respectively, for this case. We highlight that the equations that determine
h_{1}^{[0]}
,
h_{3}^{[0]}
coincide with the ones obtained for
h_{0}^{[0]}
,
h_{2}^{[0]}
in Section
4
.

5.

Following the same steps as in Section
6
, we note that

\displaystyle e^{2i\hat{x}}=e^{2i\left(X-X_{0}+X_{0}\right)/\varepsilon^{2}-2i\hat{\chi}}=e^{2i\left(X-X_{0}\right)/\varepsilon^{2}}\,e^{2iX_{0}/\varepsilon^{2}}\,e^{-2i\hat{\chi}},

which yields

\displaystyle e^{2i\left(X-X_{0}\right)/\varepsilon^{2}}\,\varepsilon^{N+2\,\gamma}\,\kappa^{N+2\,\gamma}\,\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{N}{2}+\gamma\right)}{\left(X_{0}-X\right)^{\frac{N}{2}+\gamma}}\sim\sqrt{2\,\pi}\,\varepsilon\,\absolutevalue{\kappa}\,e^{-\rho\,\hat{\theta}^{2}}.

Therefore,

\mathbf{R}_{N,2}^{[2]}=2\,\left(A_{1}\,C_{-1}+\bar{A}_{1}\,C_{1}\right)\,\mathbf{W}_{0}^{[2]}+\left(C_{1}\,e^{ix}+C_{-1}\,e^{-ix}\right)\,\mathbf{W}_{1}^{[2]}\\
-\frac{1}{\rho}\,\left(i\,C_{1}^{\prime}\,e^{ix}-i\,C_{-1}^{\prime}\,e^{-ix}\right)\mathbf{W}_{1,3}^{[3]}\\
-2\,\kappa^{-2\,\gamma}\,e^{-i\hat{\chi}}\,\frac{\sqrt{2\,\pi}\,\absolutevalue{\kappa}}{\rho^{\frac{1}{2}}}\,e^{-\rho\,\hat{\theta}^{2}}\,h_{0}^{[0]}\,e^{-ix}\,\mathbf{W}_{1,3}^{[3]}+2\,\kappa^{-2\,\gamma}\,e^{-i\hat{\chi}}\,\frac{\sqrt{2\,\pi}\,\absolutevalue{\kappa}}{\rho^{\frac{1}{2}}}\,e^{-\rho\,\hat{\theta}^{2}}\,h_{2}^{[0]}\,e^{ix}\,\mathbf{W}_{1,3}^{[3]}\\
+2\,\left(A_{1}\,C_{1}\,e^{2ix}+\bar{A}_{1}\,C_{-1}\,e^{-2ix}\right)\,\mathbf{W}_{2}^{[2]},

which implies that the analog of the solvability conditions at order
\mathcal{O}\left(\varepsilon^{3}\right)
, (
78
) and (
79
), yield

\displaystyle C_{-1}^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle=2\,\sqrt{2\,\pi}\,i\,\hat{\theta}\,h_{0}^{[0]}\,\rho^{3/2}\,\absolutevalue{\kappa}\,\kappa^{-2\,\gamma}\,e^{-\hat{\theta}^{2}\,\rho-i\hat{\chi}}

\displaystyle C_{1}^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle=2\,\sqrt{2\,\pi}\,i\,\hat{\theta}\,h_{2}^{[0]}\,\rho^{3/2}\,\absolutevalue{\kappa}\,\kappa^{-2\,\gamma}\,e^{-\hat{\theta}^{2}\,\rho-i\,\hat{\chi}},

which yield

\displaystyle C_{-1}

\displaystyle=-i\,\pi\,\kappa^{-2\,\gamma}\,h_{0}^{[0]}\,e^{-i\hat{\chi}},

\displaystyle C_{1}

\displaystyle=-i\,\pi\,\kappa^{-2\,\gamma}\,h_{0}^{[0]}\,e^{-i\hat{\chi}}.

Therefore, in this case, as we cross the Stokes’ line, the following function gets triggered by
\kappa^{2}=\kappa_{+}^{2}/2
:

\displaystyle-i\,\pi\,\varepsilon^{-2\,\gamma}\,\kappa^{-2\,\gamma}\,e^{2iX_{0}/\varepsilon^{2}}\,e^{-2i\hat{\chi}}\,\left(h_{2}^{[0]}\,e^{ix}+h_{0}^{[0]}\,e^{-ix}\right)\,\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]},

which implies

\displaystyle L_{2}=L_{2}^{+}

\displaystyle=-i\,\pi\,\varepsilon^{-2\,\gamma}\,\kappa^{-2\,\gamma}\,e^{2iX_{0}/\varepsilon^{2}}\,e^{-2i\hat{\chi}}\,K_{2},

(109)

with the corresponding development also for
\kappa_{-}^{2}/2
.

In summary, the only change one needs to make in this case to apply the same theory in order to estimate the width of the snaking is to scale (
84
) by a factor of
\dfrac{1}{2}
, take the factor
\absolutevalue{\kappa}
out of it, and multiply the power of the exponential terms by 2.

Appendix C
Joining fronts

Let us recall that we have analysed two solutions at the same time (see sub-Section
2.3
). They correspond to fronts that join two steady states in both directions, up and down (see (
25
) and (
26
)). Now, we need to join those solutions to construct a homoclinic orbit, which turns out to be a localized solution of system (
4
) (see Figure
3
), and obtain an approximation of the
homoclinic snaking
bifurcation curve.

Note that the solution we obtained for the remainder in Sections
5
and
6
is exponentially growing on
X
and becomes order 1 when
X\sim\mathcal{O}\left(1/\varepsilon^{2}\right)
. Therefore, if we let
2\,L
be the width of the support of this solution on
x
in (
4
), then the localized solution can be constructed by joining an up-front in the range
0\leq X\leq L/\varepsilon^{2}
with a down-front in the range
L/\varepsilon^{2}\leq X\leq 2\,L/\varepsilon^{2}
. With this, we now proceed to perform the matching.

The up-front solution is given by

\displaystyle\mathbf{u}=\sum_{r=1}^{N}\varepsilon^{r}\,\mathbf{u}^{[r]}+\mathbf{R}_{N},

(110)

where

\displaystyle\mathbf{u}^{[1]}

\displaystyle=A_{1}\,e^{i\left(x-\hat{\chi}\right)}\,\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]}+c.c.,

\displaystyle\mathbf{u}^{[3]}

\displaystyle=A_{3}\,e^{i\left(x-\hat{\chi}\right)}\,\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]}+c.c.,

and a first-order approximation of the remainder has also been determined. Still, it depends on the function
\delta\left(R_{1}\right)
, which is related to the separation of one parameter from the Maxwell point (see equation (
66
)), which was studied in the examples developed in Section
7
.

Now, for
1\ll X\ll 1/\varepsilon^{2}
, we have

\displaystyle A_{1}\sim\left(\Delta_{1,1}+\Delta_{1,2}\,e^{-2\,\sqrt{\beta_{1}}\,X}\right)\,e^{\Phi\,i},

(111)

where

\displaystyle\Delta_{1,1}=\sqrt{-\frac{2\,\beta_{1}}{\beta_{3}}},\qquad\Delta_{1,2}=\sqrt{-\frac{2\,\beta_{1}}{\beta_{3}}}\,\frac{-1+2\,\eta\,i}{2},

\displaystyle\Phi=2\,(\eta-\xi)\,\sqrt{\beta_{1}}\,X+\zeta-\eta\,\log\left(2\,\sqrt{\beta_{1}}\right),

and
\zeta=\zeta(\Xi)
is a linear function on
\Xi=\varepsilon^{2}\,X
, obtained in
D
.

Furthermore, from
D
, we have that
A_{3}
has an asymptotic behaviour, as
X\to\infty
, given by

\displaystyle A_{3}

\displaystyle\sim\left(\Delta_{3,1}+\Delta_{3,2}\,X\,e^{-2\,\sqrt{\beta_{1}}\,X}\right)\,e^{\Phi\,i},

where

\displaystyle\Delta_{3,1}

\displaystyle=-\frac{2^{-5/2}}{\alpha_{1}^{2}\,\sqrt{\beta_{1}}\,\left(-\beta_{3}\right)^{7/2}}\,\left(\alpha_{2}\,\beta_{3}^{3}\,\left(\alpha_{1,4}+2\,\alpha_{1}\,\zeta_{\Xi}\right)+\alpha_{2}^{2}\,\beta_{3}^{3}\right.

\displaystyle\quad\left.+2\,\alpha_{1}\,\left(\beta_{3}^{3}\,\alpha_{8,4}-8\,\beta_{1}^{3}\,\beta_{7,3}+4\,\beta_{1}^{2}\,\beta_{3}\,\beta_{5,3}-2\,\beta_{1}\,\beta_{3}^{2}\,\beta_{3,3}+4\,i\,\alpha_{1}\,\beta_{1}\,\beta_{3}^{3}\,\omega_{4}\right)\right),

\displaystyle\Delta_{3,2}

\displaystyle=\frac{2^{-3/2}}{\alpha_{1}^{2}\,\left(-\beta_{3}\right)^{7/2}}\,\left(1-2\,\eta\,i\right)\,\left(\alpha_{2}\,\beta_{3}^{3}\,\left(\alpha_{2}+\alpha_{1,4}+2\,\alpha_{1}\,\zeta_{\Xi}\right)\right.

\displaystyle\quad+\alpha_{1}\,\left(-\beta_{1}^{2}\,\beta_{3}^{2}\,\left(\alpha_{6,4}+\alpha_{7,4}\right)+2\,\beta_{3}^{3}\,\alpha_{8,4}-4\,\beta_{1}^{3}\,\beta_{7,3}\right).

With this, we note that the up-front solution is, asymptotically, given by

\displaystyle\mathbf{u}

\displaystyle\sim\varepsilon\,\mathbf{u}^{[1]}+\varepsilon^{3}\,\mathbf{u}^{[3]}

\displaystyle=\varepsilon\,\left(\left(\Delta_{1,1}+\Delta_{1,2}\,e^{-2\,\sqrt{\beta_{1}}\,X}\right)+\varepsilon^{2}\,\left(\Delta_{3,1}+\Delta_{3,2}\,X\,e^{-2\,\sqrt{\beta_{1}}\,X}\right)\right)\,e^{\Phi\,i}\,e^{i\left(x-\hat{\chi}\right)}\,\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]}+c.c.,

(112)

and, by the symmetric properties of the amplitude equation (
7
) (see sub-Section,
2.3
), we have that the down-front is given by (
112
) when we change
X\to L/\varepsilon^{2}-X
, and
x\to L/\varepsilon^{4}-X
. However, the corresponding two solutions may have different phase shifts. Therefore, for
1\ll L/\varepsilon^{2}-X\ll 1/\varepsilon^{2}
, an asymptotic approximation of the down-front is given by

\displaystyle\mathbf{u}

\displaystyle\sim\varepsilon\,\left(\left(\Delta_{1,1}+\Delta_{1,2}\,e^{-2\,\sqrt{\beta_{1}}\,\left(L/\varepsilon^{2}-X\right)}\right)+\varepsilon^{2}\,\left(\Delta_{3,1}+\Delta_{3,2}\,\left(L/\varepsilon^{2}-X\right)\,e^{-2\,\sqrt{\beta_{1}}\,\left(L/\varepsilon^{2}-X\right)}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\quad\times\left(2\,\sqrt{\beta_{1}}\right)^{-\eta\,i}\,e^{2\,i\,(\eta-\xi)\,\sqrt{\beta_{1}}\,\left(L/\varepsilon^{2}-X\right)}\,e^{i\,\zeta}\,e^{i\left(L/\varepsilon^{4}-x-\check{\chi}\right)}\,\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]}+c.c.,

(113)

where
\check{\chi}\in\mathbb{R}
is the phase shift for the down-front.

Now, we highlight that, for our expansion to be valid, we need to ensure that these match for
X=\mathcal{O}\left(\varepsilon^{2}\right)
and
L/\varepsilon^{2}-X=\mathcal{O}\left(1/\varepsilon^{2}\right)
. Nevertheless, the expressions of these fronts will not be uniformly valid in these limits due to the term
X\,e^{-2\,\sqrt{\beta_{1}}\,X}
present in
A_{3}
(unless
\Delta_{3,2}=0
). Therefore, in the general case, we must construct an extra outer expansion valid inside the extended periodic domain (see the periodic pattern when the fronts merge in Figure
3
) to match it with these two fronts.

C.1
Outer expansion inside the extended periodic domain

To obtain a suitable outer approximation, we take the usual expansion, (
110
), only focusing on the limit as
X\to\infty
(see the periodic pattern when the fronts merge in Figure
3
). In particular, we note that

\displaystyle A_{1}=\Delta_{1,1}\,e^{\Phi\,i}

solves the amplitude equation at fifth order, (
7
). With this, we can solve the equation for the remainder at order five, (
66
), by setting
B_{1}(X,\Xi)=\left(\Lambda_{1}(X,\Xi)+i\,\Omega_{1}(X,\Xi)\right)\,e^{i\,\Phi(X,\Xi)}
, which transforms that equation into

\displaystyle\alpha_{1}\,\Lambda_{1_{X\!X}}-\gamma_{1,2,-}\,\Omega_{1_{X}}+\gamma_{1,1}\,\Lambda_{1}

\displaystyle=0,

(114)

\displaystyle\alpha_{1}\,\Omega_{1_{X\!X}}+\gamma_{1,2,+}\,\Lambda_{1_{X}}

\displaystyle=0,

(115)

where

\displaystyle\gamma_{1,1}

\displaystyle=\frac{4\,\left(\alpha_{4}\,\left(\alpha_{3}+\alpha_{4}\right)+4\,\alpha_{1}\,\alpha_{7}\right)\,\beta_{1}^{2}}{3\,\alpha_{1}\,\beta_{3}^{2}},

\displaystyle\gamma_{1,2,\pm}

\displaystyle=2\,\alpha_{1}\,\Phi_{X}+\left(\alpha_{3}\pm\alpha_{4}\right)\,\Delta_{1}^{2}+\alpha_{2}.

Now, if we integrate (
115
) once, we obtain

\displaystyle\Omega_{1_{X}}=-\dfrac{\gamma_{1,2,+}}{\alpha_{1}}\,\Lambda_{1},

which transforms (
114
) into

\displaystyle\alpha_{1}\,\Lambda_{1_{X\!X}}-4\,\alpha_{1}\,\beta_{1}\,\Lambda_{1}=0,

Therefore,

\displaystyle\Lambda_{1}=\Psi(\Xi)\,e^{2\,\sqrt{\beta_{1}}\,X}+\Pi(\Xi)\,e^{-2\,\sqrt{\beta_{1}}\,X},

which implies

\displaystyle\Omega_{1}=2\,\eta\,\left(\Psi(\Xi)\,e^{2\,\sqrt{\beta_{1}}\,X}-\Pi(\Xi)\,e^{-2\,\sqrt{\beta_{1}}\,X}\right).

With this, we can obtain the leading-order solution of the amplitude equation at order seven, (
126
) (see
D
for an explanation regarding notation and the new variables present in this expansion):

\alpha_{1}\,A_{3_{X\!X}}+i\,\alpha_{2}\,A_{3_{X}}+i\,\alpha_{3}\,\absolutevalue{A_1}^{2}\,A_{3_{X}}+i\,\alpha_{4}\,A_{1}^{2}\,\bar{A}_{3_{X}}+\alpha_{5}\,A_{3}+2\,\alpha_{6}\,\absolutevalue{A_1}^{2}\,A_{3}+3\,\alpha_{7}\,\absolutevalue{A_1}^{4}\,A_{3}\\
+i\,\alpha_{3}\,\bar{A}_{1}\,A_{1_{X}}\,A_{3}+2\,i\,\alpha_{4}\,A_{1}\,\bar{A}_{1_{X}}\,A_{3}+\alpha_{6}\,A_{1}^{2}\,\bar{A}_{3}+2\,\alpha_{7}\,\absolutevalue{A_1}^{2}\,A_{1}^{2}\,\bar{A}_{3}+i\,\alpha_{3}\,A_{1}\,A_{1_{X}}\,\bar{A}_{3}\\
+i\,\alpha_{1,4}\,A_{1_{X}}+i\,\alpha_{2,4}\,\absolutevalue{A_1}^{2}\,A_{1_{X}}+i\,\alpha_{3,4}\,\absolutevalue{A_1}^{4}\,A_{1_{X}}+i\,\alpha_{4,4}\,A_{1}^{2}\,\bar{A}_{1_{X}}+i\,\alpha_{5,4}\,\absolutevalue{A_1}^{2}\,A_{1}^{2}\,\bar{A}_{1_{X}}\\
+\alpha_{6,4}\,\bar{A}_{1}\,\left(A_{1_{X}}\right)^{2}+\alpha_{7,4}\,A_{1}\,\absolutevalue{A_{1_X}}^{2}+\alpha_{8,4}\,A_{1}+\alpha_{9,4}\,\absolutevalue{A_1}^{2}\,A_{1}+\alpha_{10,4}\,\absolutevalue{A_1}^{4}\,A_{1}+\alpha_{11,4}\,\absolutevalue{A_1}^{6}\,A_{1}\\
+2\,\alpha_{1}\,A_{1_{X\!\Xi}}+i\,\alpha_{2}\,A_{1_{\Xi}}+i\,\alpha_{3}\,\absolutevalue{A_1}^{2}\,A_{1_{\Xi}}+i\,\alpha_{4}\,A_{1}^{2}\,\bar{A}_{1_{\Xi}}=0,

In particular, note that
A_{3}=\Delta_{3,1}\,e^{\Phi\,i}
solves the seventh-order equation for
A_{3}
.

Now, we note that the full homogeneous equation for the remainder at order seven is given by (
134
) (see
E
). However, for simplicity, as stated in
D
, we can assume that
A_{2}=B_{2}=0
as that is, generically, the case (the right-hand side of the differential equations to determine
A_{2}
and
B_{2}
can be forced to equal zero). This transforms the equation for the remainder at order seven into:

\alpha_{1}\,B_{3_{X\!X}}+i\,\alpha_{2}\,B_{3_{X}}+i\,\alpha_{3}\,\absolutevalue{A_1}^{2}\,B_{3_{X}}+i\,\alpha_{3}\,\bar{A}_{1}\,A_{1_{X}}\,B_{3}+i\,\alpha_{3}\,A_{1}\,A_{1_{X}}\,\bar{B}_{3}+i\,\alpha_{4}\,A_{1}^{2}\,\bar{B}_{3_{X}}\\
+2\,i\,\alpha_{4}\,A_{1}\,\bar{A}_{1_{X}}\,B_{3}+\alpha_{5}\,B_{3}+\alpha_{6}\,A_{1}^{2}\,\bar{B}_{3}+2\,\alpha_{6}\,\absolutevalue{A_1}^{2}\,B_{3}+2\,\alpha_{7}\,\absolutevalue{A_1}^{2}\,A_{1}^{2}\,\bar{B}_{3}+3\,\alpha_{7}\,\absolutevalue{A_1}^{4}\,B_{3}\\
+i\,\alpha_{1,3}\,B_{1_{X\!X\!X}}+\alpha_{2,3}\,B_{1_{X\!X}}+\alpha_{3,3}\,\absolutevalue{A_1}^{2}\,B_{1_{X\!X}}+\alpha_{3,3}\,\bar{A}_{1}\,A_{1_{X\!X}}\,B_{1}+\alpha_{3,3}\,A_{1}\,A_{1_{X\!X}}\,\bar{B}_{1}\\
+\alpha_{4,3}\,A_{1}^{2}\,\bar{B}_{1_{X\!X}}+2\,\alpha_{4,3}\,A_{1}\,\bar{A}_{1_{X\!X}}\,B_{1}+i\,\alpha_{5,3}\,B_{1_{X}}+i\,\alpha_{6,3}\,\absolutevalue{A_1}^{2}\,B_{1_{X}}+i\,\alpha_{6,3}\,\bar{A}_{1}\,A_{1_{X}}\,B_{1}\\
+i\,\alpha_{6,3}\,A_{1}\,A_{1_{X}}\,\bar{B}_{1}+i\,\alpha_{7,3}\,\absolutevalue{A_1}^{4}\,B_{1_{X}}+2\,i\,\alpha_{7,3}\,\absolutevalue{A_1}^{2}\,\bar{A}_{1}\,A_{1_{X}}\,B_{1}+2\,i\,\alpha_{7,3}\,\absolutevalue{A_1}^{2}\,A_{1}\,A_{1_{X}}\,\bar{B}_{1}\\
+i\,\alpha_{8,3}\,A_{1}^{2}\,\bar{B}_{1_{X}}+2\,i\,\alpha_{8,3}\,A_{1}\,\bar{A}_{1_{X}}\,B_{1}+i\,\alpha_{9,3}\,\absolutevalue{A_1}^{2}\,A_{1}^{2}\,\bar{B}_{1_{X}}+i\,\alpha_{9,3}\,A_{1}^{3}\,\bar{A}_{1_{X}}\,\bar{B}_{1}\\
+3\,i\,\alpha_{9,3}\,\absolutevalue{A_1}^{2}\,A_{1}\,\bar{A}_{1_{X}}\,B_{1}+\alpha_{10,3}\,A_{1_{X}}^{2}\,\bar{B}_{1}+2\,\alpha_{10,3}\,\bar{A}_{1}\,A_{1_{X}}\,B_{1_{X}}+\alpha_{11,3}\,A_{1}\,\bar{A}_{1_{X}}\,B_{1_{X}}\\
+\alpha_{11,3}\,A_{1}\,A_{1_{X}}\,\bar{B}_{1_{X}}+\alpha_{11,3}\,\absolutevalue{A_{1_X}}^{2}\,B_{1}+\alpha_{12,3}\,B_{1}+\alpha_{13,3}\,A_{1}^{2}\,\bar{B}_{1}+2\,\alpha_{13,3}\,\absolutevalue{A_1}^{2}\,B_{1}\\
+2\,\alpha_{14,3}\,\absolutevalue{A_1}^{2}\,A_{1}^{2}\,\bar{B}_{1}+3\,\alpha_{14,3}\,\absolutevalue{A_1}^{4}\,B_{1}+3\,\alpha_{15,3}\,\absolutevalue{A_1}^{4}\,A_{1}^{2}\,\bar{B}_{1}+4\,\alpha_{15,3}\,\absolutevalue{A_1}^{6}\,B_{1}\\
+2\,\alpha_{1}\,B_{1_{X\!\Xi}}+i\,\alpha_{2}\,B_{1_{\Xi}}+i\,\alpha_{3}\,\absolutevalue{A_1}^{2}\,B_{1_{\Xi}}+i\,\alpha_{3}\,\bar{A}_{1}\,A_{1_{\Xi}}\,B_{1}+i\,\alpha_{3}\,A_{1}\,A_{1_{\Xi}}\,\bar{B}_{1}+i\,\alpha_{4}\,A_{1}^{2}\,\bar{B}_{1_{\Xi}}\\
+2\,i\,\alpha_{4}\,A_{1}\,\bar{A}_{1_{\Xi}}\,B_{1}+i\,\alpha_{3}\,\bar{A}_{1}\,A_{3}\,B_{1_{X}}+i\,\alpha_{3}\,A_{1}\,\bar{A}_{3}\,B_{1_{X}}+i\,\alpha_{3}\,\bar{A}_{1}\,A_{3_{X}}\,B_{1}+i\,\alpha_{3}\,A_{1}\,A_{3_{X}}\,\bar{B}_{1}\\
+i\,\alpha_{3}\,A_{1_{X}}\,\bar{A}_{3}\,B_{1}+i\,\alpha_{3}\,A_{1_{X}}\,A_{3}\,\bar{B}_{1}+2\,i\,\alpha_{4}\,A_{1}\,A_{3}\,\bar{B}_{1_{X}}+2\,i\,\alpha_{4}\,A_{1}\,\bar{A}_{3_{X}}\,B_{1}\\
+2\,i\,\alpha_{4}\,\bar{A}_{1_{X}}\,A_{3}\,B_{1}+2\,\alpha_{6}\,\bar{A}_{1}\,A_{3}\,B_{1}+2\,\alpha_{6}\,A_{1}\,\bar{A}_{3}\,B_{1}+2\,\alpha_{6}\,A_{1}\,A_{3}\,\bar{B}_{1}+2\,\alpha_{7}\,A_{1}^{3}\,\bar{A}_{3}\,\bar{B}_{1}\\
+6\,\alpha_{7}\,\absolutevalue{A_1}^{2}\,A_{1}\,\bar{A}_{3}\,B_{1}+6\,\alpha_{7}\,\absolutevalue{A_1}^{2}\,\bar{A}_{1}\,A_{3}\,B_{1}+6\,\alpha_{7}\,\absolutevalue{A_1}^{2}\,A_{1}\,A_{3}\,\bar{B}_{1}=0.

Now, if we set
B_{3}=\left(\Lambda_{3}(X,\Xi)+i\,\Omega_{3}(X,\Xi)\right)\,e^{i\,\Phi}
, we obtain two equations given by

\displaystyle\alpha_{1}\,\Lambda_{3_{X\!X}}+\gamma_{1,3}\,\Omega_{3_{X}}-\gamma_{1,4}\,\Lambda_{3}=\left(\gamma_{1,5}\,\Psi+\gamma_{1,6}\,\Psi_{\Xi}\right)\,e^{2\,\sqrt{\beta_{1}}\,X}+\left(\gamma_{1,7}\,\Pi-\gamma_{1,6}\,\Pi_{\Xi}\right)\,e^{-2\,\sqrt{\beta_{1}}\,X},

(116)

\displaystyle\alpha_{1}\,\Omega_{3_{X\!X}}+\gamma_{1,8}\,\Lambda_{3_{X}}=\left(\gamma_{1,9}\,\Psi+\gamma_{1,8}\,\Psi_{\Xi}\right)\,e^{2\,\sqrt{\beta_{1}}\,X}+\left(\gamma_{1,10}\,\Pi+\gamma_{1,8}\,\Pi_{\Xi}\right)\,e^{-2\,\sqrt{\beta_{1}}\,X},

(117)

where

\displaystyle\gamma_{1,3}

\displaystyle=\frac{\left(\alpha_{3}-3\,\alpha_{4}\right)\,\beta_{1}}{\beta_{3}},

\displaystyle\gamma_{1,4}

\displaystyle=4\,\alpha_{1}\,\beta_{1}-\frac{\gamma_{1,3}\,\gamma_{1,8}}{\alpha_{1}},

\displaystyle\gamma_{1,5}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{24\,\alpha_{1}^{3}\,\beta_{3}^{5}}\,\left(2\,\alpha_{1}\,\left(-2\,\alpha_{3}\,\beta_{1}\,\left(\alpha_{1}\,\beta_{3}^{2}\,\left(28\,\beta_{1}^{2}\,\left(\alpha_{3,4}+\alpha_{5,4}\right)-15\,\beta_{3}\,\beta_{1}\,\left(\alpha_{2,4}+\alpha_{4,4}\right)\right.\right.\right.\right.

\displaystyle\gamma_{1,6}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\sqrt{\beta_{1}}\,\left(\left(\alpha_{3}^{2}-2\,\alpha_{3}\,\alpha_{4}-3\,\alpha_{4}^{2}\right)\,\beta_{1}+8\,\alpha_{1}^{2}\,\beta_{3}^{2}\right)}{2\,\alpha_{1}\,\beta_{3}^{2}},

\displaystyle\gamma_{1,9}

\displaystyle\quad+3\,\alpha_{2}\,\beta_{1}\,\beta_{3}^{3}\,\left(8\,\alpha_{1}^{3}\,\beta_{3}^{2}\,\left(5\,\left(\alpha_{3}+\alpha_{4}\right)\,\zeta_{\Xi}-8\,\beta_{1}\,\alpha_{6,4}\right)\right.

\displaystyle\quad\left.-8\,\left(\alpha_{3}+\alpha_{4}\right)\,\alpha_{1}^{2}\,\beta_{3}\,\left(\left(\alpha_{3}-\alpha_{4}\right)\,\beta_{1}-2\,\beta_{3}\,\alpha_{1,4}\right)+\left(\alpha_{3}+\alpha_{4}\right)^{2}\,\left(\alpha_{3}-3\,\alpha_{4}\right)\,\beta_{1}\,\alpha_{1,4}\right)

\displaystyle\quad\left.+3\,\alpha_{2}^{2}\,\left(\alpha_{3}+\alpha_{4}\right)\,\beta_{1}\,\beta_{3}^{3}\,\left(20\,\alpha_{1}^{2}\,\beta_{3}^{2}+\left(\alpha_{3}+\alpha_{4}\right)\,\left(\alpha_{3}-3\,\alpha_{4}\right)\,\beta_{1}\right)\right),

\displaystyle\gamma_{1,10}

\displaystyle=-\gamma_{1,9}+\frac{2\,\beta_{1}\,\left(\left(\alpha_{3}+\alpha_{4}\right)^{2}\,\beta_{1}+4\,\alpha_{1}^{2}\,\beta_{3}^{2}\right)}{\alpha_{1}\,\beta_{3}^{2}}\,\omega_{4}.

Next, note that we can integrate (

) with respect to
X
, to obtain

\displaystyle\Omega_{3_{X}}=\frac{1}{\alpha_{1}}\,\left(\frac{1}{2\,\sqrt{\beta_{1}}}\,\left(\left(\gamma_{1,9}\,\Psi+\gamma_{1,8}\,\Psi_{\Xi}\right)\,e^{2\,\sqrt{\beta_{1}}\,X}-\left(\gamma_{1,10}\,\Pi+\gamma_{1,8}\,\Pi_{\Xi}\right)\,e^{-2\,\sqrt{\beta_{1}}\,X}\right)-\gamma_{1,8}\,\Lambda_{3}\right),

which can be replaced into (

\displaystyle\alpha_{1}\,\Lambda_{3_{X\!X}}-4\,\alpha_{1}\,\beta_{1}\,\Lambda_{3}=-4\,\alpha_{1}\,\sqrt{\beta_{1}}\,\left(\left(\gamma_{1,11}\,\Psi+\Psi_{\Xi}\right)\,e^{2\sqrt{\beta_{1}}X}+\left(\gamma_{1,11}\,\Pi-\Pi_{\Xi}\right)\,e^{-2\,\sqrt{\beta_{1}}\,X}\right),

(118)

where

\displaystyle\gamma_{1,11}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{384\,\alpha_{1}^{6}\,\sqrt{\beta_{1}}\,\beta_{3}^{7}}\,\left(3\,\alpha_{2}^{2}\,\left(-144\,\alpha_{1}^{4}\,\beta_{3}^{4}-28\,\alpha_{1}^{2}\,\left(\alpha_{3}-3\,\alpha_{4}\right)\,\left(\alpha_{3}+\alpha_{4}\right)\,\beta_{1}\,\beta_{3}^{2}\right.\right.

\displaystyle\quad\left.+\left(\alpha_{3}+\alpha_{4}\right)^{2}\,\left(\alpha_{3}-3\,\alpha_{4}\right)^{2}\,\beta_{1}^{2}\right)\,\beta_{3}^{3}-3\,\alpha_{2}\,\left(288\,\alpha_{1}^{5}\,\beta_{3}^{4}\,\zeta_{\Xi}\right.

\displaystyle\quad\left.+16\,\alpha_{1}^{4}\,\beta_{3}^{3}\,\left(\left(\alpha_{3}-15\,\alpha_{4}\right)\,\beta_{1}+8\,\beta_{3}\,\alpha_{1,4}\right)\right.

\displaystyle\quad+8\,\alpha_{1}^{3}\,\left(\alpha_{3}-3\,\alpha_{4}\right)\,\beta_{1}\,\beta_{3}^{2}\left(12\,\beta_{1}\,\alpha_{6,4}+7\,\left(\alpha_{3}+\alpha_{4}\right)\,\zeta_{\Xi}\right)

\displaystyle\quad+8\,\alpha_{1}^{2}\,\left(\alpha_{3}+\alpha_{4}\right)\,\left(\alpha_{3}-3\,\alpha_{4}\right)\,\beta_{1}\,\beta_{3}\,\left(\left(\alpha_{3}-\alpha_{4}\right)\,\beta_{1}+4\,\beta_{3}\,\alpha_{1,4}\right)

\displaystyle\quad-2\,\alpha_{1}\,\left(\alpha_{3}-3\,\alpha_{4}\right)^{2}\,\left(\alpha_{3}+\alpha_{4}\right)\,\beta_{1}^{2}\,\left(\left(\alpha_{3}+\alpha_{4}\right)\,\zeta_{\Xi}-2\,\beta_{1}\,\alpha_{6,4}\right)

\displaystyle\quad\left.-\left(\alpha_{3}+\alpha_{4}\right)^{2}\,\left(\alpha_{3}-3\,\alpha_{4}\right)^{2}\,\beta_{1}^{2}\,\alpha_{1,4}\right)\,\beta_{3}^{3}

\displaystyle\quad+2\,\alpha_{1}\,\left(-48\,\alpha_{1}^{3}\,\left(\alpha_{3}-3\,\alpha_{4}\right)\,\beta_{1}\,\left(4\,\beta_{1}^{2}\,\left(\alpha_{3,4}+\alpha_{5,4}\right)\right.\right.

\displaystyle\quad\left.-3\,\beta_{1}\,\beta_{3}\,\left(\alpha_{2,4}+\alpha_{4,4}\right)+3\,\beta_{3}^{2}\,\alpha_{1,4}\right)\,\beta_{3}^{4}+96\,\alpha_{1}^{4}\,\left(8\,\beta_{1}^{3}\,\beta_{7,3}-8\,\beta_{1}^{2}\,\beta_{3}\,\beta_{5,3}\right.

\displaystyle\quad\left.+\beta_{3}^{2}\,\left(6\,\beta_{3,3}+\left(9\,\alpha_{4}-3\,\alpha_{3}\right)\,\zeta_{\Xi}\right)\,\beta_{1}-4\,\beta_{3}^{3}\,\alpha_{8,4}\right)\,\beta_{3}^{4}

\displaystyle\quad-2\,\alpha_{1}\,\left(\alpha_{3}+\alpha_{4}\right)\,\left(\alpha_{3}-3\,\alpha_{4}\right)^{2}\,\beta_{1}^{2}\,\beta_{3}^{2}\,\left(4\,\left(\alpha_{3,4}+\alpha_{5,4}\right)\,\beta_{1}^{2}\right.

\displaystyle\quad\left.-3\,\beta_{3}\,\left(\alpha_{2,4}+\alpha_{4,4}\right)\,\beta_{1}+3\,\beta_{3}^{2}\,\alpha_{1,4}\right)

\displaystyle\quad-4\,\alpha_{1}^{2}\,\left(\alpha_{3}-3\,\alpha_{4}\right)\,\left(\alpha_{3}+\alpha_{4}\right)\,\beta_{1}\,\beta_{3}^{2}\,\left(60\,\beta_{1}^{3}\,\beta_{7,3}\right.

\displaystyle\quad-\beta_{1}^{2}\,\beta_{3}\,\left(\beta_{3}\,\left(31\,\alpha_{6,4}+7\,\alpha_{7,4}\right)+16\,\beta_{5,3}\right)

\displaystyle\quad\left.+\beta_{3}^{2}\,\left(3\,\left(\alpha_{3}-3\,\alpha_{4}\right)\,\zeta_{\Xi}-6\,\beta_{3,3}\right)\,\beta_{1}+24\,\beta_{3}^{3}\,\alpha_{8,4}\right)

\displaystyle\quad-\left(\alpha_{3}-3\,\alpha_{4}\right)^{2}\,\left(\alpha_{3}+\alpha_{4}\right)^{2}\,\beta_{1}^{2}\,\left(24\,\beta_{1}^{3}\,\beta_{7,3}-4\,\beta_{3}\,\left(\beta_{3}\,\alpha_{6,4}+3\,\beta_{5,3}\right)\,\beta_{1}^{2}\right.

\displaystyle\quad\left.\left.\left.+6\,\beta_{1}\,\beta_{3}^{2}\,\beta_{3,3}-3\,\beta_{3}^{3}\,\alpha_{8,4}\right)\right)\right).

Now, as the term on the right-hand side of (
118
) is secular, we need to set

\displaystyle\Psi=\Psi_{0}\,e^{-\gamma_{1,11}\,\Xi},\qquad\text{and}\qquad\Pi=\Pi_{0}\,e^{\gamma_{1,11}\,\Xi},

in order to ensure the solution is bounded.

In summary, we can conclude that the far-field solution inside the localized pattern is given by

\displaystyle\mathbf{u}^{*}

\displaystyle\sim\left(\varepsilon\,\left(\Delta_{1,1}+\Lambda_{1}+i\,\Omega_{1}\right)+\varepsilon^{3}\,\Delta_{3,1}\right)\,e^{\Phi\,i}\,e^{i\left(x+\tilde{\chi}\right)}\,\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]}+c.c.

\displaystyle=\varepsilon\,\left(\Delta_{1,1}+(1+2\,\eta\,i)\,\Psi_{0}\,e^{2\,\sqrt{\beta_{1}}\,X}\,e^{-\gamma_{1,11}\,\Xi}\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\left.+(1-2\,\eta\,i)\,\Pi_{0}\,e^{-2\,\sqrt{\beta_{1}}\,X}\,e^{\gamma_{1,11}\,\Xi}+\varepsilon^{2}\,\Delta_{3,1}\right)\,e^{\Phi\,i}\,e^{i\left(x-\tilde{\chi}\right)}\,\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]}

\displaystyle+c.c.,

(119)

where
\tilde{\chi}
is the phase shift for the fast oscillations.

The determination of the constants
\Psi_{0}
and
\Pi_{0}
can be carried out by matching the fronts and the far-field solutions inside the localized patterns. Specifically, they can be found by using
[
10
]

\displaystyle\lim_{X\to\infty}\mathbf{u}\left(x,X,0^{-}\right)\sim\lim_{\Xi\to 0^{+}}\mathbf{u}^{*}(x,X,\Xi),

(120)

for matching (
112
) to (
119
), and

\displaystyle\lim_{X-L/\varepsilon^{2}\to-\infty}\mathbf{u}\left(x,X,L^{+}\right)\sim\lim_{\Xi\to L^{-}}\mathbf{u}^{*}(x,X,\Xi).

(121)

for matching (
113
) to (
119
).

However, these limits depend on the function
\delta=\delta\left(R_{1}\right)
, which is related to the separation of one parameter from the Maxwell point (see equation (
66
)), and depends on the specific form of the vector field posed in (
1
).

C.2
Summary of
C

The calculations we carried out up until Section
6
let us obtain an approximation of the width of the homoclinic snaking close to codimension-two Turing bifurcation points. Nevertheless, from the beginning, we stated that we have been studying two solutions at the same time, an up-front and a down-front (see sub-Section
2.3
).
C
gives the final conditions that need to be met to match these fronts. We have to bear in mind that these conditions depend on
\delta b
, the separation of parameter
b
from the Maxwell point and, therefore, after determining bounds for that parameter by using (
87
), one can use conditions (
120
) and (
121
) to obtain approximations of the homoclinic snaking as a function of
\varepsilon
.

We highlight that, in the end, although the limits (
120
) and (
121
) seem complicated to evaluate, what one really needs to do to match the fronts is to equate the corresponding coefficients of the constant and exponential terms in
X
in (
112
), (
113
), and (
119
). It is worth noting that, in the limit (
121
), one needs to match the corresponding exponentials by making use of the complex conjugates of (
112
), (
113
), and (
119
), for the equations to be well-posed (see e.g.
[
10
]
).

Appendix D
Expansion up to order 7

To iterate (
35
), we need to obtain the dominant mode of
A_{3}
and use it as part of the initial condition. Therefore, we need to study the amplitude equation at order 7. To do this, we define an extra small variable for the expansion:
\Xi=\varepsilon^{2}\,X=\varepsilon^{4}\,x
.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}\mathbf{u}}{\partial x^{2}}=\frac{\partial^{2}\mathbf{u}}{\partial x^{2}}+2\,\varepsilon^{2}\,\frac{\partial^{2}\mathbf{u}}{\partial x\partial X}+\varepsilon^{4}\,\left(\frac{\partial^{2}\mathbf{u}}{\partial X^{2}}+2\,\frac{\partial^{2}\mathbf{u}}{\partial x\partial\Xi}\right)+2\,\varepsilon^{6}\,\frac{\partial^{2}\mathbf{u}}{\partial X\partial\Xi}+\varepsilon^{8}\,\frac{\partial^{2}\mathbf{u}}{\partial\Xi^{2}}.

Thus, the equation at order five gets an extra term given by

\displaystyle 2\,k^{2}\,\hat{D}\,\frac{\partial^{2}\mathbf{u}^{[1]}}{\partial x\partial\Xi},

which does not affect the Ginzburg-Landau equation, (
7
), but it does imply that
A_{1}=A_{1}(X,\Xi)
, which yields
\varphi_{1}=\varphi_{1}(X,\Xi)=\varphi_{1}(X)+\zeta(\Xi)
, where
\zeta
is a real function still to be determined.

Here, we use the notation
A_{X}=\partial_{X}A
or
A_{X}=\dfrac{\differential A}{\differential X}
to denote partial or ordinary derivatives, disregarding the change of notation depending on whether functions depend on one or more variables. With this, we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{u}^{[5]}

\displaystyle=\absolutevalue{A_1}^{2}\,\mathbf{W}_{0}^{[5]}+\absolutevalue{A_1}^{4}\,\mathbf{W}_{0,2}^{[5]}+i\,\bar{A}_{1}\,A_{1_{X}}\,\mathbf{W}_{0,3}^{[5]}+A_{1_{X\!X}}\,e^{ix}\,\mathbf{W}_{1}^{[5]}+i\,A_{1_{X}}\,e^{ix}\,\mathbf{W}_{1,2}^{[5]}

\displaystyle\quad+i\,\absolutevalue{A_1}^{2}\,A_{1_{X}}\,e^{ix}\,\mathbf{W}_{1,3}^{[5]}+i\,A_{1}^{2}\,\bar{A}_{1_{X}}\,e^{ix}\,\mathbf{W}_{1,4}^{[5]}+A_{1}\,e^{ix}\,\mathbf{W}_{1,5}^{[5]}+\absolutevalue{A_1}^{2}\,A_{1}\,e^{ix}\,\mathbf{W}_{1,6}^{[5]}

\displaystyle\quad+\absolutevalue{A_1}^{4}\,A_{1}\,e^{ix}\,\mathbf{W}_{1,7}^{[5]}+i\,A_{1_{\Xi}}\,\mathbf{W}_{1,3}^{[3]}\,e^{ix}+A_{1}^{2}\,e^{2ix}\,\mathbf{W}_{2}^{[5]}+\absolutevalue{A_1}^{2}\,A_{1}^{2}\,e^{2ix}\,\mathbf{W}_{2,2}^{[5]}

\displaystyle\quad+i\,A_{1}\,A_{1_{X}}\,e^{2ix}\,\mathbf{W}_{2,3}^{[5]}+A_{1}^{3}\,e^{3ix}\,\mathbf{W}_{3}^{[5]}+\absolutevalue{A_1}^{2}\,A_{1}^{3}\,e^{3ix}\,\mathbf{W}_{3,2}^{[5]}+i\,A_{1}^{2}\,A_{1_{X}}\,e^{3ix}\,\mathbf{W}_{3,3}^{[5]}

\displaystyle\quad+2\,A_{1}\,\bar{A}_{2}\,\mathbf{W}_{0}^{[4]}+4\,\absolutevalue{A_1}^{2}\,A_{1}\,\bar{A}_{2}\,\mathbf{W}_{0,2}^{[4]}+i\,A_{1_{X}}\,\bar{A}_{2}\,\mathbf{W}_{0,3}^{[4]}+i\,\bar{A}_{1}\,A_{2_{X}}\,\mathbf{W}_{0,3}^{[4]}

\displaystyle\quad+2\,A_{1}\,\bar{A}_{3}\,\mathbf{W}_{0}^{[3]}+\absolutevalue{A_2}^{2}\,\mathbf{W}_{0}^{[3]}+2\,A_{2}\,\bar{A}_{3}\,\mathbf{W}_{0}^{[2]}+2\,A_{1}\,\bar{A}_{4}\,\mathbf{W}_{0}^{[2]}

\displaystyle\quad+A_{2}\,e^{ix}\,\mathbf{W}_{1}^{[4]}+A_{1}^{2}\,\bar{A}_{2}\,e^{ix}\,\mathbf{W}_{1,2}^{[4]}+2\,\absolutevalue{A_1}^{2}\,A_{2}\,e^{ix}\,\mathbf{W}_{1,2}^{[4]}+i\,A_{2_{X}}\,e^{ix}\,\mathbf{W}_{1,3}^{[4]}

\displaystyle\quad+A_{3}\,e^{ix}\,\mathbf{W}_{1}^{[3]}+2\,A_{1}\,\absolutevalue{A_2}^{2}\,e^{ix}\,\mathbf{W}_{1,2}^{[3]}+\bar{A}_{1}\,A_{2}^{2}\,e^{ix}\,\mathbf{W}_{1,2}^{[3]}+A_{1}^{2}\,\bar{A}_{3}\,e^{ix}\,\mathbf{W}_{1,2}^{[3]}

\displaystyle\quad+2\,\absolutevalue{A_1}^{2}\,A_{3}\,e^{ix}\,\mathbf{W}_{1,2}^{[3]}+i\,A_{3_{X}}\,e^{ix}\,\mathbf{W}_{1,3}^{[3]}+A_{4}\,e^{ix}\,\mathbf{W}_{1}^{[2]}+A_{5}\,e^{ix}\,\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]}+2\,A_{1}\,A_{2}\,e^{2ix}\,\mathbf{W}_{2}^{[4]}

\displaystyle\quad+3\,\absolutevalue{A_1}^{2}\,A_{1}\,A_{2}\,e^{2ix}\,\mathbf{W}_{2,2}^{[4]}+A_{1}^{3}\,\bar{A}_{2}\,e^{2ix}\,\mathbf{W}_{2,2}^{[4]}+i\,A_{1}\,A_{2_{X}}\,e^{2ix}\,\mathbf{W}_{2,3}^{[4]}+i\,A_{1_{X}}\,A_{2}\,e^{2ix}\,\mathbf{W}_{2,3}^{[4]}

\displaystyle\quad+2\,A_{1}\,A_{3}\,e^{2ix}\,\mathbf{W}_{2}^{[3]}+A_{2}^{2}\,e^{2ix}\,\mathbf{W}_{2}^{[3]}+2\,A_{1}\,A_{4}\,e^{2ix}\,\mathbf{W}_{2}^{[2]}+2\,A_{2}\,A_{3}\,e^{2ix}\,\mathbf{W}_{2}^{[2]}

\displaystyle\quad+3\,A_{1}^{2}\,A_{2}\,e^{3ix}\,\mathbf{W}_{3}^{[4]}+3\,A_{1}^{2}\,A_{3}\,e^{3ix}\,\mathbf{W}_{3}^{[3]}+3\,A_{1}\,A_{2}^{2}\,e^{3ix}\,\mathbf{W}_{3}^{[3]}+\ldots+c.c.,

where ‘
\ldots
’ represents terms that are multiples of
e^{4ix}
or
e^{5ix}
, and

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{0}\,\mathbf{W}_{0}^{[5]}

\displaystyle=-a_{1}\,J_{\mathbf{u}}\,\mathbf{f}_{1,0}(\mathbf{0})\,\mathbf{W}_{0}^{[4]}-b_{1}\,J_{\mathbf{u}}\,\mathbf{f}_{0,1}(\mathbf{0})\,\mathbf{W}_{0}^{[4]}-a_{2}\,J_{\mathbf{u}}\,\mathbf{f}_{1,0}(\mathbf{0})\,\mathbf{W}_{0}^{[3]}-b_{2}\,J_{\mathbf{u}}\,\mathbf{f}_{0,1}(\mathbf{0})\,\mathbf{W}_{0}^{[3]}

\displaystyle\quad-a_{3}\,J_{\mathbf{u}}\,\mathbf{f}_{1,0}(\mathbf{0})\,\mathbf{W}_{0}^{[2]}-b_{3}\,J_{\mathbf{u}}\,\mathbf{f}_{0,1}(\mathbf{0})\,\mathbf{W}_{0}^{[2]}-a_{1}^{2}\,J_{\mathbf{u}}\,\mathbf{f}_{2,0}(\mathbf{0})\,\mathbf{W}_{0}^{[3]}-a_{1}\,b_{1}\,J_{\mathbf{u}}\,\mathbf{f}_{1,1}(\mathbf{0})\,\mathbf{W}_{0}^{[3]}

\displaystyle\quad-b_{1}^{2}\,J_{\mathbf{u}}\,\mathbf{f}_{0,2}(\mathbf{0})\,\mathbf{W}_{0}^{[3]}-2\,a_{1}\,a_{2}\,J_{\mathbf{u}}\,\mathbf{f}_{2,0}(\mathbf{0})\,\mathbf{W}_{0}^{[2]}-\left(a_{1}\,b_{2}+a_{2}\,b_{1}\right)\,J_{\mathbf{u}}\,\mathbf{f}_{1,1}(\mathbf{0})\,\mathbf{W}_{0}^{[2]}

\displaystyle\quad-2\,b_{1}\,b_{2}\,J_{\mathbf{u}}\,\mathbf{f}_{0,2}(\mathbf{0})\,\mathbf{W}_{0}^{[2]}-a_{1}^{3}\,J_{\mathbf{u}}\,\mathbf{f}_{3,0}(\mathbf{0})\,\mathbf{W}_{0}^{[2]}-a_{1}^{2}\,b_{1}\,J_{\mathbf{u}}\,\mathbf{f}_{2,1}(\mathbf{0})\,\mathbf{W}_{0}^{[2]}

\displaystyle\quad-a_{1}\,b_{1}^{2}\,J_{\mathbf{u}}\,\mathbf{f}_{1,2}(\mathbf{0})\,\mathbf{W}_{0}^{[2]}-b_{1}^{3}\,J_{\mathbf{u}}\,\mathbf{f}_{0,3}(\mathbf{0})\,\mathbf{W}_{0}^{[2]}-2\,\mathbf{F}_{2,0,0}\left(\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]},\mathbf{W}_{1}^{[4]}\right)

\displaystyle\quad-2\,\mathbf{F}_{2,0,0}\left(\mathbf{W}_{1}^{[2]},\mathbf{W}_{1}^{[3]}\right)-2\,a_{1}\,\mathbf{F}_{2,1,0}\left(\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]},\mathbf{W}_{1}^{[3]}\right)-2\,b_{1}\,\mathbf{F}_{2,0,1}\left(\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]},\mathbf{W}_{1}^{[3]}\right)

\displaystyle\quad-a_{1}\,\mathbf{F}_{2,1,0}\left(\mathbf{W}_{1}^{[2]},\mathbf{W}_{1}^{[2]}\right)-b_{1}\,\mathbf{F}_{2,0,1}\left(\mathbf{W}_{1}^{[2]},\mathbf{W}_{1}^{[2]}\right)-2\,a_{2}\,\mathbf{F}_{2,1,0}\left(\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]},\mathbf{W}_{1}^{[2]}\right)

\displaystyle\quad-2\,b_{2}\,\mathbf{F}_{2,0,1}\left(\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]},\mathbf{W}_{1}^{[2]}\right)-a_{3}\,\mathbf{F}_{2,1,0}\left(\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]},\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]}\right)-b_{3}\,\mathbf{F}_{2,0,1}\left(\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]},\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]}\right)

Moreover, to find the resonant solution to (
108
), we note that the corresponding equation will be given by

\mathcal{M}_{1}\,\mathbf{u}_{1}^{[5]}=-\mathbf{Q}_{1}^{[5]}\,A_{1}^{\prime\prime}-i\,\mathbf{Q}_{2}^{[5]}\,A_{1}^{\prime}-i\,\mathbf{Q}_{3}^{[5]}\,\absolutevalue{A_1}^{2}\,A_{1}^{\prime}-i\,\mathbf{Q}_{4}^{[5]}\,A_{1}^{2}\,\bar{A}_{1}^{\prime}\\
-\mathbf{Q}_{5}^{[5]}\,A_{1}-\mathbf{Q}_{6}^{[5]}\,\absolutevalue{A_1}^{2}\,A_{1}-\mathbf{Q}_{7}^{[5]}\,\absolutevalue{A_1}^{4}\,A_{1}.

(122)

Unfortunately, we cannot simply solve the equation for each term separately, as we cannot ensure that
\mathbf{Q}_{p}^{[5]}\in\mbox{im}\left(\mathcal{M}_{1}\right)
for every
p=1,\ldots,7
. Nevertheless, from (
7
), we know that

\displaystyle\alpha_{1}\,A_{1}^{\prime\prime}+i\,\alpha_{2}\,A_{1}^{\prime}+i\,\alpha_{3}\,\absolutevalue{A_1}^{2}\,A_{1}^{\prime}+i\,\alpha_{4}\,A_{1}^{2}\,\bar{A}_{1}^{\prime}+\alpha_{5}\,A_{1}+\alpha_{6}\,\absolutevalue{A_1}^{2}\,A_{1}+\alpha_{7}\,\absolutevalue{A_1}^{4}\,A_{1}

\displaystyle=0,

which implies

\left(\alpha_{1}\,A_{1}^{\prime\prime}+i\,\alpha_{2}\,A_{1}^{\prime}+i\,\alpha_{3}\,\absolutevalue{A_1}^{2}\,A_{1}^{\prime}+i\,\alpha_{4}\,A_{1}^{2}\,\bar{A}_{1}^{\prime}\right.\\
\left.+\alpha_{5}\,A_{1}+\alpha_{6}\,\absolutevalue{A_1}^{2}\,A_{1}+\alpha_{7}\,\absolutevalue{A_1}^{4}\,A_{1}\right)\,\frac{\bm{\psi}}{\langle\bm{\psi},\bm{\psi}\rangle}=\mathbf{0}.

Therefore, we see that (
122
) is equivalent to

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{1}\mathbf{u}_{1}^{[5]}

\displaystyle=\left(-\mathbf{Q}_{1}^{[5]}+\alpha_{1}\,\frac{\bm{\psi}}{\langle\bm{\psi},\bm{\psi}\rangle}\right)\,A_{1}^{\prime\prime}+i\,\left(-\mathbf{Q}_{2}^{[5]}+\alpha_{2}\,\frac{\bm{\psi}}{\langle\bm{\psi},\bm{\psi}\rangle}\right)\,A_{1}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\quad+i\,\left(-\mathbf{Q}_{3}^{[5]}+\alpha_{3}\,\frac{\bm{\psi}}{\langle\bm{\psi},\bm{\psi}\rangle}\right)\,\absolutevalue{A_1}^{2}\,A_{1}^{\prime}+i\,\left(-\mathbf{Q}_{4}^{[5]}+\alpha_{4}\,\frac{\bm{\psi}}{\langle\bm{\psi},\bm{\psi}\rangle}\right)\,A_{1}^{2}\,\bar{A}_{1}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\quad+\left(-\mathbf{Q}_{5}^{[5]}+\alpha_{5}\,\frac{\bm{\psi}}{\langle\bm{\psi},\bm{\psi}\rangle}\right)\,A_{1}+\left(-\mathbf{Q}_{6}^{[5]}+\alpha_{6}\,\frac{\bm{\psi}}{\langle\bm{\psi},\bm{\psi}\rangle}\right)\,\absolutevalue{A_1}^{2}\,A_{1}

\displaystyle\quad+\left(-\mathbf{Q}_{7}^{[5]}+\alpha_{7}\,\frac{\bm{\psi}}{\langle\bm{\psi},\bm{\psi}\rangle}\right)\,\absolutevalue{A_1}^{4}\,A_{1},

which fulfills that the coefficient of each term related to
A_{1}
is a vector that belongs to
\mbox{im}\left(\mathcal{M}_{1}\right)
. Therefore,

\displaystyle R_{3}

\displaystyle=R_{3,h}+R_{3,p}

which lets us note that, as

\displaystyle\quad\left.+3\,\alpha_{2}^{2}\,\left(\alpha_{3}+\alpha_{4}\right)\,\beta_{3}^{3}+6\,\alpha_{1}\,\beta_{3}^{3}\,\left(\alpha_{2}\,\left(\alpha_{3}+\alpha_{4}\right)+4\,\alpha_{1}^{2}\,\beta_{3}\right)\,\zeta_{\Xi}\right)\,X\,e^{2\,\sqrt{\beta_{1}}\,X}\,e^{i\,\varphi_{1}}.

Once again, this expression must equal zero for

to remain finite as

, then we can choose

\displaystyle\beta_{7,3}

(128)

\displaystyle\zeta_{\Xi}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{12\,\alpha_{1}^{2}\,\beta_{3}^{3}}\,\left(\left(\alpha_{3}+\alpha_{4}\right)\,\left(2\,\beta_{1}^{2}\,\left(\beta_{3}\,\left(3\,\alpha_{6,4}+\alpha_{7,4}\right)-2\,\beta_{5,3}\right)-9\,\beta_{3}^{2}\,\alpha_{8,4}+6\,\beta_{1}\,\beta_{3}\,\beta_{3,3}\right)\right.

\displaystyle\quad\left.-2\,\alpha_{1}\,\beta_{3}\,\left(4\,\beta_{1}^{2}\,\left(\alpha_{3,4}+\alpha_{5,4}\right)-3\,\beta_{1}\,\beta_{3}\,\left(\alpha_{2,4}+\alpha_{4,4}\right)+3\,\beta_{3}^{2}\,\alpha_{1,4}\right)\right),

(129)

whilst, if
\alpha_{2}\neq 0
, then we choose

\displaystyle\alpha_{2,4}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{12\,\alpha_{1}^{2}\,\beta_{1}\,\beta_{3}^{3}}\,\left(3\,\alpha_{2}\,\beta_{3}^{3}\,\left(2\,\alpha_{1}\,\left(2\,\beta_{1}\,\alpha_{6,4}+2\,\alpha_{1}\,\beta_{3}+\left(\alpha_{3}+\alpha_{4}\right)\,\zeta_{\Xi}\right)+\left(\alpha_{3}+\alpha_{4}\right)\,\alpha_{1,4}\right)\right.

\displaystyle\quad+2\,\alpha_{1}\,\left(2\,\alpha_{1}\,\beta_{3}^{2}\,\left(4\,\beta_{1}^{2}\,\left(\alpha_{3,4}+\alpha_{5,4}\right)-3\,\beta_{3}\,\beta_{1}\,\alpha_{4,4}+3\,\beta_{3}^{2}\,\alpha_{1,4}\right)+3\,\alpha_{2}^{2}\,\left(\alpha_{3}+\alpha_{4}\right)\,\beta_{3}^{3}\right.

\displaystyle\quad+\left(\alpha_{3}+\alpha_{4}\right)\,\left(-\beta_{1}^{2}\,\beta_{3}\,\left(\beta_{3}\,\left(5\,\alpha_{6,4}+\alpha_{7,4}\right)+4\,\beta_{5,3}\right)+3\,\beta_{3}^{3}\,\alpha_{8,4}+12\,\beta_{1}^{3}\,\beta_{7,3}\right)

\displaystyle\quad\left.\left.+12\,\alpha_{1}^{2}\,\beta_{3}^{4}\,\zeta_{\Xi}\right)\right),

(130)

\displaystyle\zeta_{\Xi}

\displaystyle=\begin{multlined}\frac{1}{6}\,\left(\frac{\beta_{1}^{2}\,\beta_{3}\,\left(\beta_{3}\,\left(\alpha_{6,4}+\alpha_{7,4}\right)-8\,\beta_{5,3}\right)-6\,\beta_{3}^{3}\,\alpha_{8,4}+12\,\beta_{1}^{3}\,\beta_{7,3}+6\,\beta_{1}\,\beta_{3}^{2}\,\beta_{3,3}}{\alpha_{2}\,\beta_{3}^{3}}\right.\\
\left.-\frac{3\,\left(\alpha_{2}+\alpha_{1,4}\right)}{\alpha_{1}}\right),\end{multlined}

where (
128
) and (
130
) correspond to equations that must be solved in terms of the parameters of the expansion in order to obtain a correction to the Maxwell point, and (
129
) and (
D
) correspond to the values of
\zeta_{\Xi}
in these two cases, where the equalities (
128
) and (
130
) have been used to explicitly state the value of
\zeta_{\Xi}
in each case. We highlight that, in both cases,
\zeta_{\Xi}\in\mathbb{R}
is a constant, which implies that
\zeta
is a linear function on
\Xi
.

Remark 9
.

The integrals in this appendix were defined as
\displaystyle{\int^{X}\differential s}
just for simplicity, as the integrals for the homogeneous solution do converge when computing
\displaystyle{\int_{X_{0}}^{X}\differential s}
, but the same integrals for the particular solution do not.

In any case, we highlight that what is highlighted in Remark
9
does not change the value of the leading-order coefficient of
A_{3}
at
X=X_{0}
, which is given by

-\frac{e^{\pi\,\xi+\zeta\,i}}{192\,\alpha_{1}^{3}\,\beta_{1}^{5/4}\,\left(-\beta_{3}\right)^{9/2}\,\sqrt{-\frac{1}{X_{0}-X}}\,\left(X_{0}-X\right)^{2-\eta\,i}}\\
\times\left(-3\,(2\,\pi+3\,i)\,\alpha_{2}\,\beta_{3}^{3}\,\left(\left(\alpha_{3}+\alpha_{4}\right)\,\sqrt{\beta_{1}}+2\,i\,\alpha_{1}\,\beta_{3}\right)\,\left(\alpha_{1,4}+2\,\alpha_{1}\,\zeta_{\Xi}\right)\right.\\
+i\,\alpha_{1}\,\left(2\,\alpha_{1}\,\beta_{3}\,\left(\beta_{3}\,\left(-16\,\beta_{1}^{5/2}\,\left(\alpha_{3,4}+\alpha_{5,4}\right)+i\,\beta_{1}^{2}\,\left(5\,\beta_{3}\,\left(\alpha_{6,4}+\alpha_{7,4}\right)+8\,\beta_{5,3}\right)\right.\right.\right.\\
\left.\left.-6\,(2\,\pi+3\,i)\,\beta_{3}^{2}\,\alpha_{8,4}+6\,i\,\beta_{1}\,\beta_{3}\,\beta_{3,3}\right)+12\,(2\,\pi-3\,i)\,\beta_{1}^{3}\,\beta_{7,3}\right)\\
+\left(\alpha_{3}+\alpha_{4}\right)\,\sqrt{\beta_{1}}\,\left(\beta_{1}^{2}\,\beta_{3}\,\left(\beta_{3}\,\left(15\,\alpha_{6,4}-\alpha_{7,4}\right)+8\,\beta_{5,3}\right)+6\,(-3+2\,\pi\,i)\,\beta_{3}^{3}\,\alpha_{8,4}\right.\\
\left.\left.+12\,(-5-2\,\pi\,i)\,\beta_{1}^{3}\,\beta_{7,3}+6\,\beta_{3}^{2}\,\beta_{1}\,\beta_{3,3}\right)\right)\\
\left.-3\,(2\,\pi+3\,i)\,\alpha_{2}^{2}\,\beta_{3}^{3}\,\left(\left(\alpha_{3}+\alpha_{4}\right)\,\sqrt{\beta_{1}}+2\,\alpha_{1}\,\beta_{3}\,i\right)\right).

\displaystyle\quad+2\,A_{2}\,\bar{A}_{3}\,B_{1}\,e^{ix}\,\mathbf{W}_{1,2}^{[3]}+2\,\bar{A}_{2}\,A_{3}\,B_{1}\,e^{ix}\,\mathbf{W}_{1,2}^{[3]}+2\,A_{2}\,A_{3}\,\bar{B}_{1}\,e^{ix}\,\mathbf{W}_{1,2}^{[3]}
