\displaystyle\quad+A_{1}^{3}\,\bar{B}_{1}\,e^{2ix}\,\mathbf{W}_{2,2}^{[6]}+3\,\absolutevalue{A_1}^{2}\,A_{1}\,B_{1}\,e^{2ix}\,\mathbf{W}_{2,2}^{[6]}+2\,\absolutevalue{A_1}^{2}\,A_{1}^{3}\,\bar{B}_{1}\,e^{2ix}\,\mathbf{W}_{2,3}^{[6]}

\displaystyle\quad+4\,\absolutevalue{A_1}^{4}\,A_{1}\,B_{1}\,e^{2ix}\,\mathbf{W}_{2,3}^{[6]}+2\,A_{1_{X}}\,B_{1_{X}}\,e^{2ix}\,\mathbf{W}_{2,4}^{[6]}+i\,A_{1}\,B_{1_{X}}\,e^{2ix}\,\mathbf{W}_{2,5}^{[6]}

\displaystyle\quad+i\,A_{1_{X}}\,B_{1}\,e^{2ix}\,\mathbf{W}_{2,5}^{[6]}+i\,\absolutevalue{A_1}^{2}\,A_{1}\,B_{1_{X}}\,e^{2ix}\,\mathbf{W}_{2,6}^{[6]}+i\,A_{1}^{2}\,A_{1_{X}}\,\bar{B}_{1}\,e^{2ix}\,\mathbf{W}_{2,6}^{[6]}

\displaystyle\quad+2\,i\,\absolutevalue{A_1}^{2}\,A_{1_{X}}\,B_{1}\,e^{2ix}\,\mathbf{W}_{2,6}^{[6]}+i\,A_{1}^{3}\,\bar{B}_{1_{X}}\,e^{2ix}\,\mathbf{W}_{2,7}^{[6]}+3\,i\,A_{1}^{2}\,\bar{A}_{1_{X}}\,B_{1}\,e^{2ix}\,\mathbf{W}_{2,7}^{[6]}

\displaystyle\quad+A_{1}\,B_{1_{X\!X}}\,e^{2ix}\,\mathbf{W}_{2,8}^{[6]}+A_{1_{X\!X}}\,B_{1}\,e^{2ix}\,\mathbf{W}_{2,8}^{[6]}+i\,A_{1}\,B_{1_{\Xi}}\,e^{2ix}\,\mathbf{W}_{2,3}^{[4]}+i\,A_{1_{\Xi}}\,B_{1}\,e^{2ix}\,\mathbf{W}_{2,3}^{[4]}

\displaystyle\quad+2\,A_{1}\,B_{2}\,e^{2ix}\,\mathbf{W}_{2}^{[5]}+2\,A_{2}\,B_{1}\,e^{2ix}\,\mathbf{W}_{2}^{[5]}+A_{1}^{3}\,\bar{B}_{2}\,e^{2ix}\,\mathbf{W}_{2,2}^{[5]}+3\,\absolutevalue{A_1}^{2}\,A_{1}\,B_{2}\,e^{2ix}\,\mathbf{W}_{2,2}^{[5]}

\displaystyle\quad+3\,A_{1}^{2}\,A_{2}\,\bar{B}_{1}\,e^{2ix}\,\mathbf{W}_{2,2}^{[5]}+3\,A_{1}^{2}\,\bar{A}_{2}\,B_{1}\,e^{2ix}\,\mathbf{W}_{2,2}^{[5]}+6\,\absolutevalue{A_1}^{2}\,A_{2}\,B_{1}\,e^{2ix}\,\mathbf{W}_{2,2}^{[5]}

\displaystyle\quad+i\,A_{1}\,B_{2_{X}}\,e^{2ix}\,\mathbf{W}_{2,3}^{[5]}+i\,A_{1_{X}}\,B_{2}\,e^{2ix}\,\mathbf{W}_{2,3}^{[5]}+i\,A_{2}\,B_{1_{X}}\,e^{2ix}\,\mathbf{W}_{2,3}^{[5]}+i\,A_{2_{X}}\,B_{1}\,e^{2ix}\,\mathbf{W}_{2,3}^{[5]}

\displaystyle\quad+2\,A_{2}\,B_{2}\,e^{2ix}\,\mathbf{W}_{2}^{[4]}+2\,A_{1}\,B_{3}\,e^{2ix}\,\mathbf{W}_{2}^{[4]}+2\,A_{3}\,B_{1}\,e^{2ix}\,\mathbf{W}_{2}^{[4]}+A_{1}^{3}\,\bar{B}_{3}\,e^{2ix}\,\mathbf{W}_{2,2}^{[4]}

\displaystyle\quad+3\,A_{1}^{2}\,\bar{A}_{2}\,B_{2}\,e^{2ix}\,\mathbf{W}_{2,2}^{[4]}+3\,A_{1}^{2}\,A_{2}\,\bar{B}_{2}\,e^{2ix}\,\mathbf{W}_{2,2}^{[4]}+3\,\absolutevalue{A_1}^{2}\,A_{1}\,B_{3}\,e^{2ix}\,\mathbf{W}_{2,2}^{[4]}

\displaystyle\quad+3\,\bar{A}_{1}\,A_{2}^{2}\,B_{1}\,e^{2ix}\,\mathbf{W}_{2,2}^{[4]}+3\,A_{1}\,A_{2}^{2}\,\bar{B}_{1}\,e^{2ix}\,\mathbf{W}_{2,2}^{[4]}+3\,A_{1}^{2}\,\bar{A}_{3}\,B_{1}\,e^{2ix}\,\mathbf{W}_{2,2}^{[4]}

\displaystyle\quad+3\,A_{1}^{2}\,A_{3}\,\bar{B}_{1}\,e^{2ix}\,\mathbf{W}_{2,2}^{[4]}+6\,\absolutevalue{A_1}^{2}\,A_{2}\,B_{2}\,e^{2ix}\,\mathbf{W}_{2,2}^{[4]}+6\,\absolutevalue{A_2}^{2}\,A_{1}\,B_{1}\,e^{2ix}\,\mathbf{W}_{2,2}^{[4]}

\displaystyle\quad+6\,\absolutevalue{A_1}^{2}\,A_{3}\,B_{1}\,e^{2ix}\,\mathbf{W}_{2,2}^{[4]}+i\,A_{2}\,B_{2_{X}}\,e^{2ix}\,\mathbf{W}_{2,3}^{[4]}+i\,A_{2_{X}}\,B_{2}\,e^{2ix}\,\mathbf{W}_{2,3}^{[4]}

\displaystyle\quad+i\,A_{1}\,B_{3_{X}}\,e^{2ix}\,\mathbf{W}_{2,3}^{[4]}+i\,A_{1_{X}}\,B_{3}\,e^{2ix}\,\mathbf{W}_{2,3}^{[4]}+i\,A_{3}\,B_{1_{X}}\,e^{2ix}\,\mathbf{W}_{2,3}^{[4]}+i\,A_{3_{X}}\,B_{1}\,e^{2ix}\,\mathbf{W}_{2,3}^{[4]}

\displaystyle\quad+2\,A_{3}\,B_{2}\,e^{2ix}\,\mathbf{W}_{2}^{[3]}+2\,A_{2}\,B_{3}\,e^{2ix}\,\mathbf{W}_{2}^{[3]}+2\,A_{1}\,B_{4}\,e^{2ix}\,\mathbf{W}_{2}^{[3]}+2\,A_{4}\,B_{1}\,e^{2ix}\,\mathbf{W}_{2}^{[3]}

\displaystyle\quad+2\,A_{4}\,B_{2}\,e^{2ix}\,\mathbf{W}_{2}^{[2]}+2\,A_{3}\,B_{3}\,e^{2ix}\,\mathbf{W}_{2}^{[2]}+2\,A_{2}\,B_{4}\,e^{2ix}\,\mathbf{W}_{2}^{[2]}+2\,A_{1}\,B_{5}\,e^{2ix}\,\mathbf{W}_{2}^{[2]}

\displaystyle\quad+2\,A_{5}\,B_{1}\,e^{2ix}\,\mathbf{W}_{2}^{[2]}+\ldots+c.c.

where ’
\ldots
’ represents terms that are multiples of
e^{3ix}
,
e^{4ix}
,
e^{5ix}
, or
e^{6ix}
.

Last but not least, the solvability condition at order
\mathcal{O}\left(\varepsilon^{7}\right)
is given by

\alpha_{1}\,B_{3_{X\!X}}+i\,\alpha_{2}\,B_{3_{X}}+i\,\alpha_{3}\,\absolutevalue{A_1}^{2}\,B_{3_{X}}+i\,\alpha_{3}\,\bar{A}_{1}\,A_{1_{X}}\,B_{3}+i\,\alpha_{3}\,A_{1}\,A_{1_{X}}\,\bar{B}_{3}+i\,\alpha_{4}\,A_{1}^{2}\,\bar{B}_{3_{X}}\\
+2\,i\,\alpha_{4}\,A_{1}\,\bar{A}_{1_{X}}\,B_{3}+\alpha_{5}\,B_{3}+\alpha_{6}\,A_{1}^{2}\,\bar{B}_{3}+2\,\alpha_{6}\,\absolutevalue{A_1}^{2}\,B_{3}+2\,\alpha_{7}\,\absolutevalue{A_1}^{2}\,A_{1}^{2}\,\bar{B}_{3}+3\,\alpha_{7}\,\absolutevalue{A_1}^{4}\,B_{3}\\
+\alpha_{1,2}\,B_{2_{X\!X}}+i\,\alpha_{2,2}\,B_{2_{X}}+i\,\alpha_{3,2}\,\absolutevalue{A_1}^{2}\,B_{2_{X}}+i\,\alpha_{3,2}\,\bar{A}_{1}\,A_{1_{X}}\,B_{2}+i\,\alpha_{3,2}\,A_{1}\,A_{1_{X}}\,\bar{B}_{2}\\
+i\,\alpha_{4,2}\,A_{1}^{2}\,\bar{B}_{2_{X}}+2\,i\,\alpha_{4,2}\,A_{1}\,\bar{A}_{1_{X}}\,B_{2}+\alpha_{5,2}\,B_{2}+\alpha_{6,2}\,A_{1}^{2}\,\bar{B}_{2}+2\,\alpha_{6,2}\,\absolutevalue{A_1}^{2}\,B_{2}\\
+2\,\alpha_{7,2}\,\absolutevalue{A_1}^{2}\,A_{1}^{2}\,\bar{B}_{2}+3\,\alpha_{7,2}\,\absolutevalue{A_1}^{2}\,A_{1}\,B_{2}+i\,\alpha_{3}\,\bar{A}_{1}\,A_{2}\,B_{2_{X}}+i\,\alpha_{3}\,A_{1}\,\bar{A}_{2}\,B_{2_{X}}+i\,\alpha_{3}\,\bar{A}_{1}\,A_{2_{X}}\,B_{2}\\
+i\,\alpha_{3}\,A_{1_{X}}\,\bar{A}_{2}\,B_{2}+i\,\alpha_{3}\,A_{1}\,A_{2_{X}}\,\bar{B}_{2}+i\,\alpha_{3}\,A_{1_{X}}\,A_{2}\,\bar{B}_{2}+2\,i\,\alpha_{4}\,\bar{A}_{1_{X}}\,A_{2}\,B_{2}+2\,i\,\alpha_{4}\,A_{1}\,A_{2}\,\bar{B}_{2_{X}}\\
+2\,i\,\alpha_{4}\,A_{1}\,\bar{A}_{2_{X}}\,B_{2}+2\,\alpha_{6}\,\bar{A}_{1}\,A_{2}\,B_{2}+2\,\alpha_{6}\,A_{1}\,\bar{A}_{2}\,B_{2}+2\,\alpha_{6}\,A_{1}\,A_{2}\,\bar{B}_{2}+2\,\alpha_{7}\,A_{1}^{3}\,\bar{A}_{2}\,\bar{B}_{2}\\
+6\,\alpha_{7}\,\absolutevalue{A_1}^{2}\,\bar{A}_{1}\,A_{2}\,B_{2}+6\,\alpha_{7}\,\absolutevalue{A_1}^{2}\,A_{1}\,\bar{A}_{2}\,B_{2}+6\,\alpha_{7}\,\absolutevalue{A_1}^{2}\,A_{1}\,A_{2}\,\bar{B}_{2}+i\,\alpha_{1,3}\,B_{1_{X\!X\!X}}+\alpha_{2,3}\,B_{1_{X\!X}}\\
+\alpha_{3,3}\,\absolutevalue{A_1}^{2}\,B_{1_{X\!X}}+\alpha_{3,3}\,\bar{A}_{1}\,A_{1_{X\!X}}\,B_{1}+\alpha_{3,3}\,A_{1}\,A_{1_{X\!X}}\,\bar{B}_{1}+\alpha_{4,3}\,A_{1}^{2}\,\bar{B}_{1_{X\!X}}+2\,\alpha_{4,3}\,A_{1}\,\bar{A}_{1_{X\!X}}\,B_{1}\\
+i\,\alpha_{5,3}\,B_{1_{X}}+i\,\alpha_{6,3}\,\absolutevalue{A_1}^{2}\,B_{1_{X}}+i\,\alpha_{6,3}\,\bar{A}_{1}\,A_{1_{X}}\,B_{1}+i\,\alpha_{6,3}\,A_{1}\,A_{1_{X}}\,\bar{B}_{1}+i\,\alpha_{7,3}\,\absolutevalue{A_1}^{4}\,B_{1_{X}}\\
+2\,i\,\alpha_{7,3}\,\absolutevalue{A_1}^{2}\,\bar{A}_{1}\,A_{1_{X}}\,B_{1}+2\,i\,\alpha_{7,3}\,\absolutevalue{A_1}^{2}\,A_{1}\,A_{1_{X}}\,\bar{B}_{1}+i\,\alpha_{8,3}\,A_{1}^{2}\,\bar{B}_{1_{X}}+2\,i\,\alpha_{8,3}\,A_{1}\,\bar{A}_{1_{X}}\,B_{1}\\
+i\,\alpha_{9,3}\,\absolutevalue{A_1}^{2}\,A_{1}^{2}\,\bar{B}_{1_{X}}+i\,\alpha_{9,3}\,A_{1}^{3}\,\bar{A}_{1_{X}}\,\bar{B}_{1}+3\,i\,\alpha_{9,3}\,\absolutevalue{A_1}^{2}\,A_{1}\,\bar{A}_{1_{X}}\,B_{1}+\alpha_{10,3}\,A_{1_{X}}^{2}\,\bar{B}_{1}\\
+2\,\alpha_{10,3}\,\bar{A}_{1}\,A_{1_{X}}\,B_{1_{X}}+\alpha_{11,3}\,A_{1}\,\bar{A}_{1_{X}}\,B_{1_{X}}+\alpha_{11,3}\,A_{1}\,A_{1_{X}}\,\bar{B}_{1_{X}}+\alpha_{11,3}\,\absolutevalue{A_{1_X}}^{2}\,B_{1}+\alpha_{12,3}\,B_{1}\\
+\alpha_{13,3}\,A_{1}^{2}\,\bar{B}_{1}+2\,\alpha_{13,3}\,\absolutevalue{A_1}^{2}\,B_{1}+2\,\alpha_{14,3}\,\absolutevalue{A_1}^{2}\,A_{1}^{2}\,\bar{B}_{1}+3\,\alpha_{14,3}\,\absolutevalue{A_1}^{4}\,B_{1}+3\,\alpha_{15,3}\,\absolutevalue{A_1}^{4}\,A_{1}^{2}\,\bar{B}_{1}\\
+4\,\alpha_{15,3}\,\absolutevalue{A_1}^{6}\,B_{1}+2\,\alpha_{1}\,B_{1_{X\!\Xi}}+i\,\alpha_{2}\,B_{1_{\Xi}}+i\,\alpha_{3}\,\absolutevalue{A_1}^{2}\,B_{1_{\Xi}}+i\,\alpha_{3}\,\bar{A}_{1}\,A_{1_{\Xi}}\,B_{1}+i\,\alpha_{3}\,A_{1}\,A_{1_{\Xi}}\,\bar{B}_{1}\\
+i\,\alpha_{4}\,A_{1}^{2}\,\bar{B}_{1_{\Xi}}+2\,i\,\alpha_{4}\,A_{1}\,\bar{A}_{1_{\Xi}}\,B_{1}+i\,\alpha_{3}\,\bar{A}_{1}\,A_{3}\,B_{1_{X}}+i\,\alpha_{3}\,A_{1}\,\bar{A}_{3}\,B_{1_{X}}+i\,\alpha_{3}\,\absolutevalue{A_2}^{2}\,B_{1_{X}}\\
+i\,\alpha_{3}\,\bar{A}_{1}\,A_{3_{X}}\,B_{1}+i\,\alpha_{3}\,A_{1}\,A_{3_{X}}\,\bar{B}_{1}+i\,\alpha_{3}\,A_{1_{X}}\,\bar{A}_{3}\,B_{1}+i\,\alpha_{3}\,\bar{A}_{2}\,A_{2_{X}}\,B_{1}+i\,\alpha_{3}\,A_{1_{X}}\,A_{3}\,\bar{B}_{1}\\
+i\,\alpha_{3}\,A_{2}\,A_{2_{X}}\,\bar{B}_{1}+i\,\alpha_{4}\,A_{2}^{2}\,\bar{B}_{1_{X}}+2\,i\,\alpha_{4}\,A_{1}\,A_{3}\,\bar{B}_{1_{X}}+2\,i\,\alpha_{4}\,A_{1}\,\bar{A}_{3_{X}}\,B_{1}+2\,i\,\alpha_{4}\,\bar{A}_{1_{X}}\,A_{3}\,B_{1}\\
+2\,i\,\alpha_{4}\,A_{2}\,\bar{A}_{2_{X}}\,B_{1}+\alpha_{6}\,A_{2}^{2}\,\bar{B}_{1}+2\,\alpha_{6}\,\bar{A}_{1}\,A_{3}\,B_{1}+2\,\alpha_{6}\,A_{1}\,\bar{A}_{3}\,B_{1}+2\,\alpha_{6}\,A_{1}\,A_{3}\,\bar{B}_{1}+2\,\alpha_{6}\,\absolutevalue{A_2}^{2}\,B_{1}\\
+2\,\alpha_{7}\,A_{1}^{3}\,\bar{A}_{3}\,\bar{B}_{1}+3\,\alpha_{7}\,\bar{A}_{1}^{2}\,A_{2}^{2}\,B_{1}+3\,\alpha_{7}\,A_{1}^{2}\,\bar{A}_{2}^{2}\,B_{1}+6\,\alpha_{7}\,\absolutevalue{A_1}^{2}\,A_{1}\,\bar{A}_{3}\,B_{1}+6\,\alpha_{7}\,\absolutevalue{A_1}^{2}\,\bar{A}_{1}\,A_{3}\,B_{1}\\
+6\,\alpha_{7}\,\absolutevalue{A_1}^{2}\,A_{1}\,A_{3}\,\bar{B}_{1}+6\,\alpha_{7}\,\absolutevalue{A_1}^{2}\,A_{2}^{2}\,\bar{B}_{1}+6\,\alpha_{7}\,A_{1}^{2}\,\absolutevalue{A_2}^{2}\,\bar{B}_{1}+12\,\alpha_{7}\,\absolutevalue{A_1}^{2}\,\absolutevalue{A_2}^{2}\,B_{1}\\
+i\,\alpha_{3,2}\,\bar{A}_{1}\,A_{2}\,B_{1_{X}}+i\,\alpha_{3,2}\,A_{1}\,\bar{A}_{2}\,B_{1_{X}}+i\,\alpha_{3,2}\,\bar{A}_{1}\,A_{2_{X}}\,B_{1}+i\,\alpha_{3,2}\,A_{1_{X}}\,\bar{A}_{2}\,B_{1}+i\,\alpha_{3,2}\,A_{1}\,A_{2_{X}}\,\bar{B}_{1}\\
+i\,\alpha_{3,2}\,A_{1_{X}}\,A_{2}\,\bar{B}_{1}+2\,i\,\alpha_{4,2}\,A_{1}\,A_{2}\,\bar{B}_{1_{X}}+2\,i\,\alpha_{4,2}\,A_{1}\,\bar{A}_{2_{X}}\,B_{1}+2\,i\,\alpha_{4,2}\,\bar{A}_{1_{X}}\,A_{2}\,B_{1}+2\,\alpha_{6,2}\,\bar{A}_{1}\,A_{2}\,B_{1}\\
+2\,\alpha_{6,2}\,A_{1}\,\bar{A}_{2}\,B_{1}+2\,\alpha_{6,2}\,A_{1}\,A_{2}\,\bar{B}_{1}+2\,\alpha_{7,2}\,A_{1}^{3}\,\bar{A}_{2}\,\bar{B}_{1}+6\,\alpha_{7,2}\,\absolutevalue{A_1}^{2}\,\bar{A}_{1}\,A_{2}\,B_{1}\\
+6\,\alpha_{7,2}\,\absolutevalue{A_1}^{2}\,A_{1}\,\bar{A}_{2}\,B_{1}+6\,\alpha_{7,2}\,\absolutevalue{A_1}^{2}\,A_{1}\,A_{2}\,\bar{B}_{1}=0.

(134)

Appendix F
Logic of the code

The GitHub repository,
[
24
]
, contains two types of code, a Python code that computes the amplitude equations and finds the asymptotic expansion of the Maxwell point up to order 7, which can be located within the folder ‘demos’; and a Mathematica code that performs the asymptotic expansion up to a high order (see (
35
)) to obtain
c_{r}^{[0]}
(see (
43
)). These codes were developed in different platforms due to the capabilities of each one (Python because of the existence of classes, and Mathematica because of the symbolic manipulation).

F.1
Python code

To run the main Python code,
Beyond-all-order asymptotics.py
, one requires
Python
3.13.5, together with the following packages:
IPython
8.30.0,
sympy
1.13.3, and
mpmath
1.3.0.

Apart from that, the code requires a Python file
foo.py
within a folder called
foo
with the definitions of

•

a dictionary,
parameters
, which specifies all the parameters of the system that are not being expanded, together with their values;

•

a list,
unevaluatedparameters
, which specifies all the parameters that are required not to be evaluated by Python (the recommendation is to make this list empty; otherwise, the code will take longer to run);

•

the strings
par1
and
par2
, which are the names of the parameters
a
and
b
to be expanded;

•

a string,
muval
, which is the value of the square of the wavenumber,
k^{2}
;

•

a list of strings,
equilibrium
, that contains the algebraic coordinates of the steady state of the system;

•

a list of strings,
var
, which is the vector of coordinates of the system;

•

a list of lists
diffmatrix
, which encodes the diffusion matrix of the system;

•

a vector of strings,
kinetics
, which corresponds to the reaction of the system;

•

a real number,
tol
, which corresponds to the threshold that is accepted to check for Turing bifurcation and codimension-two conditions;

•

a string,
phiunit
, that is a boolean variable to state whether the vector
\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]}
is required to be a unit vector;

•

a boolean variable,
simp
that states whether the algebraic expressions of vectors and coefficients are to be simplified or not (if the system is algebraically complicated, it might take a long time to simplify these expressions);

•

a boolean variable,
simple
, which states whether the system is ‘simple’ or not, on order for the equations for
\alpha_{i,2}
to be solved explicitly or not (this variable is relevant only when the coefficients
a_{1},b_{1},a_{3},b_{3},a_{5},b_{5}
have not been set to be zero from the start);

•

and a function
extrapars(extraparvals)
, that edits the dictionary that evaluates the parameters of the expansion following, for example, the definition
extraparvals[aNF[1]]=0
to define
a_{1}=0
, for the first order expansion of the parameter
a
of your system.

For example, for the Brusselator system, the file
Brusselator.py
is set up as follows:

parameters = {’delta’: Mul(Add(21, sqrt(313)), Pow(48, - 1))}

unevaluatedparameters = []

par1 = ’a’
par2 = ’c’

muval = ’(21 + sqrt(313))/(16 * delta**2)’

translate = True

equilibrium = [’a’, ’(c - 1)/a’]

var = [’u’,
’v’]

diffmatrix=[[’delta^2’, 0],
[0, 1]]

kinetics=[’a - c * u + u**2*v’,
’(c - 1)*u - u**2*v’]

tol = 1e-7

parameter_functions = {}

phiunit = ’n’

simp = ’y’

simple = ’y’

def extrapars(extraparvals):
extraparvals[aNF[0]] = 3
extraparvals[aNF[2]] = 1
extraparvals[aNF[3]] = 0
extraparvals[aNF[4]] = 0
extraparvals[aNF[5]] = 0
extraparvals[aNF[6]] = 0
extraparvals[bNF[0]] = Mul(Add(969, Mul(37, sqrt(313))),
Pow(128, - 1))
extraparvals[bNF[3]] = 0
return extraparvals,

which can be read together with example (
96
) to have an idea of how to set up the code.
After this has been set up, one just needs to run
Beyond-all-order asymptotics.py
on a terminal and, after the prompt ‘Enter the name of the system you would like to analyze: ’ appears, one must enter
foo
.

In particular, after importing the packages, the main Python script shifts the variables and the parameters of the system so that the steady state under analysis is
\mathbf{0}
and the codimension-two bifurcation point occurs at
(a,b)=(0,0)
. Then, the code computes the derivatives of the system up to order 7 to compute the Taylor expansions required to compute the vectors corresponding to the solutions at each order.

After the problem has been set up in Python, the code starts by obtaining the vectors
\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]}
and
\bm{\psi}
, which span the kernels of
\mathcal{M}_{1}
and
\mathcal{M}_{1}^{\intercal}
, respectively. To do this, Python runs loops to find a row and a column that can be removed from
\mathcal{M}_{1}
to obtain an invertible submatrix, as explained in
[
25
]
. The indices of these rows and columns are saved as
criticalrow
and
criticalcol
, respectively. After this, the
criticalrow
coordinate of
\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]}
is set as 1, and the other coordinates are found by solving the corresponding linear system of equations that now has an invertible matrix of coefficients. The same thing is done for
\psi
, but using the transpose of
\mathcal{M}_{1}
, and reversing
criticalrow
and
criticalcol
.

Next, to solve systems of equations in the case of having an invertible matrix of coefficients, as in the case of
\mathbf{W}_{0}^{[2]}
, defined as the solution to

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{0}\,\mathbf{W}_{0}^{[2]}=-\mathbf{F}_{2,0,0}\left(\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]},\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]}\right),

the code performs

DS_phiphi_00 = second_order(0, 0, phiNF, phiNF)

negativeRHS.actualcoord = DS_phiphi_00

W02NF = linearsolver(W02NF, negativeRHS, coefmat0),

where
DS_phiphi_00
represents
\mathbf{F}_{2,0,0}\left(\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]},\bm{\phi}_{1}^{[1]}\right)
, and has an intuitive name so that the code is ‘easy-to-follow’. Then, as most of the terms on the right-hand side of the equations defining the vectors of the expansion have a negative sign in front, we need to pass the negative right-hand side to the function
linearsolver
, which takes care of the sign automatically.

All the vectors are found in the same way. We highlight that the code solves the equations ‘quickly’, as it does not work with the explicit expressions of
\mathcal{M}_{0}
and
negativeRHS.actualcoord
(as they can be messy) when solving the equations. Instead, it solves the equation with dummy symbolic variables that are replaced after the solution has been obtained.

One last thing to highlight here has to do with the extra terms that appear in the equation for the remainder due to the separation of
b
from the Maxwell point. This can be included near the end of the Python script. Unfortunately, this step is model-dependent, as it can be seen in Section
7
, so this step requires some thinking in terms of the dominant nonlinearities of the system. For example, for the Brusselator, we have that the extra term in the amplitude equation is given by

\displaystyle\frac{1}{66}\,\left(37-\sqrt{313}\right)\,\frac{\Delta c}{\varepsilon^{4}}\,A_{1},

and this was obtained with the following lines of code, which are present in the main Python script:

elif modelname==’Brusselator’:
deltagamma = symbols(’deltagamma’, real = True)

quantity = psiNF.actualcoord.dot(Mul(deltagamma, SS_phi_01)) \
.subs(extraparvals) # Linear

quantity = simplify(quantity.subs(phiNF_eval).subs(muNF, muval) \
.subs(parameters))

where the variable
quantity
equals

\displaystyle\frac{\delta\gamma}{66}\,\left(37-\sqrt{313}\right).

The output of the Python code comprises three key things:

1.

First, the code shows you the order of the equation it is solving. It goes from order 1 to order 7 (this last order is not reported in the output, as the code does other calculations at that order). After order 4, if everything goes correctly and the code solves all the solvability conditions, then the output ‘The expansion to meet solvability conditions has been found.’ will be shown. Otherwise, it will show ‘print(’Something went wrong. Review extra parameters.’)’. If this output is shown, the code will keep running, but it is recommended that one stops it and reviews the system and parameter values provided, and the solutions that Python has found for the solvability conditions, which are present in the variable
extraparvals
.

Specifically, the solvability conditions (
10
), (
14
), (
102
), (
103
), (
104
) are coded in the variables
equation1
–
equation5
, respectively. If the code shows the error mentioned above, one can see the value of each of these variables. If any of them is not zero, then that solvability condition is not being met. Some extra input might be needed. We recommend checking the output and fixing the Python code ‘foo.py’ accordingly.

Also, at order 7, if all the calculations are carried out successfully, then the code will show the output ‘A Maxwell point has been found for the parameter values provided.’ If not, the code will show ‘Something went wrong. Review extra parameters.’ One can check what the problem is by checking the value of the variable
Maxwell_equation
, which should equal zero, together with the signs of the variables
beta1
,
beta3
, and
beta5
, which should be positive, negative, and positive, respectively. If any of these conditions do not hold, it means that the code was not able to find a Maxwell point with the input provided.

2.

After that, Python will show 4 values that are needed to run the Mathematica code explained below. Specifically, Python outputs the values of
ANF1
,
ANF3
,
eta
, and
xi
, which correspond to (
29
), (
133
), and the terms defined in (
28
), respectively. Also, these values correspond to the variables
C11NF
,
A3_coef.subs(omega1NF, 0)
,
etaNF
, and
xiNF
, respectively.

3.

The last key part of the output corresponds to a file named
objs.pkl
that is saved within the folder
foo
. This file contains the variables
alpha1
–
alpha7
, and
ANF1
,
ANF3
,
eta
,
xi
, and
extraparvals
that are saved after running the script for the model within the folder
foo
. These variables can be obtained by running the four lines commented at the end of the code. These variables can be used to check that the code’s output is consistent with what is shown in this paper in Tables
1
–
5
.

Some of the information shown in this paper is also saved in Python, but they are not needed for running the Mathematica code. For example, the variables
alpha1
–
alpha7
correspond to the coefficients of the normal form (
7
). The same type of notation was used to define in Python the coefficients of the amplitude equations (
123
), and (
124
). Also, the variable
ratio
has the value of the ratio
\frac{h_{1}}{k_{1}}
defined in (
42
).

F.2
Mathematica code

After running the Python code, one needs to run the Mathematica code to perform the iteration to find an approximation of
c_{r}^{[0]}
(see (
43
)). Apart from setting up the system itself, one needs to give the values of
\eta
,
\xi
,
A_{1}
, and
A_{3}
, where the latter correspond to the approximations of the first and cubic order amplitudes at the singularity
X_{0}
. Specifically, the code requires the following variables:

•

nmax
, which represents the maximum order to be computed by the code;

•

var
, which represents an array with the variables of the system;

•

par
, which represents an array with the parameters of the system;

•

nvar
, which represents the number of variables of the system and is computed automatically by using the length of the array
var
;

•

fixedpar
, which represents an array to evaluate the parameters of the system that are fixed;

•

parval
, which represents an array to evaluate the values of the parameters of the expansion at the codimension-two bifurcation point;

•

\eta
val
, which represents the value of
\eta
obtained from the Python code
see (
28
)
;

•

\xi
val
, which represents the value of
\xi
obtained from the Python code
see (
28
)
;

•

\kappa
val
, which represents the value of
\kappa
to be considered in the computation of the limit defining
c_{r}^{[0]}
(see (
43
));

•

C11eval
,
which represents an array to evaluate the first order expansion of
A_{1}
at
X_{0}
(see (
29
))
;

•

A3eval
,
which represents an array to evaluate the first order expansion of
A_{3}
at
X_{0}
(see (
133
));

•

kinetics
, which represents an array with the vector field,
\mathbf{f}
, defined by the system (
1
);

•

diffmatrix
, which represents a 2-dimensional array with the diffusion matrix,
\hat{D}
, defined by the system (
1
);

For example, in the case of the Swift-Hohenberg 2-3 equation, we have

⬇

nmax

=

181;

var

=

{
u
,

v
};

par

=

{
c
,

e
};

nvar

=

Length
[
var
];

fixedpar

=

{};

parval

=

{
c

->

0,

e

->

0.280975743474508}

//

N
;

\eta
val

=

{
\eta
NF

->

-0.0184553367631391}

//

N
;

\xi
val

=

{
\xi
NF

->

0}

//

N
;

\kappa
val

=

{
\kappa
NF
^2

->

I
}

//

N
;

C11eval

=

{
C11

->

0.592158254607366*
I
}

//

N
;

A3eval

=

{
ANF_3

->

-0.149272058209306

+

0.0309130877820341*
I
}

//

N
;

kinetics

=

{-

(1

+

c
)*
u

-

3*
e
*
u
^2

-

u
^3

-

2*
v
,

v
};

diffmatrix

=

({{0,

-

1},

{-

1,

0}});

After providing all this information, the code obtains the vectors that span the kernels of
\mathcal{M}_{1}
and
\mathcal{M}_{1}^{\intercal}
, respectively, following the same logic as the Python code. After this, it makes the change

\displaystyle\mathbf{u}\to\sum_{p=0}^{N_{\max}}\varepsilon^{p}\mathbf{u}_{p},

and it starts a loop where it computes the
p
-th order of the Taylor expansion of the kinetics of the system by differentiating the kinetics
p
times and dividing them by
p!
. After obtaining that expression, for each order, the code replaces

\displaystyle\mathbf{u}_{p}\to\sum_{r=-p}^{p}e^{irkx}\,\mathbf{W}_{q},

and generates a second loop to solve the corresponding equation for each value of
r
between
0
and
p
at each order,
p
(the code does not solve the equations from
-p
to
0
because those vectors are known due to (
36
), which the code also uses). An extra step is needed only for solving the equation corresponding to the equation for
r=1
, which is the resonant one. For that equation, a solvability condition is determined, which lets the code obtain the amplitudes we are after in order to obtain the limit in (
43
).

Finally, the argument of the limit in (
43
) is used to compute an approximation of
c_{r}^{[0]}
for large values of
n
. The code also plots the result if one runs the cells below the iteration itself.

As stated in
B
, some cases need extra care and that depends on whether the dominant submodes are
r=0,\pm 2
or, in the critical case in which
c_{0}^{[0]}=c_{\pm 2}^{[0]}=0
, then one needs to change some indices appropriately (see the code within the folders Iteration/Swift-Hohenberg 3-5 to see how the formula changes).
